vii
Foreword  |
Foreword
In your hands you hold a remarkable book. Please don’t be fooled by familiar thoughts—this isn’t 
just another work on tongue diagnosis. In reality it’s so much more.
When I received the manuscript from Ann, it came with a handwritten note stating that her aim 
in writing the book was “to eschew the idea that tongue appearances need to be memorized in 
order for tongue diagnosis to be learned.” I was immediately captivated by the vision set out in 
these words.
I ﬁrst met Ann during her teaching cycle of Advanced Acupuncture in Barcelona, in the Spring of 
2014.  Tere, amidst the bustling throng of downtown Barcelona—a stone’s throw from Gaudi’s 
Basílica de la Sagrada Família—something very special was happening. A medicine that had been 
meticulously so preserved—reﬁned through cultivation, and incubated through the millennia—
was being revealed by a softly spoken lady with razor sharp precision. 
Te resonance was strong, deep and, in today’s troubled times, immensely relevant.
For those seeking a Classically trained teacher who is both faithful to the ancient traditions 
and highly innovative in adapting it for modern times, you’re in the right place. Ann seamlessly 
blends classical scholarship with eﬀective clinical practice. As all clinicians know, any high brow 
philosophy or irrelevant, obscure practice will not stand in the crucible of the clinic. Tere, having 
been counselled and prescribed to, all that matters to the patient is whether they feel better.
Everything in this work is faithful to the lineage of the author’s esteemed teacher, but its real 
power is that it’s also faithful to Ann’s lived experience. It speaks directly to the times in which 
she lives and the people that she serves.
So to the subject of the work, Tongue diagnosis. Early in the text, Ann illustrates the application 
of this astonishing medicine through several case histories. She then follows with this passage: 
“Te tongue is a marvellous window into the way the body responds to pathology. As a diagnostic 
tool we can use it to understand the multitude of responses the body can make. Te tongue shows 
us where the body feels it can and cannot reach to enact those responses.”
In the Classical traditions of Chinese medicine, pulse taking is the gold standard that any physician 
worth their salt needs to become proﬁcient in. Te layers and nuances of the animating presence 
of spirit on the physiology of the body—and its struggles with pathology—are all revealed to 
a still and receptive clinician. Ann has often remarked that you should be able to accurately 
and appropriately administer treatment to anyone after carefully listening to their pulse. When 
studying pulse taking with her, she takes you on an amazing tour of the landscape of the pulses—
often taking a long time to deeply listen to all the layers, and noting the information that is

<!-- p.15 -->
viii
 |    Tongue Diagnosis
yielded. Indeed her perception of pulses is so attuned that she creates paintings of them—capturing 
the images that they evoke in her imagination. 
Here, in this remarkable text, the author conveys the precision and real life accuracy of a well honed 
pulse practice when applied to tongue diagnosis. No appearance of the tongue or symptom is left 
unaccounted for. Ann shows the reader how to deduce the clinical condition of anyone—even by a 
remote consultation—and prepare an appropriate treatment strategy by carefully examining what 
the tongue reveals. In some ways this is more accessible and swifter to learn than the pulses. It also 
has the advantage of being applicable to all patients regardless of location.
One might not imagine that the tongue could yield so much information. When I studied this 
subject I found it very dry, often contradictory and confusing. I rarely saw, nor could match, the 
tongue ﬁndings with the clinical presentation of the patient. I had a sense that there was something 
missing, but couldn’t quiet discover what it was. It would be many years of seeking before the 
mysteries of this discipline were revealed to me.
When I ﬁrst encountered the teachings of Dr Yuen, he revealed a very simple, dynamic and incredibly 
useful model for seeing the relationship of Qi as it pertained to the various terrains of the body. Te 
model clearly reﬂected the functioning of physiology, and perhaps just as crucially for a clinician, the 
pathway of progress of pathology. Tis was the missing piece, the game changer. It was a model that 
enabled clear, real time clinical thinking. It enabled accurate selection of the appropriate channel 
system. And it was a way to track the eﬀect of one’s intervention immediately, as well as over the 
ensuing days, weeks, months and even years. 
When you understand this model and keep it in mind when studying and practicing this medicine, 
you’ll be able to stay rooted in the Classics, whilst innovating a speciﬁc treatment strategy to match 
the patient’s presentation and guide their healing process.
Te author has accomplished an incredible feat in conveying the spirit and the essence of this practice. 
Te reader doesn’t need to go around memorising a vast deal of tongue appearances.  Within these 
pages, the art of tongue diagnosis is communicated with great clarity and precision, both nuanced 
and clinically relevant. I urge the reader to explore these pages with openness, and to marvel at how 
each person’s history is clearly reﬂected on the tongue. Tis information, when skilfully relayed to 
the patient, will have a deep impact on them. Tey will feel seen and heard—invaluable in itself for 
establishing rapport and trust—and thus, the eﬃcacy of the clinical encounter will be established 
before any treatment is formulated or administered.  
Dr. Hung D. Tran, MBBS 
London, April 2021

<!-- p.16 -->
ix
Author’s Note  |
Author’s Note
During the COVID-19 period, I performed remote treatments from my home. At ﬁrst, I was 
quite worried about not having access to the precious and foolproof tool of pulse diagnosis. 
Tongue diagnosis became, temporarily, the principal diagnostic method, and not only did it stand 
up to the challenge, I found a new enthusiasm for the method emerge. 
Tis book arises from decades of clinical practice and observation. As Chinese medicine unfolds 
for us, we begin to realize that if you understand the principles of the medicine you can practice 
with any tools that are in front of you. In all my studies with Dr Yuen, the underlying focus 
has always been awareness of one’s chosen lens—look at everything but see through the lens 
that oﬀers the most clarity. In countless tongue presentations, I remember Dr Yuen applying 
this principle with great virtuosity—integrating classical principles and channel theory along 
with the quite postclassical practice of tongue diagnosis. When the tongue is viewed within a 
wide array of parameters, a challenging and exciting arena opens. We have the opportunity, as 
modern practitioners, to methodically synchronize familiar modern tongue practices with the 
crystalline lens of classical Chinese medicine theory and practice. Tis book is intended to be a 
comprehensive guide to this process, and to function as a companion both in the clinic and to my 
book Te Art of Pulse Diagnosis: A Step-by-Step Exploration. 
In the clinic, I rely very much on pulses to show the location and nature of pathology and to tell me 
what I should do with it. I love listening to their depth, width, length, tempo, texture, and organ 
interrelationships—contemplating what it all means in the patient’s physiological, emotional, and 
mental states. Ten I look at the tongue for additional information about the degree to which 
Heat is responding to pathology, and the status of ﬂuids. Te tongue is wonderfully useful for this 
purpose. Often, I’ll take a photo and give the patient a detailed tour of the conversation between 
Heat and ﬂuids reﬂected on their tongue. When they actually see evidence of the pulse ﬁndings, 
compliance with gently communicated dietary and lifestyle suggestions increases. 
If you are with the patient, I strongly recommend taking pulses ﬁrst and crafting a diagnosis 
before looking at the tongue. Ask questions and look at the tongue later, considering the tongue 
an oﬀering of additional information regarding the body’s response to the pathology you found 
in the pulses. Te Ten Questions and tongue diagnosis are each Ming Dynasty additions to 
acupuncture and therefore secondary in importance to pulses. 
While this book is suitable for all acupuncturists with any kind of training and there are no speciﬁc 
prerequisites for its understanding, I have made references to the Complement Channels—the 
Sinew, Luo, Divergent and Eight Extraordinary Channels—all of which appear in the Han 
Dynasty (206 BCE—220 CE) Classical texts. (My book Advanced Acupuncture, A Clinic Manual 
contains my suggested protocols for the clinical practice of these channels.) Even though tongue

<!-- p.17 -->
x
 |    Tongue Diagnosis
diagnosis originated in the Yuan Dynasty (1279–1368 CE) and ﬂourished in the Ming (1368–
1644 CE), it would be remiss to discuss the tongue without some reference to these channels, 
since they are so celebrated in the classical texts that are the foundation and origin of acupuncture. 
Te Complement Channels are those that preserve humanity; when the body is unable to release 
a pathogenic factor because of weakness, lack of Fluids, or other reasons, these channels create 
pathways that move pathology away from the organs, preserving life itself. 
By the time of the tongue diagnosis texts, most of the Complement Channels had gone into disuse. 
Te concept of latency—the body’s ingenious and complex ways of hiding pathology—was also 
largely forgotten, and with it, the view that Cold and its vehicle, Wind, are the origin of all diseases. 
Diagnosis via the tongue alone, with its primary Ming Dynasty focus on Heat may leave us wanting 
for a completely comprehensive reading, but thankfully it reliably tells us volumes about the way the 
body is responding to pathology. 
Lastly, in this book there are many photographs. It is expressly not intended to be a catalog of 
photos. I urge you not to memorize any tongue herein. Te photos merely serve to illustrate the 
mechanisms, and the book should be able to stand without the photos. If the mechanisms are 
understood, diagnosis of any tongue is possible even with its inevitably unique, apparent, and 
seemingly puzzling contradictions. Chinese Medicine is an art as much as it is a magniﬁcent and 
profound medicine. I’m so glad that we’re here together to experience and practice it. 
With best wishes for your practice, 
Ann
New York, 2021

<!-- p.18 -->
xi
Contents  |
Abbreviations
At the ﬁrst appearance of Chinese words, I’ve observed the convention of hyphenating their 
pinyin spelling with their English translation. Wei Qi means Wei-Defensive Qi, Ying Qi 
means Ying-Nutritive Qi, Yuan Qi means Yuan-Constitutional Qi, Jing means Jing-Essence, 
Tin Fluids means Jin-Tin Fluids, and Tick Fluids means Ye-Tick Fluids. When words 
such as Heat and Cold are capitalized, it signiﬁes a complexity in the word that exceeds its 
basic meaning in English. Heat implies a response generated by the body that is viewed later 
as inﬂammation as it becomes trapped by its complicator, Damp. Cold, when capitalized, 
captures the notion that Cold is constrictive and destructive and creates complications such as 
stagnation and tightness. 
Left and Right 
Te words “left” and “right” always refer to the patient’s left and right. As you look at a 
photograph, the right side of the photograph shows the left side of the patient’s tongue. Te 
left side of the photograph shows the right side of the patient’s tongue.
Glossary
Bean: a unit of pressure in pulse taking
Bi Syndrome: pain caused by Wind (instability in the channel).
Cold: the contraction, tightness and slowing of functions caused by cold foods and drinks, 
climatic cold, or from the depletion of Yang Qi.
Cun, guan, and chi, translate to “measurement, gateway, template”: the three locations on the 
wrist occupied by the ﬁngers during pulse taking.
Dai Mai, translates as “the Belt Channel”: the channel that runs around the belt line, responsible 
for releasing what has been held onto for a long time, or holding onto things that cannot be 
released. 
Dampness: stagnated or accumulated Fluids, creating swellings, phlegm, and congestion. 
Dampness is often present as compensation for dehydration or other deﬁciencies. 
Diaphragm: the very large muscle that separates the abdomen from the chest, shaped like a 
giant mushroom and spanning the entire lower ribcage. It moves with the breath but tightens 
under various stresses. 
Heat: the inﬂammation that arises from the consumption of irritating foods (fried, spicy foods,

<!-- p.19 -->
xii
 |    Tongue Diagnosis
allergens, GMOs), or from the naturally generated response to pathology, including internal cold.
Jiao, translates to “Burner” or “Heater”: any one of three regions of the torso. Tese are divided 
at the belt and at the diaphragm. Broadly speaking they are the chest, the middle abdomen, and 
the area below the waist. Te area above the diaphragm is the Upper Jiao. Te area between the 
diaphragm and Dai Mai is the Middle Jiao. Te area below Dai Mai is the Lower Jiao.  
Jing-Essence: the deepest level of Yin, Yin at the constitutional level.  
Latency: the act or state of having hidden away pathology somewhere in the body so that they 
cannot access the organs. Te Divergent and Luo Channels achieve this for the body.
Mediumship: any ﬂuid: thin ﬂuids (Jin), thick ﬂuids (Ye), blood, or essence (Jing) 
Pathology: any challenge to the immune system, to Wei-Defensive Qi. 
Qi: the animating life force related to breath, warmth, and spirit. 
Wei-Defensive Qi: the Qi that ﬂows in the exterior anatomy, gut and smooth muscle, defending 
the body against entry of pathogens. It also ﬂows to energize muscles for responses to challenges. 
It is a subset of Yang Qi.
Wind: Instability in the ﬂow of Qi or/and Blood. External Wind is contracted by exposure. 
Internal Wind is caused by deﬁciencies in mediumship (substance).
Yang: moving, warming energy
Yin: substance; anything that can be weighed. However, the term Yin nearly always applies to 
some kind of ﬂuid. Yin can mean the collective ﬂuids, but can also refer to the mediumship of the 
Kidneys. Usage depends entirely on context. 
Ying-Nutritive Qi: the collective of Qi that builds us through gathering and transforming 
nourishment from the outside world, and ﬂows in Blood and Fluids.
Yuan-Constitutional Qi: constitutional vitality that ﬂows in the deepest level of the body: in the 
reproductive system, bone, bone marrow, spine, brain, and Eight Extraordinary Channels.

<!-- p.20 -->
1
Tongue Diagnosis and Visible Responses    |
1  
Tongue Diagnosis and Visible Responses  
Tongue diagnosis examines responses to pathology and the compensations that result from 
those responses. Understanding the responses enables the practitioner to deduce the nature of 
the pathology.
Case 1
My patient, Lina’s husband Chad, sent me an urgent text. Tey had been chatting and enjoying 
lunch together when the phone rang. Te caller was Jay, a close friend and regular visitor to 
their apartment. Chad answered, “Hi Jay! Yeah, that was fun yesterday, we had a great time. Can 
I call you back? Lina and I are having lunch.”  When Chad returned to the table, Lina said to 
him, “I know that I should know who Jay is, but I really can’t place him. Can you remind me 
who he is?” Chad thought she was kidding, but then saw that she was serious. 
Chad wanted to take her to the emergency room but Lina insisted that he send me a text ﬁrst. 
I asked for a photo of her tongue. It was deep red, shiny with no coat except for a diagonal area 
of the tongue behind a line starting a third of the way back on its right side, extending to the 
rear of the tongue. Te majority of the tongue was so peeled that it appeared to be missing the 
top two millimeters of ﬂesh. Te features were gone as though polished oﬀ. No evidence of taste 
buds or texture were evident in the peeled region. I asked was it shaking. Yes. Were there one-
sided neurological signs? No. Was there a problem with vision? Yes, the lower right portion of 
the visual ﬁeld in both eyes had black and white zigzagging patterns. Internal Wind, I thought, 
with an underlying Yin deﬁciency. I had them pinch and pull upward very hard on GB-21 
(the top of the shoulder) and to immediately eat something Yin (a mushroom, some leftover 
rice, some soup, a banana, anything Yin). Tey had some carrots and Chad quickly steamed 
them. After the pulling on GB-21, the vision issues subsided then disappeared, and after eating 
the carrots, some almond milk, and a banana, her memory fully returned. Troughout the day, 
Chad—fascinated by the notion that a diagnosis could be made by looking at the tongue—
asked to see Lina’s tongue. Later he told me that over the course of that day, the coat gradually 
“grew back” on the tongue, like a curtain being drawn diagonally across a window. Te tongue 
became covered as it normally would be and the return of the coat correlated exactly with the 
rate of her return to normal. With a change in diet to include much more wet food, she has not 
experienced it again.
Chad was witnessing the tongue’s display of the body’s response to pathology. Te extreme 
Yin deﬁciency had called for an emergency response from the body to accumulate Yin to stem 
the escape of Kidney Yang. Te body was drawing Yin from the Lungs and the digestive tract

<!-- p.21 -->
2
 |    Tongue Diagnosis
down to the Kidneys to anchor Yin back there. Te event was relayed in chronological order for 
all to see. 
Te tongue is a marvelous window into the way the body responds to pathology. As a diagnostic 
tool we can use it to understand the multitude of responses the body can make. Te tongue shows 
us where the body feels it can and cannot reach to enact those responses.
Case 2
A patient arrived with a very deep and very wide midline crack extending from the middle toward 
both tip and rear of the tongue. Te crack was ﬁlled with a coagulated gray-colored phlegm. His 
main issue was insomnia. He couldn’t quiet his mind suﬃciently to be able to enter a restful state. 
He was certain from his searches on the Internet that his underlying issue was Dampness—an 
accumulation of Phlegm. He’d been to several acupuncturists demanding that they treat him for 
Dampness, but the insomnia persisted. I told him that I didn’t agree with his diagnosis of himself. 
Te wide midline crack indicated a profound Yin deﬁciency to which his body was responding by 
accumulating as much pathological Dampness as possible. Te clue that Damp was the response 
to the Yin deﬁciency was the “gluing together” of the tongue on either side of the crack, with the 
body reaching for any and all resources it could obtain to build back Yin. Te treatment, therefore, 
was to nourish Yin and regulate Dampness. It would take quite a while for the tongue to repair 
itself completely, but the crack did become noticeably shorter and narrower quite rapidly and 
sleep was dramatically improved within the month. 
Case 3
A young man came in carrying an iced coﬀee; he was bent double and wearing a diaper so that 
he could venture away from home with a severe illness diagnosed in Western medicine as Crohn’s 
disease. Upon questioning, the usual suspects implicated in this type of illness were in play: garlic, 
onion, hot spices, coﬀee, chocolate, gluten, and sugar. Removal of these culprits was not going to 
be suﬃcient to get him through to health, because the tongue showed a distinct band of tension 
at the level of the diaphragm. Te body was responding to the possibility that the extreme Heat 
might aﬀect the Heart. Te Heat, therefore, could not vent upward due to this tension and was 
being forced downward, where it played havoc in the Lower Jiao. Te daily diet—frying and 
sweating the gut—added to the turmoil, and the organs themselves were becoming eroded. Apart 
from the immediate cessation of all the causative factors in the diet, the treatment required the 
releasing of the chest ﬁrst, then the clearing of Heat. Te utterly amazed patient was free of 
debilitating symptoms within ninety days.

<!-- p.22 -->
3
A Brief History of  Tongue Diagnosis    |
2
A Brief History of  Tongue Diagnosis  
Examination of the Tongue in the Classical Period
Te history of tongue diagnosis is surprising; what many don’t realize is that it’s a late addition 
to acupuncture practice. If you’re a clinician practicing medicine according to the Han Dynasty 
texts (206 BCE—220 CE), it’s strangely liberating to think that the early clinicians were not 
really thinking about the tongue much at all. I say “liberating” because sometimes you can look 
at a tongue and marvel at how much of the information you just found in the pulses is not 
showing on the tongue. Tere are good reasons for this. 
Tongue diagnosis developed to replace abdominal palpation during a period when touching 
the patient was taboo. Te Song Dynasty (960–1279 CE) was a time of puritanism: abdominal 
palpation1 and pulse taking were not widely practiced. Patients pointed to the locations of their 
complaints on ivory dolls, and court physicians diagnosed by feeling the vibrations in strings 
tied to patients’ wrists. Imagine, even as a practiced clinician, trying to do that. Something 
had to change. Puritanism ultimately called for something to look at, rather than touch. If 
the abdomen was not to be palpated, then perhaps the beginning of the alimentary canal, 
the tongue, should be able to provide information about the digestive system. Te tongue 
would surely reﬂect the abdomen.2 It was in this cultural environment that tongue examination 
developed to become an actual system of diagnosis. 
In the Ming Dynasty, two other major events occurred: the great plague (also known as “the Black 
Death”) swept through Europe and China, and the printing press arrived. Te West would like 
to think Europeans invented it, but no, it was yet more Chinese brilliance: Bi Sheng (990–1051 
CE) brought us movable-type printing a full four hundred years before Johannes Gutenberg’s 
innovations. As the technology became widespread, printers joyfully disseminated fashionable 
material of the time. In the ﬁeld of medicine, the topic du jour happened to be tongue diagnosis, 
so there happens to be a comparative plethora of material about the tongue dating from that 
early printing period. Te very ﬁrst tongue diagnosis text was the 1341 book, Te Golden Mirror 
Records. Te Heat of the plague projected a changing, technicolor show on the tongue, making 
these materials all the more fascinating, especially with the advent of multicolor printing in the 
early Ming Dynasty.
Te broad coinciding of these three events—puritanism, plague and press—led to the innovation 
of using the tongue as a diagnostic tool.
1 Abdominal diagnosis and palpation were practiced extensively until the end of the Tang Dynasty (618–907CE).
2  Later emerged a significant focus on the Spleen, whose Primary Channel emerges from the chest at LU-1 and then spreads throughout the 
root of the tongue. The tongue can be seen as an extension of the flesh which is controlled by the Spleen.

<!-- p.23 -->
4
 |    Tongue Diagnosis
But what was the status of the tongue before that—in the period spanning the Han and Tang 
Dynasties? How was it viewed clinically, when acupuncture was at the pinnacle of its development—
when all the channels of acupuncture were in use, when pulse diagnosis was sophisticated, and 
when practitioners, in command of the entire network of over sixty acupuncture channels, were 
conﬁdent and successful in treating everything? 
In the classical texts, there is no mention of the tongue being examined and analyzed to derive a 
diagnosis. Tis is because, in order for the tongue to be a diagnostic tool, there would need to be 
a diagnostic system applied to the tongue. Tis system would cover all the aspects of the tongue—
varieties of coatings, distributions of moisture, lists of movements, colors, and textures—along 
with a translation of these into assessments of Wei, Ying, and Yuan Qi and all the various media. 
But when the tongue is cited in the classical texts, it’s listed only as a sign—a sign of prognosis 
perhaps worth noting, but not at all a diagnostic system. For example, chapter 32 of the Su Wen, 
a chapter on internal Heat, says: When the Lung has a Heat disease, at ﬁrst the patient shivers, there 
is receding Qi and the ﬁnest body hair rises. Te patient has an aversion to Wind and Cold. Te upper 
surface of the tongue is yellow. Te body is hot. Chapters 4 and 31 of the Su Wen say that diseases of 
the Spleen will show at the base of the underside of the tongue (where the Spleen Channel ends). 
Elsewhere in the Su Wen, in chapters 16, 17, 41, 47, 52 and 63, it’s noted that the tongue might 
be misshapen or curled, or that speech is aﬀected because so is the tongue, especially when the 
disease is advanced. Chapters 31 and 33 describe dryness of the tongue as a symptom. Chapter 
48 of the Su Wen says that if the tongue can be moved, there is hope for recovery. A little later, 
Zhang Zhong Jing (c. 150–219 CE) referred to the tongue coat with the radical for fetus. He 
says that upon the invasion of the body by an external pathogenic factor (EPF), the coat becomes 
impregnated by that EPF and shows the Yang stages of penetration of that EPF. 
In the Nan Jing, the four diagnostic tools are discussed, but when it refers to looking (in chapter 
13), it is referring to the complexion, not the tongue. Te Nan Jing mentions that Cold and Heat 
will cause the lips and tongue to become dry. It also mentions that when the Liver Channel is 
diseased, the testicles and tongue will be retracted. 
Te tongue is mentioned twenty-one times in Hua To’s (c. 140–208 CE) book, Te Central 
Treasury Classic. He, too, writes that the general appearance of the tongue can signify the severity 
of disease, that a curled tongue indicates impending death (Scroll 3, chapters 48 and 49). He lists 
several signs that, if concurrent with a black tongue, deem a disease terminal. Scroll 1, chapter 26 
says that the tongue may become stiﬀ enough to aﬀect speech, and includes rigidity of the tongue 
in a long list of symptoms of Spleen pathology (quoting Bian Que, born c. ﬁfth century BCE, in 
the last instance). Scroll 1, chapter 25, mentions the relationship of the Heart, Small Intestine and 
tongue. Scroll 2, chapter 41 mentions heat toxin sores.

<!-- p.24 -->
5
A Brief History of  Tongue Diagnosis    |
In the Jia Yi Jing (scrolls 7 and 8) Huang Fu Mi (215–282 CE) lists either a degenerating 
tongue or a curled tongue midway through several long lists of signs and symptoms. Each of 
these lists relates to a diﬀerent type of illness, including rebellious Qi, Tai Yang, Shao Yang, 
Shao Yin, and Jue Yin presentations. 
Chao Yuan Fang’s (550–630 CE) book, Te Origin of Symptoms of Diseases discusses signs and 
symptoms that appear with certain simple presentations of the tongue. Scroll 28 relates the 
following to the tongue: oral and tongue sores, rigidity, dryness, scorching, swelling, and heaviness. 
It also describes systemic Heat rising to the Heart and Spleen, aﬀecting their connection to the 
tongue and mouth. If the tongue is dry, the teeth are also checked for dryness and yellowing. In 
scroll 10, he says the colors of the sublingual veins demonstrate Heat in the Heart and Spleen. 
Scroll 5 says that Qi stagnation is often accompanied by a white slipperiness on the tongue. He 
also mentions that during febrile diseases, if the root of the tongue is shriveled or withered, one 
should not needle. 
In his book, Prescriptions Worth a Tousand Ducats, Tang Dynasty giant Sun Si Miao (581–682 
CE) postulated that the tongue may contribute to diagnosis, mentioning the Qi of the tongue 
in its ﬂaccid or rigid state, that a hyperactive tongue can accompany epilepsy, and that a fetus 
will die if the mother’s tongue is blue or green. 
Troughout the classical texts there is no mention of a system of diagnosis that involves the 
tongue. But the tongue does oﬀer a marvelous reﬂection of the body’s responses to its challenges. 
It’s a thesis on the body’s responses to pathology.

<!-- p.25 -->
6
 |    Tongue Diagnosis
Te Beginning of Tongue Diagnosis and the  
Advent of Focus on Heat as Pathology  
During the Song Dynasty, there were invasions of nomadic Mongolian tribes from the north. A 
series of rapid, sweeping epidemics, including the Black Death, spread along the Silk Road, both 
east into China and west into Europe, killing almost half their populations. Flares of the epidemic 
would erupt, as epidemics do, killing swaths of the population, then disappear. During this time, 
most medical and religious practitioners died.3
Until the Black Death, China had experienced diseases that were routinely treatable—chieﬂy 
acute diseases—not pestilence. Te sheer intensity of the plague and its high mortality rate led  the 
remaining people to feel that the old medicine was ineﬀective, eliciting a shift in attitude toward 
disease. Heat, once known to be crucially important as a generated response to pathology, was 
now seen as a chief evil, the new primary pathogenic factor. Te resulting Wen Bing school, of the 
late Ming Dynasty, eventually prevailed. Its popularization by the new printing press led to the 
emergence of more written information on the tongue and on Wen Bing theory perhaps than any 
other tradition.
In modern Chinese Medicine (known as TCM), which is heavily inﬂuenced by Wen Bing theory, 
Heat is still seen as a principal pathology. Today, therefore, tongue diagnosis has its focus on the 
observation of Heat and the movement of Heat through the body. In this book we’ll examine how 
practitioners can beneﬁt from the tongue’s revelations about Heat. 
  
3  In defense, China turned inward; contact with the outside world ceased. This shift is reflected in Japanese acupuncture even today, as developments 
that occurred later in China do not appear in Japanese practice. Examples of Chinese Song Dynasty practices preserved to this day by Japanese 
practitioners are abdominal palpation, the Earth School, and the School of Nourishing Yin.

8
 |    Tongue Diagnosis
3  
Cold in Tongue Diagnosis  
Tongue diagnosis focuses on Heat, but the primary pathogen in classical medicine is Cold (or 
Wind-Cold). How do we reconcile the two? 
Te principles of Chinese Medicine—the roots of our practice—are explained in the classical 
texts of the Han Dynasty—the Nei Jing, the Nan Jing, and the Shang Han Lun. Tere we learn 
that Wind-Cold is the origin of all diseases (in Western terms, Wind-Cold is the common cold, 
a routine viral infection, and even a pandemic viral infection). Seventy percent of the Shang 
Han Lun (second century CE) is devoted to treatment of the initial invasion of Cold, the Tai 
Yang stage. Te text shows that Heat arises as the body’s response to Cold. Te body wants to 
expel it, or burn it up. Tis is in clear contrast to the understandable bias of the Ming, where the 
progress of unforgiving, scorching epidemics was so rapid that Heat—in Western terms, bacterial 
infection—came to be seen as the origin of disease, rather than the response. In the COVID-19 
period, we experienced the same kind of scenario: people did not report sneezing, stiﬀ back of 
neck, runny nose; they reported internal Damp-Heat signs instead. Te virus (Wind-Cold) 
was strong enough to bypass the Tai Yang stage and people witnessed and identiﬁed the Shao 
Yang and Yang Ming response to COVID-19 as the actual pathology, rather than complications 
impeding the patients’ responses. 
Te adoption of the tongue as a diagnostic tool in the Ming Dynasty challenges us to hold the 
foundational theory of the classical texts ﬁrmly, while incorporating something that is very useful 
from tongue diagnosis—that is, tracking the eﬀects of Heat in the interior. 
It’s now time to do this—to fuse these two parameters together; time to ask: in what way is 
the necessarily generated Heat going out of control or how has it become trapped by blockages 
or Dampness?  What mediumship (ﬂuids) is it consuming? And how is the body trying to 
compensate for the erosion of Yin that occurs as a result of trapped or rampant Heat? Tis is the 
blessing of the art of tongue diagnosis, the fusion of theory and a reframing from the perspective 
of the roots of Chinese Medicine. 
External Cold and the Tongue
Te notion that Wind-Cold is the origin of disease is a constant in the foundational theory of the 
channels also. Sinew Channel theory begins with Cold entering the Tai Yang (Bladder) Sinew 
at the Wind gates at the back of the head and neck. Wei-Defensive Qi is a product of Kidney 
Yang and Stomach Fluids. It is the Qi of immunity carried in the Sinew Channels that responds 
immediately to Wind-Cold, in a series of perfectly orchestrated actions.

<!-- p.28 -->
9
Cold in Tongue Diagnosis    |
First, the Tai Yang Sinew tightens to confront the Cold. Te body enters a defensive stance: the 
posterior neck contracts, the shoulders rise, the head moves down and forward. Constrictive in 
nature, Cold concentrates in the center of the face, causing the nose to run. Ten, a bolstering 
surge of Yang is brought up from the Kidneys to create more Wei-Defensive Qi to respond to 
and break up the Cold. Wei Qi is thereby fortiﬁed, able to instigate sneezing and sweating; the 
Cold is expelled. If this happens quickly, as it is designed to, there may be no sign of the entire 
process in the tongue. Te pulse, however, will change dramatically during the process, as the 
Wei-Defensive level of the pulse becomes very tight, between one and six beans of pressure, 
and resumes its relaxed state once clear. 
Tough the body’s responses are organized and wise, illness will progress if the body cannot 
ward oﬀ the pathogen due to insuﬃcient resources: there are not enough ﬂuids or Yang Qi to 
mobilize those ﬂuids. If there are inadequate ﬂuids to ﬁnance a sneeze and the accompanying 
micro-sweat that releases the exterior, or if there is not enough Yang to generate a sneeze in 
the ﬁrst place, the Cold together with the Wei Qi that is chasing it begin to move inward. Te 
body responds perfectly and powerfully again, now generating a fever to burn up the pathogen, 
compounding the Heat. Te pathogen must not be allowed into the interior. Te pulses become 
rapid at the superﬁcial level since the battle is still located at the exterior. Te body’s perfect 
(Heat) response shows as the perimeter of the tongue becomes red. If the fever is allowed to 
run and do its job while the patient rests and hydrates, the Cold is overcome, the pathogen 
doesn’t reach the interior, and the patient beneﬁts from a new, improved level of health and 
outlook, since Wind (Cold’s vehicle) brings positive change.4  
If the fever is not high enough, or if the fever is suppressed through medication, the pathogen 
is not resolved and remains unchecked. It then can move to the interior, through the zones, into 
Shao Yang, Yang Ming, Tai Yin, Shao Yin, and Jue Yin, in one order or another. Heat from the 
Wei Qi accumulated around the pathogen creates havoc as it goes. Te pulses become rapid 
at the Ying, and later the Yuan, level as the disease consumes Fluids, Blood, Yin, ﬂesh, and 
eventually Jing-Essence, developing into any manner of disease along the way. Te tongue goes 
through its rainbow of reds and purples as the disease worsens. 
At every stage, if resources are available, there is an opportunity for the Complement Channels 
(especially the Luo and Divergent Channels) to assume responsibility for the pathogen and put 
it into latency (into hiding). While the pathogen is in latency, the patient appears symptom-
free. If the channels fail in their quest to hide the pathogen away, the pathogen continues on 
its inward course. Signs emerge in the tongue to indicate the pathogen is no longer in latency 
4  The survivors of the Ming Dynasty plagues failed to understand that epidemics will never be fully treatable because they are like a terribly 
miraculous force that we cannot understand; they come, do their work, and disappear in their own time. It’s no coincidence that in Europe, 
after the Black Plague came the Renaissance, a wholesale shift in human consciousness, and that after the 1918 flu came the dramatic shift of 
the end of World War I and then the Roaring Twenties. Viruses (Wind-Cold)—barely living fragments of DNA—work directly at the level 
of evolution. To think that we can stop viruses is to think that life must not change; the virus—even the common cold virus—is constantly 
mutating: an agent of change in the world.

<!-- p.29 -->
10
 |    Tongue Diagnosis
and is consuming resources with its accompanying Heat. Ultimately, the Heat breaches the giant 
membrane, the peritoneum, then enters the organs and becomes fatal. Tis process is endlessly 
fascinating as it leads us through the genius theory of the Complement Channels—the Sinew, 
Luo, Divergent, and Eight Extraordinary Channels.
Pestilent Qi Response and the Tongue
Invasion of pestilent Qi—highly infectious diseases, including the bubonic plague—diﬀers from 
the regular appearance of invasion of Wind-Cold in three aspects. First, it is so hostile that the 
Tai Yang and Shao Yang defenses are overcome; pestilent Qi appears to enter the body at Yang 
Ming, becoming internal seemingly on contact. Second, the body’s response is heightened to 
match the extreme nature of the pathogen. Using its mighty Yang Qi, the body creates much more 
Heat (Wei Qi) to battle Yersinia pestis (the bacterium of the plague), for example, than it would 
less potent pathogens. Tird, the pathogen’s passage through the zones and the Complement 
Channels, though theoretically standard, is so rapid that it quickly reaches the organs—the most 
interior treasures. Death approaches rapidly. Troughout this process the tongue moves through 
gradations of redness: bright red, dark red, crimson, deep crimson, purple, and deep purple. Te 
pulse progresses from rapid to extremely rapid. 
Tose who survived the dramatic, valiant response to pestilent Qi faced decimation of their Yang 
Qi; their tongues were left pale. Since the central tenet of the Ming Dynasty was that Heat is 
likely the principal cause of illness, and since tongue diagnosis is a Ming Dynasty invention, the 
tongue diagnosis tradition focuses on viewing the tongue in terms of Heat damage. A pale tongue 
was understood to be the result of Heat having consumed Yang Qi, leaving Cold in its wake. In 
times of pestilence, this view of the tongue may be accurate but it is not always true for other 
conditions. Unfortunately, the notion that Cold was a prime pathogen was largely disregarded 
during the Ming.
Internal Cold and the Tongue
Tis book focuses on the tongue’s reliable demonstration of the body’s purposeful generation of 
Heat in response to complex pathology, and on the damage Heat leaves in its wake. 
But damage done by incoming Cold is also important, however; sometimes Cold signs are signs 
of actual Cold—full Cold, you might say, rather than empty Cold (Cold from Yang deﬁciency). 
If the tongue is pale, and inquiry reveals that the patient is engaged in Cold-related habits, the 
pathogen is likely Cold. Cold constricts and creates stagnation of Qi, which in turn stagnates 
Blood, leaving the tongue pale, pale purple, blue, or diﬃcult to extend. Heat would have diﬃculty

<!-- p.30 -->
11
Cold in Tongue Diagnosis    |
arising since Cold also depletes and can stiﬂe Yang, but if it were able to break through and 
create a fever, it would be a natural response—an attempt to break up that Cold. 
During the last few decades, a new breed of Cold-origin diseases has emerged. Tese 
imbalances occur in long-term vegans, strict vegetarians, raw foodists, iced water drinkers, 
and other refrigerator frequenters.5  Tese are in addition to the already established group of 
Cold imbalances in skiers, outdoor and refrigeration workers, and people who go barefoot on 
ceramic-tiled ﬂoors or other cold places. All this is fast-tracked internal Cold, gravely damaging 
Yang Qi, leaving the tongue various shades of pale. When Yang Qi is depleted, so too is its 
warming function. Tere may be insuﬃcient Yang Qi to make enough Wei-Defensive Qi to 
generate a response (including fever) to the interior Cold. Te Wei level pulses will be absent. 
Te Ying and Yuan level pulses will likely be weak, slow, and tight in some combination. Ask 
questions related to lifestyle and diet and take pulses to determine the cause of a pale tongue. 
  
5 Strict vegetarians can avoid Cold by ensuring their food is cooked and served warm or hot and by ensuring their protein intake is regular 
and adequate.

<!-- p.31 -->
12
 |    Tongue Diagnosis
4   
Orientation  
Te Four Layers of Assessment   
Historically, four layers of inquiry are considered during the assessment of a patient:
1.  Looking gives an impression of the illness. Looking can give clues. Tongue diagnosis is a 
Ming Dynasty addition to this category.
2.  Listening and Smelling reveal the expression of an illness. Sounds in the chest, breathing 
sounds, the tone of the voice, and smells, and noises from the digestive tract show how the 
body is conveying the illness outward. 
3.  Asking Questions helps determine what information is not being shared and what 
information gained so far may need more evaluation. Asking questions can conﬁrm 
observations about the tongue. Te Ten Questions are a Ming Dynasty addition to the 
medicine, and include questions related to diet, menstrual history, and emotional history, 
which can be important in corroborating ﬁndings in the tongue. 
4.  Palpating reveals that which is latent (hidden) and enables the practitioner to determine 
what the body might be repressing. Palpating includes the art of pulse taking. Tis is the 
most accurate, and therefore is the chief of the four routes of inquiry. 
Note that the tongue is a natural instrument of expression and therefore does not reveal what is being 
hidden, although we will see that it is possible to deduce what is being repressed in many cases. 
Tis “four layer” style of inquiry forms a complete set. “Looking” gives an impression of the illness, 
but certain basic ideas must be factored in before a diagnosis is possible.  
 
 
•  Most tongues have a thick coat in the morning. Tis might not be an indication of Damp, but 
simply be a reﬂection of the generation of Yin, a key function of sleep. So an 8:30a.m. patient 
who presents with a thick coat might not have a Damp condition at all. 
•  Also in the morning, in some, tongues are commonly red on the sides and the tip. Tis is 
not necessarily an indicator of Heat, but may be an indicator of Yang being mobilized to the 
surface of the body, since Yang Qi, and its subset, Wei Qi, are most active in the morning. So an 
early morning patient who presents with a tongue with a red tip and red sides might have had 
a hard workout in the gym that morning or may have rushed to the appointment. Tat tongue 
may be representing temporary overheating or temporary overactivation of Yang Qi—it might 
not indicate pathology at all.

<!-- p.32 -->
13
Orientation    |
•  Children have a natural exuberance of Yang, often causing the perimeter of their tongues 
to appear redder than the rest of the tongue. However, if that red perimeter or other areas 
of the tongue have indicators of pathology, the redness is of interest to the practitioner.
If you’re unable to take pulses, it’s important to ask questions about activity, habits, and foods 
eaten to get the fuller picture pulse taking would provide. It would have been clear from the 
pulses that the Heat was in fact from the earlier gym visit and is not systemic (since the pulses 
will not be rapid at the moderate or deep levels). In the earlier example, you may see that the 
Yin on the tongue was accumulated during sleep and is not pathological, since the pulses will 
be wide and deep rather than slippery. Still, tongue assessment is very useful, it just needs to be 
approached with clarity and awareness of the various possible explanations for its appearance. 
Te Tongue as a Complete Diagnostic Tool
If the tongue is to be a complete diagnostic tool, it must show the practitioner all three levels 
of Qi, since they are the basis of physiology: Wei-Defensive Qi (the Qi of the exterior), Ying-
Nutritive Qi (the Qi of Blood and Fluids), and Yuan-Constitutional Qi (the deepest level of 
Qi).
Wei
Protective Qi
Blood and Fluids
Constitutional Qi 
Ying
Yuan
In the following chapters we will see that by casting an especially broad net of inquiry pertaining 
to the three levels of Qi, the tongue can yield information worthy of adding to our pulse 
ﬁndings:
Te Tree Levels of Qi
• Te Tongue Coat reﬂects Wei-Defensive Qi.
• Te Tongue Body reﬂects Ying-Nutritive Qi.
• Te Sublingual Veins reﬂect Yuan-Constitutional Qi.

<!-- p.33 -->
14
 |    Tongue Diagnosis
Ying - tongue body 
Yuan - sublingual veins
Wei - tongue coat
Each level of Qi is assessed. Te practitioner examines: 
• the coat and its moisture, texture, color, coverage 
•  the body and its movement, posture, orientation, moisture, length, thickness, size, shape, 
color, luminescence, spots, protrusions, accumulations, swellings, silhouette, proﬁle, cracks, 
and erosions
• the sublingual veins and their color, shape, and integrity 
•  the map of the organs as reﬂected on the tongue. Tis, however, is very much secondary in 
importance to the above observations.
Channels Connecting Directly to the Tongue
Channels that connect to the tongue also inform the diagnosis: 
•  Te Spleen Primary Channel goes to the root of the tongue. It controls the integrity of the 
ﬂesh (tonicity) of the tongue and manages Blood going to the tongue. 
•  Te Kidney Primary Channel goes to the root of the tongue where it spreads its Qi. Tis 
channel initiates movement of the tongue and relays information from the constitution to 
the tongue. 
•  Te Heart Luo Channel goes to the tongue. Tis channel allows the tongue to express 
emotions and enables speech to project the truth of one’s life purpose. It also allows the 
tongue to express emotions that may be in latency, freeing them through speech. (Note: Te 
Heart Primary Channel goes to the throat and resonates through the eyes; it does not go to 
the tongue.)

<!-- p.34 -->
15
Orientation    |
HT Luo Channel
KI Primary Channel
SP Primary Channel
What Does Tis All Mean in Practice?  
In practice, the appearance of the tongue is separated into three broad considerations: 
• the appearance of the coat
• the appearance of the body of the tongue, and 
• the appearance of the sublingual veins
Diagnoses are made by:
• gaining a general impression of the tongue
• observing the qualities of the coat 
• observing the qualities of the tongue body 
• observing the qualities of the sublingual veins
• observing the status of the channels as they attach to the tongue
•  superimposing the map of the organs on the tongue and deducing where the response to 
pathology is focused
We will arrive at step-by-step diagnosis protocols at page 124.

<!-- p.35 -->
16
 |    Tongue Diagnosis
5  
Te Tongue Coat Reveals Wei Qi Status
(Te Immune System and Musculoskeletal Movement)  
Analysis of the tongue coat reveals the status of Wei-Defensive Qi—the Qi of the immune 
system. Wei Qi guards the exterior and governs our ability to ward oﬀ disease through sneezing, 
sweating, coughing, vomiting, defecation and urination. Without strong Wei Qi, we are weak and 
vulnerable, unable to resolve illness, acute or chronic. Internally, Wei Qi controls the movement of 
smooth muscle—in the bronchi, heart, gut, bladder, blood vessels, and uterus. Wei Qi also governs 
the movement of the musculoskeletal system, allowing us to move away from harm.  
Assessment of the following four factors of the tongue coat helps determine where pathology is 
inﬂuencing the formation or management of Wei Qi. 
1. Moisture of the coat 
2. Texture of the coat
3. Color of the coat
4. Coverage of the coat
Tongue Moisture  
Observation of moisture on the tongue is perhaps the most important observation in tongue 
diagnosis and is far more important than the coat color. When I was in acupuncture school, 
during my favorite class, Clinical Case Review, where we presented our cases from the clinic, if 
a student mentioned the tongue but didn’t report the status of tongue moisture, Dr. Yuen would 
ask for it without fail, reminding us that the moisture is of greatest importance. Without adequate 
ﬂuids, pathogens can be neither resisted nor evacuated.
Wei Qi and the moisture on the tongue arise from Jin (Tin) Fluids. Te Stomach produces two 
broad types of Fluids: Jin-Tin Fluids and Ye-Tick Fluids. Tin Fluids are further divided into 
two classes: pure and turbid.6  (Turbid means denser, not to be confused with polluted.) Pure Jin-
Tin Fluids go to the sensory oriﬁces to irrigate the ears, eyes, nose, sinuses, throat, and mouth, 
including the tongue. Turbid Jin-Tin Fluids go to the Sinews to allow Wei Qi actions, including 
the production, and enabling of, sweat. 
6  The word turbid simply means denser and has no negative connotation unless the balance of pure and turbid is off, in which case there is “too 
much turbidity” or “a turbid coat.”

Te coat reﬂects the general status of all Fluids, but the actual stuﬀ of the coat itself is Jin-
Tin Fluid. A normal tongue coat is moist: it has clear-looking, clean moisture, indicating that 
Pure Fluids are being produced. Tat means the immune system has the resources it needs to 
expel Wind-Cold (the pathogen and aftermath of the action of a pathogenic factor). It is able 
to create the micro-sweat or sneeze necessary to defend the exterior.  Also, a tongue coat that is 
neither sticky nor turbid and without accumulations reﬂects a free moving gut. A free moving 
gut ensures freedom from stagnation and the Damp conditions that follow, including parasites 
and fermentation (yeast conditions), lassitude, brain fog, weight gain, phlegm accumulation, and 
generally weakened digestion.
Tongue coat moisture is also a mirror of Stomach Yin (including the mucosal lining) and hydration 
levels in the entire alimentary canal. A dry coat is a sign of insuﬃcient hydration for the complete 
and eﬃcient transformation of food and drinks. Transportation in the gut becomes unreliable; 
elimination may become irregular or incomplete.
If the tongue coat is absent, Wei Qi (the immune system) is extremely weak; the patient may be 
deeply vulnerable to external pathogens. If the body is unable to expel a pathogen, it must at least 
have suﬃcient ﬂuids to put that pathogen into latency, to hide it away from the organs.
Aside from digestion and defense, ﬂuids must be available to temper Heat and to prevent its 
progression. When Heat is unchecked or when uncontrolled Heat (inﬂammation) creates ﬁre 
toxins (free radicals), terminal diseases may result. 
Dehydration  
Tough a “normal” coat is quite thin, lightly white, and evenly distributed over the entire surface 
of the tongue, in our deeply dehydrated, overstimulated and underslept culture, it is a treat to 
see one. Your treatments will be undermined if the patient is engaged in dehydrating habits. 
Dehydration along with imbalance in the emotions are a root of all diseases. 
When I ﬁrst encounter a dehydrated patient, I ﬁnd that they are usually doing one or more of the 
following: not eating enough soup, not drinking enough water, not eating enough saturated fat 
(they are often adhering to a low-fat diet), eating sugar, eating processed foods, going to bed late, 
drinking carbonated beverages, coﬀee, alcohol, too much tea, taking unnecessary supplements

<!-- p.37 -->
18
 |    Tongue Diagnosis
they can’t handle anyway, or eating garlic, onion, hot spices, or chocolate. All these strip the body 
of its hydration. In the ﬁrst encounter, while you don’t want to alienate your patient, it may be a 
good idea to make sure they know that these things adversely aﬀect not only the treatment, but 
also their immune system, by robbing it of its currency—systemic ﬂuids. 
Tongue Coat Texture  
Te texture of the tongue coat is determined by the Qi of the Spleen and Lungs, which is in turn 
dependent on the strength of Kidney Yang. Te “steaming” action of the Spleen transforms the 
food and ﬂuids the Stomach churns. Te Spleen ascends its Qi, carrying that steam with it all 
the way to the beginning of the alimentary canal,7  to the tongue. Te Lungs then diﬀuse those 
ﬂuids—at this stage known as Lung Yin—spreading them over the surface of the tongue.
SP ascention
LU diffusion
If this steaming mechanism is compromised, the tongue coat shows an uneven texture; the coat 
will not be smooth but will have some roughness as ﬂuids are stagnating. Often you’ll see tongue 
coats that look rough, sandy, grainy, pasty, ﬁbrous, powdery, gluey, glassy, or perhaps gritty. All 
these coats show pathology involving ﬂuids managed by any combination of the Kidneys, Spleen, 
and Lungs and indicate that Wei Qi is adversely aﬀected. Te Tin Fluids that ﬁnance Wei Qi are 
not being properly derived in the ﬁrst place. 
7 The end of the Spleen Primary Channel terminates as it disperses at the tongue.
Tongue

Tongue Coat Production Chain—Basic Observations  
If the coat is too thin or too dry and concurrently Spleen Qi is weak, distributable ﬂuids are 
not being generated in the Stomach. Tere is dehydration; the mucosal lining of the gut is 
inadequately hydrated. Or there may be stagnation (lack of movement of ﬂuids) in the gut. 
Qi is not moving adequately or freely.
If the coat is too thick, there is too much turbidity; too much turbid ﬂuid is coming up to the 
tongue. Tis means the Stomach and Spleen are not separating Fluids properly. Te Spleen 
is not transforming Fluids (or food). Spleen Qi is deﬁcient. 
If the tongue coat is reduced or absent, Spleen Qi might not be ascending. Moisture cannot 
reach the tongue.8  
If the tongue coat is not covering the entire surface—even if there are plenty of ﬂuids 
available (the Stomach and Lung pulses are wide)—the Lungs are not able to diﬀuse ﬂuids 
to the exterior; there are issues with systemic ﬂuid distribution.
Kidney Yang is the driving force behind this entire process. If Spleen Qi (good digestion) is 
the cornerstone of health, Kidney Yang is the bedrock beneath that stone. Te Spleen ascends 
Kidney Yang to the Stomach to begin the initial phase of digestion, rotting and ripening. If 
the Kidney Yang pulse (right chi position) is weak, digestion and therefore ascension of the 
coat will be compromised. 
What Does All This Mean in Practice?  
Your diagnostic process must ﬁnd the weak link in the chain of production of Wei Qi and 
treat it.
In nearly all cases, underlying all these issues are errors in diet, mentioned earlier. Either the 
diet is not hydrating; or it is dehydrating by draining or clearing; or there are factors in the 
diet consuming ﬂuids. Te most common dehydrating causes are: coﬀee, sugar, garlic, onion, 
hot spices, liquor, dried foods, processed foods, and additives. For excellent information about 
food and about cooking for health, See Andrew Sterman’s Welcoming Food, volumes 1 and 2.
 
8 See chapters 3 and 4 of The Art of Pulse Diagnosis for information on detecting directionality (the communication between organs) in the

Tongue Coat Moisture and Texture Diagnosis
If the tongue coat is:
•  dry, ﬂuids are damaged or not being ascended. Since ﬂuids are the domain of the Lungs, 
Spleen, and Kidneys, ask questions related to these organs and ﬁnd the location of stagnation 
or weakness to determine what is preventing the creation of the tongue coat. One of the 
above organs is weak, or there is an error in their communication. Or Stomach Fluids are 
deﬁcient in the ﬁrst place.
•  dry and thin, there is stagnation of Fluids in the Middle Jiao. If the coat is white, there may 
be stagnation in the Upper Jiao also. 
•  dry with diﬃculty speaking, Kidney Yang cannot ascend suﬃciently to assist in diﬀusing Qi at 
the root of the tongue. Te Kidney Channel normally enables the initiation of tongue movement. 
• dry at the front of the tongue, Wind has dried the coat.
•  thick and dry, the body has become tired trying to move pathology out. Qi has become 
deﬁcient, taxing the Stomach and damaging or stagnating ﬂuids. Tis causes the body to 
try to hold on to ﬂuids. Tis is not Dampness. Tonify Stomach Qi, move Qi, and most 
importantly nourish Stomach Yin. 
•  dry, with thick white coat, Heat has consumed Fluids, and Dampness has responded to 
that Heat, smothering it. Find where the Damp-Heat is trapped. Clear Heat before you 
transform the Dampness. If you clear Damp ﬁrst, the Heat will run rampant, causing disease.
•  greasy, dryness in the Middle Jiao (deﬁcient ﬂuids) has weakened the Spleen and created 
Heat. Dampness has responded and combined with the Heat to create a greasy coat. Heat 
pushes the Dampness up to the surface, reﬂecting the Hot-Phlegm that is developing.
• thick, yellow and greasy, there is Summer Heat (the inability to sweat suﬃciently to relieve Heat). 
• thin, greasy and white, there is oppression in the Upper Jiao (often chest oppression with nausea).
•  thin but too moist, there is not enough Qi to push pathology out. Te body has accumulated 
the ﬂuids to deal with a pathogen but cannot complete the task because Qi is deﬁcient.
• very moist, there is Dampness.
•  moist, dark, and gray, Damp in the Lower Jiao is damaging the Essence. Te Kidneys are 
taxed. Te gray color reﬂects Kidney involvement. 
•  very thick, the coat can become thick as the body tries to move Yin to expel an external 
pathogenic factor.

•  very thick and discolored, there is Phlegm or swellings. Interestingly, people with Phlegm 
often don’t have a thick coat because the Phlegm itself can block moisture from being

advanced Dampness, ask questions about heaviness, lethargy, and poor focus and look for 
slippery pulses. Ask about bowel movements and ensure elimination. If there’s a sense that 
signs and symptoms are worsening, the Phlegm is continuing to accumulate. 
•  very thick and yellow, there may be too much Stomach Fire. Te Spleen can ascend that 
Heat to the Lungs, which (under the inﬂuence of the Heat) diﬀuse Fluids too rapidly. 
Fluid can then accumulate in the Lungs, leading to a thicker coat. Te Stomach Fire 
causes the coat to turn yellow.

• powdery, there may be a Spleen Qi deﬁciency. Te Spleen is unable to hold onto the coat. 
• white and powdery on the rear of the tongue, Heat has consumed Kidney Yin.
•  very shiny, Wei Qi is being lost. Tere is a pathological emission of Qi. Or too much Wei 
Qi is meeting a pathogenic factor and has become blocked at the exterior. Heat is actually 
pushing Qi out of the body. Te concentration of Yang in the exterior creates a kind of 
illumination. Tis concentration of Heat consumes Yin. Tere is then insuﬃcient Yin to 
dissipate Heat, resulting in a physiological circuitous situation. Strengthen Wei Qi by 
nourishing Yin.
The Difference Between Slippery Moisture and Greasy Moisture on the Tongue  
Not that you would, but if you were to scrape the tongue and ﬁnd thin, watery liquid dripping 
oﬀ the tongue, you would call that slippery moisture, indicating turbidity in the Stomach.9  
But if you were to scrape the tongue and ﬁnd the coat thick, greasy, or clotted, you would 
call that greasy moisture, indicating a Spleen imbalance. Diﬀerentiate between the Stomach 
and Spleen in treatment. Dampness can be a positive response of the Stomach (to Heat), or 
it can indicate a deﬁciency of Spleen Qi as it fails to transform and transport. Diﬀerentiate 
by assessing pulses; remember, the Stomach sits higher in the guan position than the Spleen. 
 
9  The patient should not scrape their tongue on the day of treatment.

Tongue Coat Color 
Additional comments on color are included in the previous section. 
Te tongue coat is expected to be thin and white. Te white color reﬂects:
• the Lung’s eﬀusion of ﬂuids to the exterior.
•  the eﬃcient functioning of the gut in terms of transformation and transportation, especially 
the potency of the transformative Qi of the Stomach. If there is excess turbidity in the 
Stomach, the coat may be discolored (and thickened). Te lack of clarity of the coat’s color 
indicates digestive strength is insuﬃcient to fully separate Fluids. 
Tongue Coat Color Diagnosis  
If the tongue coat:
•  is thicker than normal and white, there may be an external pathogenic factor, Wind-
Cold. Wei Qi is responding to Wind-Cold, in its early stage, by moving to expel it. 
Tis movement of Wei Qi closely involves the Lungs’ movement of ﬂuids to expel the 
pathogen, hence the thicker white coat. Release the Tai Yang Jing-Well points to release 
the pathogen from the exterior. 
•  is very slightly yellow, the body is responding to the pathogen with Heat. Te tip and the 
sides of the tongue may be red as Wei Qi, which is very warming, activates to scatter the 
Cold. Te Heat is seen as a response to a Wind-Cold invasion as it tries to burn up or 
transform the pathogen. As it does so, it begins to turn the white coat yellow. 
•  is greasy and white, the condition is in Shao Yang. Te pathogenic factor is lingering due 
to Dampness. Te body is unable to expel it but hasn’t given up yet. Te tongue body may 
be slightly pale due to the underlying Qi deﬁciency. Te patient may have Bi Syndrome 
since Qi is partially stagnant. 
•  is yellow, the condition has penetrated to the interior and Heat is in Yang Ming. Tere is 
Heat in the digestive tract. Ask about nausea and coughing. 
•  is golden brown, long-term Heat is now aﬀecting the bowels. 
•  is brown, the Heat has been there a long time and is advancing. 
•  is black or gray, the Heat has been there so long it has scorched the interior or it has 
moved to the Kidneys (Lower Jiao). Tis happened because there were insuﬃcient ﬂuids 
in the Lungs to create sweat, or insuﬃcient ﬂuids in the Stomach to initiate vomiting

Tongue Coat Coverage  
Te tongue coat: 
• is normally thicker in the early morning. 
•  is normally thicker after menstruation because the body is trying to generate more Blood 
and in so doing must generate more Fluids.
•  can easily change with food and herbs. To see the full and deeper picture beyond the 
inﬂuence of the patient’s recent meals, rely on detailed pulse taking.
•  interestingly, shows the waxing or waning of Stomach ﬂuids during the course of a 
lifetime: in babies, the tongue coat develops slowly, beginning at the back of the tongue 
and slowly spreading to the tip. As people get older, the Yin slowly disappears like a 
curtain, beginning at the tip of the tongue and slowly extending all the way to the back 
of the tongue.
Tongue Coat Coverage Diagnosis  
If the coat is:
•  patchy, the body is trying to accumulate Yin. As the body gathers Yin, you may see 
pockets or patches of coat. Te body is trying to respond to the lack of Yin. Tis is not 
to be confused with the Lungs failing to disperse ﬂuids over the tongue. Te appearance 
of a “patchy” tongue is diﬀerent because it has a feeling of having been there but having 
been lost. Te patchy tongue is accompanied by pulses showing deﬁcient Yin in the left 
chi position at the deep level.
•  trying to glue together cracks all over the tongue, there is a deﬁciency of Yin together 
with stasis of Yin. As Heat damages Yin, whatever coat there is evaporates. Te body 
attempts to gather Yin and hold on to it out of desperation to repair the loss, while Yin 
continues to be eroded or Heat-damaged.  
•  peeling oﬀ, there may be a Yin deﬁciency; the body is not able to retain Yin by banking 
and consolidation. Te coat starts to peel oﬀ. Heat damages all Fluids. Treat full and empty 
Heat and pay attention to Kidney and Heart communication since the consolidating 
action is dependent on the Heart being able to communicate downward to the Kidneys. 
•  absent long term, if there is no coat for an extended period, there is deﬁcient Yin. Tis 
can include deﬁciency due to loss of Fluids causing dehydration. If there is no coat at the 
back of the tongue, the deﬁciency of Yin is due to loss of Yin, or aging. Wei Qi is weak if 
there is no coat.

Te Tongue Coat, Wei Qi, and Pulse Diagnosis
If there is no coat, or the coat is not well formed according to the criteria above, Wei Qi is 
compromised. But deﬁcient Wei Qi is always a branch of an underlying pathology. Treatment 
must focus on the root of the pathology, the weak link in the chain of Wei Qi manufacture. 
Te tongue is showing you the result of the pathology that has weakened Wei Qi, not the root 
cause. To ﬁnd the cause, if you can, take the pulses. To identify where Wei Qi production is weak, 
consider the following list of factors to ﬁnd where the root cause lies. Of course, you’ll have your 
own way of treating these things, but in case it’s useful, I’ve included quick treatment suggestions 
I often use to address the weakness. Te responses can be astonishing in their immediacy and 
eﬀectiveness. Tese changes leave you freer to continue within the hour, moving to a next phase 
of treatment. 
If the tongue coat appears to reﬂect pathology, the following may be in play: 
 
1. Kidney Yang is deﬁcient.
 
2. Spleen Qi is not ascending.
 
3. Tere is Systemic dehydration.
 
4. Stomach and Spleen are failing to transform ﬂuids.
 
5. Heat is damaging ﬂuids and the ﬂuid mechanism. 
 
6. Lung Qi is not dispersing ﬂuids. 
 
7. Tere is Liver Qi constraint.

<!-- p.46 -->
27
Te Tongue Coat Reveals Wei Qi Status  |
Tese pathologies are explained below in more detail.
1.  Kidney Yang is deﬁcient. Wei Qi ﬂows in the Sinews and is a subset of Kidney Yang; it’s 
warming and moving. It provides Yang to the Spleen and Stomach for the transformation 
and transportation of food and ﬂuids. 
 Pathology: Te absence of a good tongue coat may be showing you that there is a 
deﬁciency of Kidney Yang. Or Kidney Yang might not be supplying Yang to the 
digestive tract where the coat is manufactured. To ﬁnd out if this is the case, perform 
the following diagnosis.
Diagnosis and treatment: Listen to the deep level of the Kidney Yang pulse (the 
deep level of the right chi position). If it is weak or absent, tonify Kidney Yang, 
perhaps with moxa at  KI-3. Check the pulse again.
2.  Spleen Qi is not ascending. Te coat is brought from the digestive tract to the tongue 
by the ascending action of the Spleen. But ﬁrst, the Spleen must access Kidney Yang for 
its own strength then ascend more Kidney Yang to the Stomach, which is the origin of 
ﬂuids. 
 Pathology: Te absence of a good tongue coat may be showing you that the Spleen 
is weak. If the Spleen is not able to access or ascend Kidney Yang, the Spleen might 
not have enough Qi to ascend ﬂuids from the Stomach to coat the tongue. To ﬁnd 
out if this is the case, perform the following diagnosis.
Diagnosis and treatment: Press down on the Spleen pulse and release a tiny amount 
of pressure from the Lung pulse. You should feel the Qi moving upward from the 
Spleen toward the Lung pulse. If you don’t feel that, ascend Spleen Qi, boosting its 
function of connecting Kidney Yang to itself. Use the only trajectory that does this: 
the third branch of the Kidney Primary Channel. It has two points: KI-2 and SP-8. 
Needle and moxa both points, imagining they are connected together. Moxa safely 
until the patient reports they can feel the two connected by an unmistakable line. 
Check the pulse again. 
3.  Tere is Systemic dehydration. Wei Qi is derived from Gu Qi, speciﬁcally the turbid 
aspect of Stomach Fluids. 
 Pathology: Te absence of a good tongue coat may be showing you that there is 
signiﬁcant systemic dehydration. To ﬁnd out if this is the case, perform the following 
diagnosis.
 Diagnosis and treatment: Note the width of the Spleen position at the moderate

<!-- p.47 -->
28
 |    Tongue Diagnosis
level. It should be nice and wide. If it’s narrow or thin, nourish Stomach Fluids by 
tonifying ST-42 or/and CV-12. Check the pulse again.
If the tongue coat is not clean, thin, white, and evenly distributed, the following may be occurring. 
4.  Stomach and Spleen are failing to transform ﬂuids. Te color and texture of the coat 
indicates how eﬃciently the Spleen and Stomach are transforming and generating Fluids. If 
the coat doesn’t look clean, the Spleen and Stomach are not transforming and transporting 
Fluids. 
 Pathology: If the tongue coat is discolored or impure-looking, it may be showing you 
that the transforming capability of the digestive tract is weak. To ﬁnd out if this is the 
case, perform the following diagnosis.
 Diagnosis and treatment: Listen to the strength of the moderate level of the Spleen 
position and listen to the deep level of the right Kidney position (Kidney Yang). 
Tonify Spleen Qi and ensure you have suﬃcient Kidney Yang. Simple moxa on SP-3 
and KI-3 can work beautifully if these are missing. 
5.  Heat is damaging Fluids and the ﬂuid mechanism. Wei Qi is damaged by Heat as Heat 
damages the Fluids that form Wei Qi. Te texture of the coat indicates to what degree Heat 
is damaging Fluids and aﬀecting ﬂuid mechanisms. 
Pathology: If the coat looks scorched or dry and yellow, there may be Heat damaging 
the digestive tract and its transformation and transportation of Fluids. 
Diagnosis and treatment: Take pulses and locate the rapid pulse. Clear Heat from 
the Stomach, perhaps through ST-44, and then clear Heat related to the location you 
found in your pulse ﬁndings. Check the pulse again.
6.  Lung Qi is not dispersing Fluids. Te distribution of Wei Qi to the surface of the body is 
governed by the Lungs. Te evenness of the tongue coat coverage is a reﬂection of how well 
the Lungs are able to distribute Fluids systemically. 
Pathology: If the coat is uneven, the tongue may be showing you that the Lungs 
are not dispersing their Qi to the exterior. To ﬁnd out if this is the case, perform the 
following diagnosis.
Diagnosis and treatment: If the Lungs are not diﬀusing Lung Qi, you will ﬁnd that as 
you lift your ﬁnger very slowly from the moderate level of the Lung pulse all the way 
to the surface of the skin, the pulse will not follow your ﬁnger all the way; you will not 
feel it against your ﬁnger as you release pressure from the pulse. To get the Lungs to

diﬀuse Wei Qi, needle LU-1 or LU-7 and pull the needle up to the surface with the

7.  Tere is Liver Qi constraint. Te Liver is responsible for the smooth ﬂow of Wei Qi, 
both to the exterior and in the smooth muscle. 
 Pathology: If the coat appears uneven or has accumulations, it may be showing you

Diagnosis and treatment: If the moderate level of the Liver pulse is tight or wiry,

6   
Te Tongue Body Reveals Ying Qi Status
(Blood and Fluids) 
Te body of the tongue reﬂects Ying-Nutritive Qi (the Qi of Blood and Fluids). Analysis of the 
tongue body tells us about the eﬀect of Heat on Blood and Fluids. It also tells of the eﬀect that 
Heat is having on the organs that deal with Blood: the Heart, Liver, and Spleen. 
Movement and Posture of the Tongue Body 
Movement and posture of the tongue is governed by the channels that connect to the tongue: 
Kidney Primary Channel, Spleen Primary Channel, and Heart Luo Channel. 10
Kidney Channel 
Te Kidney Primary Channel goes to the root of the tongue, where its Qi initiates tongue 
movement. If the tongue is dry and there is diﬃculty initiating tongue movement, Kidney Qi is 
inadequate. 
Spleen Channel 
Movement and posture of the tongue rely on Qi reaching it. Te Spleen Primary Channel goes 
to the root of the tongue, and so the Spleen is responsible for its posture, general movement, 
swallowing, and chewing. If the tongue appears ﬂaccid, droopy, ﬂopping, or hanging, there is 
insuﬃcient ascension of Spleen Qi. 
Heart Luo Channel 
Te tongue is known classically as the sprout of the Heart. Te connection between the Heart and 
its sprout is the Heart Luo, a conduit of Blood. Since Blood houses the emotions, the Heart Luo 
enables the tongue to express emotions. When the mind overrides the tongue in the expression of 
emotions, the tongue becomes stiﬀ as Blood ﬂow to the tongue slows. Te tongue acquires shades 
of purple as a result. Tis in turn reﬂects emotional pathology: a state where the patient is seeking 
to ﬁnd latency for unexpressed emotions. When the Blood is in free ﬂow and the emotions are 
free, the tongue exhibits its natural bland red color, a color we now call pink. 
Movement and Posture Analysis 
Analysis of the movement of the tongue is an important part of diagnosis. Te ﬁrst part of the 
10  The Heart Primary Channel does not go to the tongue, though it does go to the throat and the eyes.
Wei
Ying
Yuan

<!-- p.50 -->
31
Te Tongue Body Reveals Ying Qi Status  |
analysis of movement involves consideration of the three channels that attach to the tongue. 
1.  If there is diﬃculty in the initiation of movement—for example, if the patient cannot 
initiate movement because of paralysis, shock, trauma, stuttering, or damage—the Kidney 
Channel should be treated. If these are a chief complaint, key points would be KI-27 and 
CV-23, paired with individually appropriate distal Kidney Channel points. 
2.  If there is a lack of tonicity of the tongue, Spleen Qi is not suﬃciently ascending to 
enable movement to be vital. 
3.  If the tongue is stiﬀ and purple, the Heart Luo Channel is enabling the withholding of 
speech for emotional reasons. 
Wind and the Tongue 
When mediumship (Fluids, Blood, Yin or Jing) become deﬁcient, the channels lack the 
stability they are provided by the mass of that medium. Te instability manifests as twitches, 
tremors, shaking, moving pain, and ultimately stroke. Te tongue reﬂects wind as tiny erratic 
movements, major unpredictable movement, stiﬀness, and deviation.
In addition to all the above, the following can be considered: 
If the tongue is: 
• stiﬀ, 
•  Liver Qi is stagnated. Te smooth ﬂow of Liver Qi enables free movement of the 
musculature, or 
• the Heart is not adequately moving Blood, or
• the Lungs are not adequately moving Blood, or
• Heat has depleted ﬂuids in the tongue, causing stiﬀness, or
•  Heat has either consumed Stomach Fluids, or blocked the passage of Stomach Fluids 
to the tongue, or
• Blood stasis is causing the tongue to stiﬀen.
• so stiﬀ it is curled or retracted, one or more of the above conditions is advanced.

Te length of the tongue body is determined by the amount of Kidney Yang available to assist in

Te tongue appears short if the muscles can’t extend due to a lack of Kidney Yang. Tere might

and compressed. Tis is a sign of Cold contracting and retracting the tongue. 
Turbidity building in the Stomach as weak digestive function fails to separate the pure from the 
impure can also impede the movement of Stomach Fluids, and result in an apparent shortening 
of the tongue. 
If the tongue appears long, there is too much Heat. Te tongue can be particularly long in 
menopause when Heat rises from the Kidneys and at the same time the tongue tapers at the 
front because there is a Yin deﬁciency. Te tongue can also redden as Yang escapes when there is 
insuﬃcient Yin to anchor it. In these cases, the tongue will also be shiny.

Te thickness of the tongue body is determined by the complement of mediumship (Fluids,

the principle food of the Spleen and Stomach.12 Just as a swollen tongue can indicate a 
digestive tract overwhelmed with reﬁned carbohydrates, a very thin or crepe-like tongue 
can indicate a deﬁciency of grain. Although Blood building can be achieved with other 
foods, safe grains naturally bolster the Spleen and Stomach in all their roles, including 
Blood-building.

has been swollen for a long time, it will have toothmarks. Reduce reﬁned carbohydrates 
and eliminate sugar, sticky foods, and processed foods.

oats, rye, barley). Phlegmatic accumulations are being sent outward or upward. If being 
sent upward, the swelling will either be in the middle section of the tongue sides (the

Differentiating Scalloping
Scalloping is a term given a swollen tongue that bears impressions of the teeth on its sides. 
Tere are two types of scalloping: 
• Spleen Damp scalloping
• Liver Compensation scalloping
Spleen Damp scalloping occurs when the swelling in the middle  
of the tongue begins to aﬀect the sides of the tongue. Te tongue is  
swollen from the middle and the swelling extends to the sides of  
the tongue. Tis occurs when the digestive tract and the  
Spleen-Pancreas are weakened and unable to run digestion eﬃciently.  
Liver compensation type scalloping is seen at the sides and is  
clearly not emanating from the center. Te center could be slightly  
swollen or not swollen at all. Tere might also be no elevation of the  
center at all; it could even be concave.  Tis constraint reﬂects the  
Liver having diﬃculty either storing Blood or moving Blood because  
Blood is insuﬃcient. Tere might also be constraint as the Liver  
holds back the smooth ﬂow of Qi to prevent deep penetration of a pathogenic factor.

Te Tree Jiaos
In a general sense, the tongue can be divided into thirds, each representing a Jiao (Burner, or center of 
Qi). Tese divisions are delineated by the diaphragm and Dai Mai.
Beyond breathing, the function of the diaphragm is to act as a barrier to stop further penetration 
of pathology from Upper Jiao to Middle or Lower Jiao, or to open the way to releasing pathology. 
Since the diaphragm is controlled by the Liver, constriction of this area denotes Liver Qi 
stagnation. Te Liver also commands Wei Qi. Related signs and symptoms may appear in the 
musculoskeletal system or in smooth muscle.
Diaphragm
Diaphragm
               
Dai Mai
Dai Mai is the second barrier. It separates Middle and Lower Jiaos. Te function of Dai Mai 
is to release pathology, or to hold on to pathology if there is insuﬃcient mediumship to clear it 
eﬀectively. Constraint in Dai Mai shows as the tongue appears indented, cinched, or subdivided 
across its line. 
Te diaphragm and Dai Mai separate the Jiaos. Tey rarely show in a healthy tongue. When 
they are constrained, they manifest as visible demarcations in the tongue.
