Swelling and the Tongue Silhouettes or Proﬁles  
Swelling can aﬀect the proﬁle (side view) of the tongue. From the tongue proﬁle, the practitioner 
can ascertain whether there are swellings conﬁned to any of the three Jiaos (regions or burners) 
which are separated by the diaphragm and Dai Mai (the Belt Channel).
If the distal third of the tongue is swollen, there is an accumulation of Dampness in the Upper 
Jiao, most likely in the lymphatic system. You might choose to drain the Dampness through the 
Bladder. 
If the middle third of the tongue is swollen, there is an accumulation of Dampness in the Middle 
Jiao. You might choose to transform the Dampness by treating the Spleen.
If the distal and middle thirds are both swollen, there is a failure to transform or reﬁne food and 
ﬂuids, resulting in food and ﬂuid stagnation. 
If the proximal (rear) third of the tongue is swollen, there is an accumulation of Dampness in 
the Lower Jiao. You might choose to disinhibit or drain the Dampness, unless the Dampness is 
compensating for Heat. (Treat the Heat before dealing with the Dampness.)

Origins of Dampness in Tongue Body Swellings 
Swellings indicate Dampness. Dampness can be a response to Heat (inﬂammation), often 
located in the digestive tract. Gut inﬂammation arises when substances that create strong Wei Qi 
responses are introduced. Tese include garlic, onion, hot spices, liquor, coﬀee, chocolate, and for 
some people, gluten, milk, and cheese. If the tongue is dry and swollen, there is likely more Heat 
than Damp. Tis can mean that the body is overwhelmed by the oﬀending foods or that the body 
cannot generate enough Dampness to extinguish their eﬀects. 
If the tongue is moist and swollen, there is likely more Damp than Heat. Tis can mean that 
the body is overwhelmed by damp foods (sugar, dairy, and gluten) or that the Damp response 
to inﬂaming foods is stagnating Fluids. Check whether Heat or Dampness predominates in the 
13 The profile of the Lower Jiao is deduced since a profile photograph is not possible.

<!-- p.64 -->
45
Te Tongue Body Reveals Ying Qi Status  |
pulses to identify the root pathology. Te pulses may be clearly more slippery than rapid or vice 
versa. If you are not conﬁdent taking pulses, ask the patient their historical tendencies. Over the 
course of their life, have they tended to swell with “water weight”, or do they tend to run hot?
Accumulations on the tongue body also result from improper elimination due to impairment of 
the Spleen’s transportation function. Tis can be caused by allergies or intolerances in the gut, 
often to grains. While moderate amounts of grain are essential to the proper functioning of a 
healthy gut,14 an overly sticky diet will adversely aﬀect the gut. 
Other causes of accumulations on the tongue include:
• failure to chew well
• processed food (food that came through a factory). Choose whole grains instead. 
•  genetically modiﬁed foods (GMOs). Tese contain rogue proteins unrecognizable to the 
Spleen. Te immune system is activated in response, and inﬂammation can develop. 
•  eating too much, or eating too soon after a previous meal. If a person eats a meal before 
they have separated, absorbed and prepared to eliminate the previous meal, the gut will 
become progressively stagnated. Elimination is compromised, since it is not keeping up 
with incoming food, be it grain, protein, or lipid. 
• too much cold and raw food
•  too much spicy food irritating the mucus lining of the gut and negatively aﬀecting the 
absorption of nutrients 
• sugar 
 
• vaccines, as they often contain nut, soy, wheat, pea, and egg by-products
•  improper combining of foods15 for the individual. Food combinations that the person does not 
have the capacity to digest, or for which their digestion is too slow, allow the food to ferment 
in the gut, causing autointoxication and eventually damage to the gut. Te gut protests against 
the food combinations, but if each food were eaten alone it might be well digested. 
•  Grains and dairy don’t get along well. Bagels and cream cheese, crackers and cheese, 
macaroni and cheese, pizza, etc., can ferment in the system.
•  Grains and sugar don’t get along. Peanut butter and jelly on bread, sugary doughnuts, pies, 
etc., may eventually cause a reaction because of the combination. 
14 Even nomadic peoples chewed on grasses (grains) as they moved. Grains did not suddenly appear with the advent of agriculture.
15 See Andrew Sterman’s writings on food for more discussion. These are listed in the Further Reading.

Dampness: To Drain or Not to Drain
When Dampness is responding to Heat, do not try to clear the Dampness. Leave it there. Te 
Dampness is the body’s response to Heat (inﬂammation). Even more importantly, swellings 
in the tongue can be a sign of latency; the body is using pathological ﬂuids (Dampness) to 
contain a pathogenic factor, averting a threat to the organs. In this way, Dampness is very often a 
response to Heat, quelling it and allowing it to become quiet. If any of the deep pulses are rapid, 
approach Dampness with great caution. Removing Dampness in these cases may result in auto-
intoxication since the pathogen will have lost its container, becoming systemic as it unleashes ﬁre 
toxins (free radicals). Fluids will be further damaged by the additional Heat being released into 
the system. Te body may try to mount an eradication of this pathology but the lack of ﬂuids 
may cause insuﬃcient bowel movement, vomiting, or sweating for it to clear, leading to further 
autointoxication as the toxins are caught in the bowels. Clear Heat ﬁrst if the deep pulses are 
rapid. If the tongue is pale, Qi has been consumed and there is inadequate Qi to elicit a clearing. 
To go forward, the Heat must be cleared ﬁrst, then the Fluids nourished. It is essential to have 
enough ﬂuids to ﬂush the toxins out through sweat or defecation, otherwise the ﬁre toxins will 
become systemic. (ST-44, LI-1, LI-2, and LI-11, all clear Heat. CV-12, ST-25 generate Fluids 
and move the bowels.) Te Divergent Channels are invaluable in this type of scenario, which 
includes many cancers. (See Advanced Acupuncture, A Clinic Manual.)
Flares
When either or both diaphragm and Dai Mai are tight or blocked, Qi can accumulate in a particular 
Jiao. Unable to move up or down, the trapped Qi causes swelling toward the sides.

General Color of the Tongue Body 
Te color of the tongue body is aﬀected by:
• Heat and whether the Heat is moving or trapped
• the quantity of blood going to the tongue and the freedom of that ﬂow
• the availability of the various Fluids and their free ﬂow 
• Cold
•  stagnation in the channels that connect to the tongue, the Spleen Primary, Kidney Primary, 
and Heart Luo Channel
 Some starting points for diagnosis are below:
1.  A pink tongue body is normal. 
2.  A red tongue body is reﬂecting the presence of Heat.
3.   A purple tongue body is reﬂecting either advanced Heat or blood stagnation due to 
Cold or Heat.
4.  A blue tongue body is reﬂecting Cold due to the exhaustion of Yang Qi. 
5.  A blackened tongue body is showing deep illness due to advanced Heat. 
Beyond those basic observations, ﬁne-tune your diagnosis with the following: 
If the tongue:
• is pink (translated from “pale red”), it is healthy. 
•  is pale, with tight and rapid pulses at the deep level and very weak Kidney pulses, there may 
be long-term stagnation. Long-term Heat that accumulates as a response to stagnation 
consumes Qi, resulting in Qi and Yang deﬁciencies. A pale and purple tongue results as 
there is not enough Qi to move Blood.  
• is pale with slightly slow pulses, Yang Qi is not fully circulating.
•  is pale with slightly slippery pulses, Spleen Qi has become weak, causing Dampness to 
accumulate. Tis has compromised the movement of Spleen Qi and with it, blood, to the 
tongue. 
•  is pale, with weak pulses in the guan position, there may be insuﬃcient Stomach Fire 
(gastric acid), due to the inability of the Spleen to ascend Yang Qi to the Stomach, 
resulting in further taxation on the Spleen Qi as it struggles to transform, transport and 
manufacture Blood. Tis is a circuitous or spiraling situation addressed by breaking the 
pattern with moxa and diet.

Small Spots on the Tongue 
Tis short section is about small spots, even specks, on the tongue, (not protrusions, lumps or 
swellings). Spots on the tongue indicate stagnation. Something is not moving adequately or is 
trapped. Tese days, I am seeing a lot of stagnation. Even people who claim not to eat much 
sugar are eating so many hidden sugars that their blood can be seriously sticky. Add to that the 
toxic fats converted from toxic sugars and we see widespread slowing of Blood in the channels 
and in the Heart. 
But spots can also occur when the body intentionally stagnates Qi as it senses too much Heat. 
Tis is why spots often accompany tight pulses. At any stage of Heat progression, the body 
may feel it has enough strength to mount an attempt to stagnate or restrain Qi to prevent 
further invasion of that Heat. Tis prevents the subsequent consumption of a medium (Blood 
or Fluids). Te stagnation will show in the tongue as spots, the color of which enables you to 
hone your diagnosis. 
If the tongue has:
• pale spots, there is insuﬃcient Blood to move pathology out. 
• red spots, heat is trapped in the interior, due to stagnation. 
• purple spots, there is blood stagnation and often emotional trauma. 
• dark spots, toxins are beginning to form. Tere is damage to tissue. 
• black spots, Heat is trapped and aﬀecting or consuming Yuan Qi. 
•  white spots, ﬁre toxins (free-radicals) are being expressed. Tere is often advanced damage

Other Observations About the Tongue Body 
If the tongue body is: 
•  shiny, Heat is consuming Qi and the Qi is escaping, projecting outward with a luminescence 
given by the Heat. You might think that a peculiar shine from the tongue is an aspect of the 
coat, or a sign of growing inner illumination. However, it’s likely to be an indication of Yang 
Qi escaping. Assess for long-term Yin depletion and nourish Yin. Sometimes new qigong, 
yoga or meditation students accelerate their practice prematurely, over-heating and drawing 
their Yang upward in an uncontrolled way. Tis also can show in a shiny tongue body. Te 
clinician can help by nourishing Yin to anchor Yang to prevent further Yang escaping. 
•  peeled, geographic, and shiny, there is a Yang deﬁciency due to the prolonged escaping of 
Yang and the consumption of Yin. Heat is now uncontrolled and the pathogen cannot be 
contained. Consolidate Qi in the Kidneys by anchoring the Qi down and nourishing Yin to 
provide ballast. KI-3 and KI-21 do this beautifully. 
• dry and small, there is exhaustion of Qi and Fluids

Sublingual Veins Reﬂect Yuan Qi  
Te sublingual area should be clear and have an openness to it. Te veins should be clearly visible, 
but not swollen. When they are otherwise, the sublingual area provides information about:
 
1. long-term pathology aﬀecting the Yuan-Constitutional level
 
2. pathology of the Heart Luo Channel and/or Shen disturbance
 
3. how much the Pericardium is controlling Blood 
 
4. how well blood is returning to the Heart 
 
5. how well the Spleen is managing Blood 
 
6. toxicity of the blood 
 
7. the Kidney Primary Channel
 
8. the Divergent Channels 
 
9. the Eight Extraordinary Channels
 
10. the Transverse Luo Channels
1. Long-term pathology aﬀects the Yuan-Constitutional level
Untreated long-term pathological accumulations in the Middle Jiao eventually aﬀect the Yuan 
level. Tis happens because Spleen ascension is then often compromised and the management 
of Blood weakened. As this post-natal Qi becomes exhausted trying to cope with pathology, 
the body reaches more deeply, calling upon Yuan-Source Qi to ﬁnance more management of 
the pathology. At some point, the Yuan level itself assumes the pathology, attempting to keep it 
latent. As pathology shifts from the Ying level (the gut) to the Yuan level, the signs that had been 
appearing in the central area of the tongue usually drain through the tongue, into the sublingual 
area. In other words, what was seen in the middle of the tongue will sink to the sublingual area 
as the pathology deepens. Often after that happens, strangely, the top surface of the tongue can 
improve in appearance, or even assume an almost normal appearance. It’s important to clear 
toxins from the constitutional level at the same time the gut is treated. (Bleeding TH-5 and GB-
37 is extremely eﬀective for this purpose and can change the appearance of the sublingual veins 
speedily.)

pathology moves to the Source level, to the constitution. Bleed Luo points, especially

2. Pathology of the Heart Luo Channel and/or Shen disturbance
Te Heart Luo Channel connects to the tongue, enabling articulation of one’s emotions. Repressed 
emotions resulting in Shen disturbance will show as stagnant purple veins. When Blood stagnation 
in the sublingual area concurs with symptoms of the Heart Luo Channel, the Heart Luo should 
be treated by letting a tiny amount of blood from HT-5 with a lancet. Symptoms include having a 
sense of being betrayed, yearning for someone or striving for something that is no longer available, 
and feeling that one’s dreams have been broken. Advanced signs include loss of speech following 
trauma or disappointment, denial of the reality of a disappointing situation, inability to talk about 
trauma, and a reactive attachment to intellectual thought. Treatment of advanced signs requires 
bleeding and immediate moxa at HT-5. (See Advanced Acupuncture, a Clinic Manual for more 
detailed instruction.) 
Heart Luo Channel is implicated in the swelling of the sublingual veins if there is speech diﬃculty 
with nervousness, impeded tongue movement and reduced moisture.
3. Pericardium fails to adequately control Blood
If the tip of the tongue is constrained and raised, the sublingual area looks tight and restricted, 
and the patient is timid, shy, or depressed, the Pericardium is tightening to protect the Heart, 
limiting the ﬂow of Blood to the Heart. Emotions then become internalized (to the Heart Luo).

and causing chest pain, the sublingual veins become very swollen. Wind-Stroke can result. 
5. Spleen fails to manage Blood 
Te tonus of the tongue is governed by the Spleen’s ascension of Blood to the end of its 
channel—the tongue. A ﬂoppy tongue demonstrates that Spleen Qi is inadequate to create 
good ascension. If there is stagnation of Blood because Blood is not being moved suﬃciently, 
the Spleen will react by constantly trying to control Blood, resulting in dark sublingual veins. 
Note also that the Spleen can react similarly when there is a Blood deﬁciency. Te pulses will 
clarify this for you; if the pulse is thin or narrow at the moderate level of the Spleen or Liver 
positions, there is a Blood deﬁciency. If the same pulses are wiry, there is stagnation of Blood.

8. Divergent Channel Activity
Te Divergent Channels connect Wei Qi and the Yuan level to bring to latency a pathogenic 
factor that the body is not equipped to handle. Tey hide away pathology that would otherwise 
harm the organs. If the Divergent Channels fail to contain pathology, toxins begin to leak and 
aﬀect Jing-Essence. If the sublingual veins are distended and have small protrusions on them, ﬁre 
toxins (including free radicals) are present and active, escaping from the Jing. Tese protrusions 
can be various colors, including white. If there are petechiae (small red spots) on the posterior 
underside of the tongue, ﬁre toxins may be active or brewing. Treat using Triple Heater Divergent 
Channel or another method of equal potency.

surface, since this is a window into the constitution. Tese eruptions can include white dots, 
white patches, unusual localized swellings, and blackened lesions.

Te Tongue and the Transformation of Jing-Essence 
into into Shen-Spirit  
Much of my practice is centered on personal transformation. Many people come asking to work 
on the full and peaceful unfolding of their Destiny, their purpose in life. Tis is such an honor, to 
be a steward in the patient’s awakening and the emerging awareness of their role in this lifetime. 
As I work, I enjoy putting diﬀerent perspectives on things. For example, one can read pulses 
through any of many lenses: the channels, the ﬂuids, the levels of Qi, the anatomical regions. And 
within each of these categories there are sublenses—inﬁnitely deep layers. Te tongue is far less 
complicated but it, too, has an interesting alternative take—reading about the unfolding of Jing, 
the unfolding of personal Destiny. 
Te tapering of the tongue can be seen as a representation of the density of the various humors. 
Te rear of the tongue is wide and thick, since the Kidneys store Jing-Essence, the thickest, densest 
medium in the body. Te middle section of the tongue represents the ﬁrst stage of transformation 
of Jing-Essence since it unfolds into Blood and Qi. Te distal third of the tongue is the narrowest, 
since the Shen is pure light and has no density.
So looking at the tongue through this lens alone, one can get a sense of the Eight Extraordinary 
Channels. A questions to ask while examining the tongue is, “What can I see in the tongue that 
describes resistance to the unfolding of Destiny? What can I see in the tongue that describes 
resistance to the world? What can I see in the tongue that describes resistance to this life?”
Te shape of the tongue is key. Is it widest at the rear? Or is the rear of the tongue narrower than the 
middle? Let’s start there. If the tongue is narrow at the rear, Jing-Essence is depleted or restricted. 
Te container of Jing-Essence—the storage of the mediumship that contains Destiny—is broken. 
Te realization of Destiny occurs via the constant conversation the Heart has with the Kidneys. 
Te Heart inquires of the Kidneys about the nature of the Qi it is to carry through to its branches: 
the tongue and the eyes. Te Kidneys respond with instruction that manifests as inspiration. If the 
rear of the tongue is narrow, there is not suﬃcient ballast in the Lower Jiao to receive Heart Qi 
as part of the communication of Heart and Kidneys. While I think that it is not the business of 
the practitioner to announce this, I do think that the practitioner can notice it and then, through 
careful choice of channel, or simple awareness, allow the space for increased interaction between 
those two organs. It’s important for the practitioner to be mindful that even the resistance to the 
unfolding of one’s nature is inherently part of one’s destined path.

Tongue Reﬂexology   
Te Tongue Map  
It is only after determining the status of Fluids and Heat that we can responsibly be interested 
in the tongue map. Te ﬁrst consideration in tongue diagnosis is moisture, since hydration is 
crucial to all aspects of physiology. Even slight dehydration impairs every physiological function; 
damage to or deﬁciency of ﬂuids must be addressed if health is to be restored. Te status of Fluids 
is evident on the tongue coat and in the ﬂesh of the tongue body. Tis can manifest as excess 
ﬂuids on the tongue, accumulations of pathological ﬂuids (coagulated coating) on the tongue, 
the absence of any coat at all, swelling of the tongue body, and the actual shriveling of the tongue 
body. Te second consideration is color, as its technicolor display adds to our understanding of 
the severity of illness. 
Although many clinicians look at the tongue map ﬁrst, it’s most useful as the third consideration. 
Te tongue map is not a complete diagnostic key; it is a speciﬁc guide to the pathologies of Heat 
and of Fluids. Te tongue map reﬂects where Heat has transferred. Heat is generated in the 
Stomach in response to a pathogen, but Heat can transfer anywhere, creating damage to ﬂuids, 
tissues, and organs. It could rise to the Lungs, descend to the Large Intestine, transfer over to the 
Liver, aﬀect the Spleen, inﬂame the Liver, try to vent through the skin or sensory oriﬁces, etc. If 
you are using the Primary Channels to clear Heat, the tongue map is key to keeping treatments 
speciﬁc to the patient.17  
Organ Pathology  
Heart  
Te most forward region of the tongue reﬂects the Heart.

Te Heart moves Blood, expresses the Shen by opening to the tongue and the eyes. It shares love and joy, 
and controls relationships. Te Heart must be relaxed to honor its resident, the Shen, whose expression is 
the purpose of life. Connecting through the eyes and tongue, the Heart must be allowed to shine its light 
outward through the eyes, speak its truth, and circulate its enthusiasm. Heat showing in the Heart area 
may aﬀect any of these functions.

causing (renal) hypertension. 
•  Tere is diﬃculty with speech, an inability to communicate adequately, clearly, conﬁdently, or

the tongue, but also, the Pericardium region on the tongue itself reﬂects this restriction. 
As unexpressed emotions percolate in the body, creating pressure and then steam upward, 
hypertension and Heat diseases can result. Over time, the tip remains red as the body brings 
Heat to try to move this stagnation. Te sublingual veins then swell and darken with the lack 
of movement and with complications involving the Heart Luo Channel.

Asymmetry and the Map
Often you’ll see a discernible asymmetry in the tongue where the left and right sides are not 
mirror images of each other. To determine left-right diﬀerentiations in the tongue, note that the 
right side of the tongue reﬂects Yin and, therefore, the Yin organs. Te left side of the tongue 
reﬂects the Yang and the Yang organs. Use this information if the tongue is asymmetrical to 
reveal the meaning of the diﬀerences showing in the two halves. For example, if the two halves 
of the center area of the tongue appear diﬀerent from each other, the right side of center will give 
you more information about the Spleen. Te left side of center will

Liver and Gallbladder  
Te Gallbladder is reﬂected on the left lateral band of the tongue; the Liver on the right lateral 
band. (Te right side of the body reﬂects Yin and the left side of the body reﬂects Yang.) 
LR

Te Liver regulates and ensures the smooth ﬂow of Qi, stores Blood, engenders Heart Qi and Blood, 
brings Blood to the sinews and brain, opens to the eyes, houses the Hun, enables planning, nourishes 
the Kidneys, hair and nails, nourishes the Lower Jiao, and harmonizes the emotions. Te Gallbladder 
controls the bone and is the link between the post-natal arena and the Curious Organs.
When the sides of the tongue call your attention, the Liver and Gallbladder will be included 
in the thought process leading to diagnosis. Either there is Liver or Gallbladder pathology, 
or—if there is Heat showing along the sides of the tongue—the Liver and Gallbladder are 
engaged in responding to pathology. Te right side will show more strain if the Liver is more 
taxed than the Gallbladder; the left side will show more strain if the Gallbladder is overtaxed 
as it strains to support the Liver.

Many factors have been in play for a while by the time Qi constraint aﬀects the Liver, including

seeds. 
• Te diet is high in coﬀee,  chocolate, sugar, alcohol, hot spices, garlic, and onion. 
• Late bedtime has become a habit. 
• Emotional stress is unmanaged. 
• Te patient overworks.

Te Central Area of the Back of the Tongue   
Te central area of the back of the tongue reﬂects the Kidneys. Te Bladder is also at the rear 
but, being the Yang partner, sits anterior to the Kidneys.

Te Kidneys store Yin and Yang and are the root of the constitution. 
Te Bladder ﬂushes out the lower burner, controls the sinews, and conveys thought to movement and 
posture. 
When the center of the rear of the tongue features in your diagnosis, the Kidneys are dealing 
with pathology. 
Observations at the rear of the tongue are focused on moisture. 
•  If the rear of the tongue is dry or dry and cracked, the Bladder’s ability to move Fluids in the 
Lower Jiao—both pathological and not— is compromised. 
•  If the coat at the rear of the tongue is not rooted to the tongue (if you feel you would be able 
to wipe it oﬀ with a cloth if you tried—don’t try!), the body’s ability to hold on to Fluids is 
compromised. Tis could be due to constitutional weakness, severe disease or drug use that 
has weakened the constitution. Yang is then unable to hold the Yin in its place.  
•  If the rear of the tongue is very “cheesy-looking”, or very damp, the body is likely trying to 
build back Yin. 
• Te body could be trying to hold onto Fluids.

nourishes prenatal Qi.

 Te sides of the back of the tongue reﬂect the Large Intestine and the 
Small Intestine. 
 Te Large Intestine controls Jin-Tin ﬂuids. Since it has an outward 
vector, it is reﬂected in the outer edge of the sides of the rear of the tongue. 
Te Small Intestine separates pure and impure. It internalizes pathology

Observations at the side of the rear of the tongue are also focused on moisture. Te diﬀerentiation

•  If the sides of the rear of the tongue are withered or narrowing, Yin has been depleted and 
may be insuﬃcient to ﬁnance the latency of pathology.
•  If the coat at the sides of the rear is thickening, ﬂuids in the Lower Jiao are becoming more 
turbid or phlegmatic. Fluids are depleted, leading to systemic dehydration. 
• If the sides of the rear of the tongue are collapsed, Yang is not articulating within the Lower 
Jiao. 
•  If the sides of the rear of the tongue are swollen and red, there is Dampness responding to 
Heat in the Lower Jiao. Be careful not to drain Dampness until the Heat is cleared. 
•  If the medial aspect of the sides of the rear of the tongue are swollen, there is pathology of the 
Small Intestine (jejunum or ilium). 
•  If the sides of the rear of the tongue are absent, that is, if the tongue narrows at the rear, there 
is a serious deﬁciency of Yin. In general, this means that Fluids on a deep level, including 
hormones, are not being generated as they should be. Most often, at least in this part of the 
world, this is a result of Yin not being cultivated. Te patient declines to rest or make any 
part of their day calm. In our culture this is often the case in menopause: the patient is often 
stressed, failing to sleep or sleep early enough, and not eating enough saturated fat. 
When Yin is so deﬁcient that the rear of the tongue is wasting away, the ability to hold a 
pathogen in latency is severely compromised. Tis is because the body uses mediumship—
including Yin—to hold away from the organs, pathogens, and pathogenic factors that it cannot 
eradicate or expel. When Yin is this depleted, there is no buﬀer between the pathogen and 
the organs. Te pathogen becomes free to enter the peritoneum, then the organs, where the 
pathogen causes organ disease—the deepest form of pathology.

Triple Heater distributes Yuan Qi to the organs, clears Heat 
from the organs, controls latency and Qi, governs systemic 
irrigation, and manages surpluses of post-natal Qi to ensure 
longevity.

Te Coat and the Map  
Te tongue coat can vary from area to area. Find where there is a lack of moisture on the tongue. 
Tis is an important factor in diagnosis. 
If there is dryness or absence of coat found:
•  in the Lung area, Lung Yin is deﬁcient. If the area is inﬂamed or irritated, there may be a 
dry cough, dry skin, or a dry throat.
•  in the Spleen and Stomach area, Stomach Yin is deﬁcient—the mucosal lining is thinning. 
Tere may be dry lips, gut sensitivities, allergies. 
•  on the rear of the tongue, there is a serious depletion of ﬂuids in the Lower Jiao, including 
perhaps the ﬂuids of the constitution—the Jing. Long-term Heat may have damaged the 
Lower Jiao. Te Kidneys and the Intestines may be very dry and thinning as the illness has 
advanced far beyond simple constipation. Tere must be Yin available so that the Lower Jiao 
is able to ﬂush and clear itself; otherwise, toxins accumulate, showing as bumps on the rear 
of the tongue. Eventually, these can reenter the bloodstream and cause disease. Clear toxins 
as you hydrate. 
If the tongue has a clumping coat (often said to resemble cottage cheese), whether thick or just a 
hint:
•  on the Lower Jiao area of the tongue, there may be Damp-Heat type inﬂammation in the 
intestines or genitalia. 
•  on the Middle Jiao area of the tongue, there may be Damp-Heat in the Liver or Gallbladder, 
or parasites in the gut (including candida), or a severe inadequacy in the digestive tract’s 
capacity to separate pure from impure ﬂuids. 
•  on the Upper Jiao area of the tongue, there may be Damp-Heat in the sinuses, the upper 
lymphatic system, or the brain (known in Western medicine as candida with brain fog).
Hollowing  
Hollowing of the Jiaos shows that an organ related to that region is in a Qi or Yang deﬁcient state. 
•  Hollowing of the Upper Jiao reﬂects a Qi deﬁciency of Lungs or Heart.
•  Hollowing of the Middle Jiao reﬂects a Qi deﬁciency in the digestive tract, often a Spleen 
Qi deﬁciency.
• Hollowing of the Lower Jiao indicates a Kidney Yang deﬁciency.

Compensations  
Te tongue will swell when the body accumulates pathological Yin as it tries to compensate for 
missing mediumship or blocked communication between organs. 
•  Te Lung region may swell as the Lungs gather Yin and send it to the Kidneys to nourish 
Kidney Yin. If communication between Jiaos is blocked, locate the blockage, release it, and 
then nourish Yin. Te communication will start up again and the deﬁcit will be restored.
•  Te Kidney region may be swollen as they restrain their own Yin, due to Yin deﬁciency. 
Nourish Yin so that they can resume their support of the Heart.  
•  Te right side of the tongue may swell as the Liver struggles to store suﬃcient Blood. 
Nourish Blood.
•  Te left side of the tongue may swell as the Gallbladder tries to contain Dampness. To 
determine whether the Damp is pathological or responding to Heat, take the pulses. 
If the deep pulses are rapid, the Dampness is protective and must not be drained. Te 
Gallbladder region may also swell if it is trying to buﬀer toxins from the Jing. 
Trapped Heat  
Blockages between organs and Jiaos can result in the buildup of Heat as movement of 
mediumship and Qi is stiﬂed. Heat trapped in any area will consume Fluids, Yin, and eventually, 
the ﬂesh. If not treated, it can lead to Wei Atrophy Syndrome. Petechiae (red dots) are early 
signs of trapped Heat. As the Heat stagnates further, the dots can become progressively darker, 
becoming crimson and even purple. If very long-term, petechiae can turn white, indicating the 
presence of ﬁre toxins (in Western terms, free radicals, due to damage from inﬂammation). 
Needling Ying-Spring points, bleeding Luo points, and treating other points that release Heat 
are key to treatment, along with freeing the entrapment.

Te Simpliﬁed Map
Sometimes the tongue sends a message best read using a 
simpliﬁed map.  Tese messages should be read ﬁrst, before 
noting complexities. Te simplest (and oldest) tongue map 
consists of three areas corresponding to the three levels of Qi, 
drawn almost like ripples emerging forward from the rear of 
the tongue.  Te most posterior portion of the tongue shows 
the status of Yuan-Constitutional Qi, the ﬁrst ripple (the 
middle region) shows the status of Ying-Nutritive Qi, and the 
second ripple—the edge of the tongue—shows the status of 
Wei-Defensive Qi.  Tis maps is most useful when a tongue 
presents with a distinct redness all the way around the edge in the outermost ripple. I refer to 
such a tongue as a Wei-Qi tongue. It is common in people with skin issues including eczema 
and other rashes, allergies, autoimmune issues in an active phase, or any kind of inﬂammation 
aﬀecting the exterior.

Lines and Cracks 
Lines, cracks, and protrusions show that unresolved Heat is resulting in the consumption of Yin 
or that the diet and lifestyle are resulting in dryness. 
Folding Lines in the Tongue  
Heat originates in the Stomach, is reﬂected in the Center of the tongue, and expands outward. 
When dryness arises as a result of Heat, or when dryness is an underlying factor, the body tries 
to hold on to as much ﬂuid as possible. Folding lines begin to arise in the tongue, demonstrating 
the eﬀort of holding on to Fluids.  
 
Folding lines
Roughness, Unevenness in the Tongue  
Roughness or unevenness in the tongue can be thought of as a pre-crack. Tere is deﬁciency and 
a consequent stagnation as the body tries to hold on to what it can, but the tongue has not yet 
cracked. 
Roughness

Cracks in the Tongue  
Cracks in the tongue are caused by Heat drying the tongue enough to crack the tongue ﬂesh, 
just  as the sun cracks desert earth. Sometimes it is said that cracks reﬂect consumption of 
Blood more than other media. Heat can be introduced in various ways, but it can also be 
generated by the body in response to pathology. Cracks can also appear in situations where 
there is a deﬁciency of Fluids, Blood, or Jing, and the body is not able to gather pathological 
Yin (Dampness) in response. Tis scenario occurs when there is a Qi or Yang deﬁciency. 
Introduced Heat can come from: 
•  consuming substances that produce Heat (stimulants or irritants). Tese include: coﬀee, 
sugar, chocolate, garlic, onion, fried foods, cigarettes, alcohol, drugs, hot spices (such as 
black pepper and spicy hot red capsicum peppers), pesticides, herbicides, preservatives, 
and too much protein (especially overindulging in very Yang proteins such as beef, lamb, 
bison, venison, lobster, shrimp, and crab). 
• engaging in a very Yang, rushed, stressing view of life 
• engaging in heat-generating emotions: anger, anxiety and worry
• long-term infections, usually bacterial 
Cracks caused by Heat are diagnosed by area. Te tongue map generally reveals that cracks caused 
by: 
• dietary factors, which will ﬁrst show in the tongue center 
• grief and smoking, which will show in the Lung region 
• anger and toxins from drugs and alcohol, which will show on the tongue sides 
• worry, which will show as a midline crack 
• anxiety, which will show with tightening or withdrawal at the tip of the tongue 
•  long-term inﬂammation, which will show in the Lung or Liver areas since they control 
Wei-Defensive Qi 
•  constitutional factors, and sometimes fear, that has taxed the Kidneys, which will show in 
the Lower Jiao

Transverse (Yin) Cracks  
Transverse cracks show dryness. Te patient is dehydrated. 
Te treatment principle is to moisten dryness to generate ﬂuids. 
If there are transverse cracks with Dampness—if the tongue is very 
swollen—Damp should be cleared as you generate Fluids. But there 
must be Qi present to carry out that Damp clearing. If the tongue is 
pale, there is insuﬃcient Qi to clear the Dampness. In those cases, 
tonify Qi before you ask the body to generate Fluids.
Generally, transverse cracks tend to localize, to be concentrated in one area. Cracks can migrate 
as the Heat resulting from dryness looks for alternative locations to vent. Tis shows as cracks 
that develop Y-shaped junctions, new cracks that branch oﬀ from other cracks, and jagged turns. 
Over time, increasingly depleted resources (deﬁciency of mediumship) leads to the arising of 
Wind. Te Wind shows in lengthening of the cracks. If the cracks extend all the way to the 
sides of the tongue, the Liver has become extremely dry. 
Dryness  
Left untreated, Heat leads to full-blown Dryness. Te term Dryness refers to dehydration that 
has become advanced enough to inﬂame the huge complex membrane (the peritoneum) that 
wraps around each organ. In the long-term, Heat is able to penetrate the actual organ and 
the disease becomes serious. Moisture must be returned and Heat cleared before the organs 
atrophy. If a patient reports that they drink much more than they urinate out, the body is 
retaining ﬂuids to respond to and contain Heat (inﬂammation). 
Early stage Dryness can show in: 
• a dry coat, leaving the tongue lacking in luster or even appearing powdery 
• a peeling dry coat leaving patches of coat remaining 
• a completely evaporated coat 
• many horizontal cracks as Heat threatens to breach the peritoneum
Late stage Dryness can manifest in:
•  signiﬁcant horizontal cracks in a given area indicating severe inﬂammatory illness in the 
anatomical region reﬂected by that part of the tongue

• cracks that are lengthening over time 
• cracks extending to the sides

Late stage dryness has set in with this patient. Signiﬁcant horizontal

now severe and affecting the Ye-Thick Fluids, resulting in symptoms 
described as infertility and hypothyroidism. Signiﬁcant and consistent 
changes in diet and lifestyle in addition to treatment are required for 
healing this stage.
Very often the patient is not helping because they avoid water. Such a patient will often say they 
“don’t like water” and dehydrate themselves by drinking ﬂuids that are actively dehydrating—
coﬀee, all teas, anything carbonated. All are diuretics. Tis becomes complicated because although 
water is essential and helpful, it is not the best hydrator for the digestive tract, but rather a ﬂushing 
agent. True hydrators that are profoundly helpful are soups, congees, broths, porridges and stews.
Cracks as Heat Respond to Dampness  
If Heat is responding to Dampness, the tongue will often be very swollen with a yellow coat. Te 
coat becomes increasingly yellow as Heat accumulates in the eﬀort to overcome the Dampness. If 
Heat is allowed to predominate, Dryness will arise.
If a tongue is swollen and yet has a lot of horizontal cracks, more Yin is needed to conduct the 
clearing of Dampness. Hydration is required to move and ﬂush it out. At ﬁrst, this may seem 
counterintuitive, but the body will only release Dampness when it receives a clear and sustained 
message that the required Fluids are steadily available.

Vertical (Yang) Cracks  
Vertical cracks indicate that Heat is damaging ﬂuids. Te treatment 
principle is to clear Heat. 
Heat ultimately ulcerates and consumes Yin. Te emotions are a 
common cause of this kind of pathology as they stagnate Qi. Te body 
then creates and accumulates Heat to to try to move that Qi.  
 
Horizontal Versus Vertical Cracks  
Horizontal cracks occur when there is Dryness from dehydration. Tere are not enough ﬂuids 
in the diet. Te treatment principal is to nourish Fluids, generate Fluids, or moisten Dryness. 
Vertical cracks occur when Heat—originating from the diet or from worry, stress and 
overthinking—is consuming Fluids and Yin. Te treatment principal is to clear Heat, and/or 
to calm the Shen. 
To be very simple, vertical cracks—particularly those down the center—are usually due to 
overthinking, worry, or stress. Horizontal cracks relate to physical dehydration. 
Geographic Tongue  
Eventually, long-term Heat radiates throughout the tongue, causing cracks all over it, resulting 
in the geographic tongue. Sometimes a coat (usually a partial one) complicates a geographic 
tongue as the body responds with Dampness, attempting to extinguish the Heat. Te areas of 
the tongue that are missing coat altogether are said to have Dryness. 
In a geographic tongue (covered in cracks):
•  if the sides of the tongue have no coat, there is Dryness in the 
Liver and Gallbladder. 
•  if the middle of the tongue has no coat, there is dryness in the 
Spleen and Stomach. 
•  if the front third of the tongue has no coat, there is dryness in 
the Lungs. 
•  if the rear third of the tongue has no coat, there is dryness of 
the Kidneys, or the Lower Jiao in general. 
•  if the entire tongue is littered with protrusions and cracks, heat is drying up the tongue 
itself.

Cracks in Center  
Heat generated by worry—circular thinking about negative possibilities—consumes digestive 
ﬂuids, as digestive Qi is diverted to try to control thought. Te stagnated Qi causes Heat to 
accumulate and damage these Fluids. In this way,  worry or overthinking eventually opens a 
characteristic midline crack. Cracks can lengthen over time as the habit of overthinking persists.
Lingering disappointment also taxes the Spleen, since it involves 
thoughts that persist over time. Te Middle Jiao Heat caused by 
stagnating thoughts depletes Qi and creates a midline crack that 
eventually extends into the Heart region. 
Heat in the digestive tract from too much Yang food—for example, 
too much protein—can show in the middle, but generally appears 
on the sides, since the Liver becomes aggravated. (See Cracks in the 
Sides, below.) 
This man’s digestive tract shows an enormous amount of Heat. As Heat has a tendency to 
move, these habits have created a systemic urgency. As a result, he speaks very quickly, is 
highly reactive to stimulus, is hyperactive in the gut, urgent defecation, and has seizures. 
A history of viral encephalitis (at age nineteen) suggests Heat is a long-term feature in his 
pathology. Wind resulting from the Heat has affected the Qiao Mai, resulting in serious 
interference with the structure, given the Western diagnosis of scoliosis. Chinese Medical 
diagnosis was systemic Heat generating Wind at Yang Qiao Mai.
A crack or pockets in the middle of a peeled tongue indicate Heat is not just damaging Stomach 
Fluids but also Stomach Yin (the Stomach lining). Remember that the coat reﬂects Fluid status 
while the tongue body reﬂects Yin and structure, ﬂesh. As the crack deepens, it indicates that 
Heat is reaching to the material of the body, in this case, Stomach Yin (lining). 
A deep crack that extends from the center toward the rear of the tongue indicates Heat is moving 
from the Ying level into the Yuan level. Te Heat is aﬀecting the constitution. It’s rare to see 
a deep vertical crack extend to the rear of the tongue because the body will ﬁercely guard the 
constitution and respond with Dampness, creating a thick coat at the rear of the tongue.

This patient’s Heat sought two routes of exit, the Upper and the Lower Jiao. The Heat 
arose chieﬂy through smoking, which weakened Lung Qi, in turn stymying Wei Qi. The 
body responded to the Heat by producing Dampness, leading to Damp-Heat. Heat 
rose to the Upper Jiao, creating hypertension and anxiety. If an upward clearance 
of Damp-Heat is incomplete due to insufﬁcient Wei Qi, the sensory oriﬁces become 
blocked with the Damp-Heat. (This led to a reported diagnosis of rhinitis.) Next, 
the body tried to drain the Heat through the Lower Jiao. Again, deﬁcient Lung Qi 
responsible for clearing the Lower Jiao caused the accumulation of Damp-Heat there, 
resulting in symptoms that led to the emergence of cysts along the paravertebral 
muscles, and the reported diagnoses of kidney stones, cystitis, and enlarged prostate. 
The weakening of the Kidneys, given their taxation, led to lower back pain. The 
diagnosis given by my colleague was emptiness of the Bladder Luo Channel.
Some people are born with a deep crack, indicating constitutional trauma. In children’s tongues, 
deep cracks that were not present at birth show trauma to the constitution that likely occurred 
before the age of three. Chong Mai treatments will treat these issues and can ameliorate such 
cracks. 
Cracks in the Tip 
Heat generated by anxiety, worry, and disappointment causes cracks extending from the center 
of the tongue to the tip of the tongue. 
Anxiety diﬀers from worry in that it can be about any outcome, 
negative or positive. One might be anxious about a second date 
even after the ﬁrst date went wonderfully well. Anxiety eventually 
aﬀects the Heart (the tip). Worry is related to negative outcomes; 
as, imagining an accident, one might worry about a child forgetting 
their skateboarding helmet. Eventually the depletion and subsequent 
stagnation that result from anxiety can cause in a vertical crack in 
the tip. 
  
Cracks Just Behind or Lateral to the Tip  
Cracks just behind or just lateral to the tip of the tongue are associated with the Pericardium. 
Sometimes they appear at 45 degree angles and most often they appear as folds or tight-
looking grooves. As we know, the sides of the tongue reﬂect the Liver and the tip reﬂects the 
Heart. Heat generated by Liver constraint travels upward (showing along the sides toward the 
tip) and is intercepted by the Pericardium. Te Heat fans out as the Pericardium disperses the 
Heat, buﬀering and protecting the Heart. If insuﬃciently warded oﬀ by the Pericardium, Heat 
rises and aﬀects the Heart, leading to anxiety, insomnia, or angry outbursts.

Cracks in the Lung Region  
As the Heart communicates downward, it can transfer this Heat to the Kidneys. Since the Kidneys 
are consolidators, Heat can become trapped there.18  Te Kidneys try to vent the Heat through the 
Bladder.19  Meanwhile, the Stomach or the Liver—if untreated—continues to generate Heat and 
the Heat continues to rise to the chest. Ultimately, the Heat accumulates in the chest and aﬀects 
the Lungs. Tis mechanism produces cracks in the Lung area. 
The mechanism described above is active in this patient. An overheating diet and alcohol 
led to excess Heat, which rose to the Lungs and was transferred to the Lower Jiao, 
desiccating it. Concurrently, long-term cortisone use weakened the Kidneys, leaving them 
unable to move the Heat through the Bladder. The Heat eventually became trapped in 
the Upper Jiao, where it affected the Lungs, causing Heat to move to the skin—another 
Lung-mediated exit— since the Lungs control the exterior and also move Blood. The Liver, 
constrained by the action of trying to block the Heat coming back up, is too tight to be able 
to store adequate blood at night to prevent nightmares, and to manage Damp-Heat. This is 
the cause of the current symptoms described by his Western doctor as cutaneous vasculitis 
and hypercholesterolemia. 
Cracks only appearing in the Lung region might not relate to the progression process. If Heat is 
localized in the Lung area, these cracks might be signs of direct emotional stress associated with 
the Lungs: the experience of loss, grief, or sadness. Climatic dryness, smoking, and other pollution 
can also produce Dryness in the Lungs.

Again, Heat in the digestive tract arising from too much Yang food in the diet, such as protein,

response to the heightened quantity of acids needed to digest meats and fats. Overtaxation of 
the Liver and Gallbladder, due to excess meat in the diet, can show as cracks in the sides of the 
tongue. 
This woman craved sugar and was unable to stop eating it every time she started. 
Her diet was high in chicken, a particularly Yang (heating) food. A ganglion at LU-9 
elicited a question about her emotional history and she reported having had a major 
disappointment twenty years earlier. The depression in the Lung area may reﬂect that. 
The tremendous Heat seen through the coat in the center is visibly moving to the Lower 
Jiao, where the body is responding with Dampness. The strain of the excess Yang animal 
protein and sugar has taxed the Liver and Gallbladder to the point of cracking the 
sides of the tongue. Since the Gallbladder is the link between the post-natal arena and 
the prenatal arena, the Heat gained entree to the Jing-Essence, showing as cracking in 
the Lower Jiao. On its way, Dai Mai attempted to contain the Heat, showing as added 
redness across the Dai Mai region. She arrived at my colleague’s ofﬁce following a 
diagnosis of breast cancer (left breast) and radiation treatment. Her treatments are 
focused in the clearing of Heat and the Triple Heater Divergent Channel.   
Cracks at the Rear 
Heat in the Kidneys cracks the rear of the tongue. A crack extending from the rear of the tongue 
to the center can indicate a constitutional weakness, since there is insuﬃcient constitutional 
Yin for the digestive tract to acquire. 
Deep roughness or cracks with dampness inside them can appear in the Lower Jiao area if the 
Liver is struggling in its attempt to control Heat, Damp, or Wind. Tis happens as Liver Qi 
becomes constrained by continually pushing downward, reversing its natural ﬂow, to prevent 
Heat becoming rampant. As the condition worsens, the Lower Jiao area of the tongue becomes 
rough or cracked with a thickening coat. Tis type of constraint would manifest as headaches, 
abdominal signs, and Wind signs. 
This patient experiences frustration as her own needs are unmet and unexpressed 
while she tends only others. Heat originated in the Middle Jiao, where it resulted in 
two intestinal surgeries and an appendectomy. The Heat has been shifted from the 
Middle to the Lower Jiao, cracking the Lower Jiao region of the tongue and causing 
kidney stones, urgency in the bowels, and spontaneous abortions.

Cracks in a Pale Tongue 
In cases of excess Stomach Fire, as long-term Heat damages 
Fluids, the tongue can crack open. Stomach Fire can arise as the 
body intentionally generates Heat in response to pathology. Often 
Stomach Fire results from the chronic dehydration left in the wake 
of eating very Yang foods and processed foods, especially sugar, hot 
spices, garlic, and alcohol. But generally, midline Stomach cracks are 
due to overthinking, from mentally generated Stomach Fire. Most 
people in our culture have overactive minds due to lack of calm, 
unstructured time. Tinking stresses the Spleen, stagnating Qi in the 
Middle Jiao, causing Heat to accumulate in the digestive tract. Excess ﬁre burns and depletes the 
Spleen, damaging the mechanism of transformation. Tis is the mechanism of worry contributing 
to Stomach ulcers. Over time, Qi is consumed by Heat and the tongue is left pale and often 
swollen with a deep midline crack.  
Severe chronic internal Cold can also crack the tongue open. But the tongue will be purplish or 
bluish. 
 
Reading the Response to Pathology 
We might expect the tongue to be dry when it is cracked, but the body tries to reduce the 
adverse eﬀects of Heat by responding with Dampness. If there is a sticky coat within the cracks, 
Dampness is controlling the Heat. Sometimes Dampness looks as though it is actually trying 
to glue the tongue back together. Sometimes you’ll see patches of a sticky phlegm-like coat over 
divots, crevices, areas of missing ﬂesh, and tongue ulcers. Tis is all a response to Heat pathology. 
Tis Dampness is not pathological and should not be cleared. It’s vital in controlling the Heat, 
buying you and the patient time. Clear the actual stimulus behind the creation of the Dampness, 
that is, clear the Heat. If you try to rid the Dampness, you are telling the body it is reacting 
inappropriately; you’re sedating the immune system instead of supporting it. Symptoms will 
always worsen if you treat the response instead of the actual pathology.

Protrusions and Froth 
Eruptions, Varicosities, and Torns 
Protrusions, including eruptions, varicosities, and bumps known as thorns, reﬂect the body’s 
unsuccessful attempts to push a pathogenic factor out to the exterior. When Qi is deﬁcient 
and therefore unable to clear pathology, the body stagnates Qi to prevent its further loss. Te 
pathogen then sits in the body, unresolved. To create change, Qi must be replenished, the 
bowels must be moving daily, and the diet must shift to become one of food-based hydration 
(soups, stews, congees). 
If Qi is very weak, and eradication is still diﬃcult, food-based hydration remains essential 
because the pathogenic factor must be able to go into latency so as not to be a systemic loose 
cannon.
Protrusions vary in size from tiny petechiae to giant thorns (bumps) at the rear. Protrusions in 
small quantities are common along the sides and especially at the rear of the tongue. Markedly 
elevated or plentiful protrusions are less common.  
Tiny protrusions often occur in clusters in the distal two thirds of the tongue, showing Qi 
gathering in the Middle or Upper Jiaos. Qi trapped in the Upper Jiao causes the body to 
respond with Heat as it tries to move the stagnant Qi, causing the tip to become red. Te 
protrusions shift to the tip and cluster together. Minor protrusions at the rear of the tongue 
may indicate inﬂammation of the mucous membranes of the uterus or bladder.
Large protrusions at the back of the tongue can indicate the stagnation of fecal matter, intestinal 
polyps, masses in the Lower Jiao, or an advanced disease state. If the tongue is rough (very 
uneven) with protrusion and cracks, Heat is damaging the Lower Jiao. Te further back the 
protrusion, the more deeply aﬀected the Lower Jiao. When signiﬁcant protrusions appear at 
the rear of the tongue, high protein diets are contraindicated, since they produce Heat that the 
system can no longer tolerate.  
If there are many protrusions, the body is losing its capacity to hold pathology latent. Tere 
may be an overﬂow of ﬁre toxins in the Jing. Te tongue may be covered in thorns, indicating 
the condition requires urgent treatment. Te tongue can concurrently become pale even though 
there is rampant Heat, as Heat consumes Qi, Yin, and Blood. It might become shiny, indicating 
that Qi has been consumed.

Protrusion Color 
Protrusions are generally red, purple, or white. Tey can also present as the same color as the 
tongue as Heat consumes mediumship.
•  Red protrusions indicate Heat (inﬂammation), either from Heat itself or from Heat 
responding to stagnation. 
•  Purple protrusions show stagnation. 
•  White protrusions often indicate ﬁre toxins—the Heat is so systemic and so prolonged that 
the redness has reached another level, transforming to white, like ash in a ﬁreplace. 
•  Protrusions that are the same or a color similar to the tongue itself are either in their early 
stage, or Blood or Yang has been consumed leaving them pale. 
Note: Petechiae, though sometimes slightly raised, are not considered bona ﬁde protrusions. Petechiae 
are dots of discoloration and usually congregate. Protrusions are literally emerging from the tongue, are 
raised above the surface of the tongue, and are scattered.

dearth of mediumship and the tension in the Gallbladder Channel, affecting in turn Dai

bowels. The toxins accrued in the intestines, as shown in the presence of many thorns in that 
region. The patient presented for acupuncture following a Western diagnosis of ulcerative 
colitis.

Froth 
Froth indicates that the body is having diﬃculty moving one medium (Blood, Fluids, or Yin) to 
support a diﬀerent medium. Te most common cause of froth is persistent emotions in a person 
trying but unable to change. Since the emotions are held in the Blood, and persistent emotions 
create tremendous Heat, Blood is consumed. Te Liver becomes constrained as it tries to hold 
on to more Blood. Yin is stirred up both by the ﬁre created by the emotions, and also as the body 
reaches more deeply for resources. It strives to move one medium to support another, constraining 
Qi to reduce consumption of those resources. Meanwhile, the body is trying to compensate for 
the lack of a medium by holding on to pathological Fluids (Damp). Te stirring of Yin and 
pathological Damp can develop into Wind-Phlegm and rise to the sensory oriﬁces, creating tics 
and tremors, or at worst, epileptic seizures where the Wind-Phlegm presents as foaming at the 
mouth. Deep crying and violent outbursts can erupt as further expression of this rebellious Qi. 
Te patient is unable to cultivate calm as they seek but resist the change they need.20  Te seizures 
will persist until the mind is able to resolve the emotions. 
If the froth is near the tip of the tongue, Liver constraint is insulting Lung Yin. Te Liver must 
be regulated. Froth at the sides of the tongue indicate the eﬀort of moving Blood to support Yin. 
If the Liver is constrained, this is diﬃcult and the stirring of resources creates froth over the Liver 
and Gallbladder areas. Froth at the back of the tongue indicates Kidney Yin is deﬁcient and both 
Lung Yin and Liver Blood are moving down to support the Kidneys with urgency. Te rushed 
movement creates the frothing. If Yang is ﬂoating, the Kidneys move Lung ﬂuids back up again, 
and the rear of the tongue becomes frothy. Te treatment principle is to separate pure and turbid 
Fluids in the Lungs (CV-9 and CV-12 work beautifully), then nourish the deﬁcient medium.

Froth in an anxious patient with escaping Yang due to a Yin deﬁciency. The Lower Jiao is sluggish due to a diaphragmatic 
blockage preventing communication between Upper and Lower Jiaos. Dampness has accumulated in the Lower Jiao in response 
to the Yin deﬁciency, further complicating the lack of movement. The body tries to move Qi in the Lower Jiao to reestablish 
communication with the Upper Jiao, stirring froth as it does so.

<!-- p.129 -->
110 |    Tongue Diagnosis
12 
Tongue Diagnosis and the Complement Channels  
To assess the status of the Eight Extraordinary Channels using tongue diagnosis, look for features 
that demonstrate the principal signs of each channel. 
Te Eight Extraordinary Channels and Tongue Diagnosis
Te Chong Mai Tongue 
Chong Mai contains the blueprint of our individual existence. It governs the way our bodies 
develop and sets the curriculum for one’s lifetime. Living to one’s full potential requires the 
conﬁdence to form, embrace, and nurture relationships. When Chong Mai has not been nurtured 
both in utero and during the ﬁrst two years of life, that is, if basic physiological and psychological 
needs have not been met (such as loving touch, protection, soothing, feeding), a reaction brews in 
the abdominal muscles. A hardened, raised muscular tension in the abdominal muscles on either 
side of the midline will emerge. Tis is known as chopstick Qi or the hidden beams, a protective 
mechanism, armoring both against being shunned and against further trauma to the Chong 
Channel. Te patient will unwittingly withdraw from or block themselves from forming close 
relationships. 
Te corresponding tongue has the same two hidden beams of defense showing, usually in the 
anterior two thirds of the tongue. Generally, the tongue can appear purple or pale purple due 
to the restraint of Qi. Any combination of Chong Mai signs and symptoms may be present. If 
Chong pathology is advanced, you may see the hidden beams appear on the rear of the tongue, 
indicating that a weak Spleen has forced Jing-Essence to break down to support Blood. Regardless 
of the location of the beams, you’ll ﬁnd that the degree the Spleen is ascending is not balanced 
by the Stomach’s descending Qi, leaving the patient with a sense of never feeling fully in balance. 
Te distribution of Qi to the ﬁve limbs is compromised by tightness in the abdomen. SP-4 is a 
principal point for the harmonization of Stomach and Spleen; if needled with strong intention, it 
can release the rails in the abdomen and therefore in the tongue, restoring free movement of Qi 
in the Middle Jiao.

<!-- p.130 -->
111
Tongue Diagnosis and the Complement Channels    |
Chong Mai ﬁrst trajectory.
Chong Mai ﬁrst and  
second trajectory.
Te Ren Mai Tongue 
Ren Mai is the Sea of Yin; it provides the resources the body requires to create, build, and repair 
itself. Also, as the channel propelling the need to bond for survival (initially to the mother), it 
generates the feeling of belonging. 
If Yin is depleted, Ren Mai senses that it cannot perform these functions and stagnates Yin 
in response. Terefore the Ren Mai tongue will show evidence of both Yin stasis and Yin 
deﬁciency. Te tongue will be swollen in places, perhaps with protrusions (bumps) as the 
accumulation of Yin becomes increasingly pathological and will be peeled in patches. It may 
be retracted, unable to fully extend outward. Looking underneath the tongue, one sees that 
the tonicity of the frenulum may be noticeably altered, causing it to look tight or ﬂaccid. Te 
frenulum, which reﬂects more Du Mai, will look looser than normal if Ren Mai is overactive. 
This tongue shows a Yin deﬁciency in that 
the tongue is thin and shows Yin stasis in the 
reactive accumulation of Yin (bumps) in the 
rear of the tongue. The body is stagnating 
Blood and directing it to the Lower Jiao where 
it will break down to replenish Yin.
This is a more classic Ren Mai tongue. The 
coat is peeling off in patches while the 
body works to gather pathological Yin, 
reﬂected in the thickening yellow coat.

<!-- p.131 -->
112 |    Tongue Diagnosis
Te Du Mai Tongue 
Du Mai is the Sea of Yang, the origin of all Yang, source of the Qi that warms the digestive tract 
and therefore is responsible for the derivation of post-natal Qi from food. It supplies the Yang 
Qi needed for all movement, all immune function, and creates upward actions including the 
development of the upright stance. Du Mai governs independence, individuality, animation, and 
the quest to know the world. When Du Mai is out of balance, the patient will not be comfortable 
moving forward in life. Tis can go in two directions: either there will be overdependence on 
others, with an unwillingness to learn about the world, or there will be rebellion against that 
outside world, accompanied by hyperactivity.
If Du Mai is in a state of excess, meaning it is expressing too much Yang—creating, for example, 
headaches, seizures, dizziness, or numbness—the tongue will dart out, be quite red, and possibly 
shaky (the shaking means the Kidney Channel is also aﬀected). Tere will be an accumulation of 
Heat in the Upper Jiao region of the tongue. When Du Mai is in a state of deﬁciency, the tongue 
may be ﬂaccid and droop. In advanced states, the rear of the tongue may collapse and the tongue 
may be pale and damp as there is insuﬃcient Yang to support the Spleen. 
Te tonicity of the frenulum reﬂects the status of Du Mai; when the frenulum is very tight, 
Du Mai is overactive. Pathologies included in this presentation include exuberant Yang, internal 
Wind, and exacerbated rebellious Qi. If Du Mai is in a state of deﬁciency, you might see that the 
frenulum is ﬂaccid. 
Du Mai is in a state of deﬁciency here. The tongue 
shows a history of Du Mai being overactive 
in remnants of a red perimeter. This person 
overworked and worried, exhausting their Yang. 
The concave rear and the ﬂoppy nature of the 
tongue show the eventual collapse of Yang and the 
consequent weakness of Spleen Qi, which led to 
swelling and Dampness.
This tongue is very red, darts 
out, and dances around.

<!-- p.132 -->
113
Tongue Diagnosis and the Complement Channels    |
Te Yin Wei Mai Tongue 
Yin Wei Mai is the record keeper. It preserves an individual’s history in its structure. Tis is 
how a depressed person develops a stooped posture, or how a fearful person develops a slightly 
concave chest. Yin Wei Mai also recycles Yin, preserving the origin of the structure of the body 
by constantly bringing excess Yin back to its origin, Ren Mai, at CV-22 and 23. 
When Yin Wei Mai is out of balance, the patient is unable to let go of the past. Tey focus on 
the past and miss the opportunity to re-create and refresh themselves. Te habit of reminiscence, 
longing for the past, or dwelling on feelings—positive or negative—about those in the past, 
hurts the Heart. Blood—the residence of the emotions—becomes depleted. As always, when 
the body recognizes that it is deﬁcient in a medium, it will stagnate that medium to prevent 
further loss. And since Yin Wei Mai is producing this stagnating action, its charges, Blood and 
Yin, also stagnate, resulting in Shen disturbance and pain respectively. 
Te Yin Wei Mai tongue is characterized by tension in the Upper Jiao. Te anterior third of 
the tongue will often be elevated and/or red, either at the Pericardium or the Heart areas. 
Sometimes the sides will be visibly tight, pushing Qi forward and creating a tight band in the 
Lung area, indicating mounting frustration aﬀecting the Lungs. You might notice the patient 
revealing envy or resentment, and commonly, an inability to release the past. Sometimes, 
though, the sides are very tight and the tip of the tongue is elevated. In these cases, frustration 
has aﬀected the Heart, resulting in jealousy or even rage.
A Yin Wei Mai type tongue in a 
patient with childhood trauma 
dating from infancy.
This patient presented with 
insomnia, anxiety, restlessness, 
joint pain and back pain.

<!-- p.133 -->
114 |    Tongue Diagnosis
Te Yang Wei Mai Tongue  
Yang Wei Mai creates a balance of Yang between its origin (Du Mai) and the exterior of the body. 
It recycles excess Yang back to the Yang’s origin, Du Mai. In regulating the exchange of Yang 
between interior and exterior, it plays a key role in the conduct of immune responses. 
It’s also a record keeper, but it records the memory of actions. It records repetitive skills, such as 
riding a bike or driving a car, and even social skills. Life’s major events, its stages, turning points, 
disappointments, and major achievements are all recorded as memorable landmarks onto the 
brain by Yang Wei Mai. Diﬃculty assimilating these landmarks into the fabric of one’s life creates 
imbalances in Yang Wei Mai. Te imbalances can create trepidation in making choices about how 
we will interact with the world going forward. Are we in or out? When Yang Wei Mai is out of 
balance, there is indecision, Shao Yang (Damp-Heat) conditions emerge, and there is fear of, or 
obsession about, the future.  
If you ﬁnd symptoms of Yang Wei Mai or if you ﬁnd the Yang Wei Mai pulse, the tongue will often 
be swollen and have a thick coat (white or yellow) as Dampness responds to restrain unregulated 
Yang. As the conditions progress, Yang Qi (Wei Qi in particular) becomes deﬁcient, causing the 
tongue to become quite ﬂaccid, especially around its perimeter. If there is Wei Atrophy Syndrome 
where Yang is trapped in the exterior and has consumed Wei-Defensive Qi, the tongue may be 
crimson. 
This patient shows unregulated Yang Qi, which she experiences in the form of mania,  
palpitations, overall tension, and a lack of coordination.  
This was exacerbated by extreme anxiety. The Dampness that responded to  
contain Yang resulted in Phlegm affecting the Mind, resulting in the arising  
of suicidal tendencies. She presented after a Western diagnosis of bipolar disorder.

<!-- p.134 -->
115
Tongue Diagnosis and the Complement Channels    |
Te Yin Qiao Mai Tongue 
Te principal function of Yin Qiao Mai is cultivation of one’s identity, appreciation for oneself, 
and the ability to reside peacefully in the present moment, with structure in alignment. In the 
absence of love for oneself, the Yin Qiao Mai Channel stagnates and accumulates Yin and 
Dampness. Wind may develop. Te Yin Qiao Mai tongue tends to be short, swollen, and pale 
purple. 
A patient with Bell’s palsy.
Dampness, rebellious Qi  
and sinus infections.
Te Yang Qiao Mai Tongue 
Yang Qiao Mai brings Yang Qi from Du Mai to govern our gait and alignment, keeping us in 
balance. When Yang Qiao Mai is tight or loose, there are musculoskeletal issues related to the 
alignment of the physical structure. From an emotional point of view, when Yang Qiao Mai 
is out of balance, the patient is never happy in the present moment, always wishing they were 
somewhere else, or with someone else, or doing something else. Often they feel the world is 
not on their side, that the world is aggressive toward them. Tey cannot reside happily in the 
moment where they are. If the patient presents with signs and symptoms of Yang Qiao Mai, or 
if you ﬁnd a Yang Qiao Mai pulse, you might ﬁnd that the tongue shows tightness down the 
sides, since the channel itself travels along the lateral exterior. Te tension in the sides of the 
Yang Qiao Mai tongue causes it to be very long and red.
This patient presented after an injury from a fall that occurred after losing 
consciousness (Wind). He experiences pain in the shoulder, Wind in the legs 
(sciatica) and occiput, eye pressure issues, high blood pressure, and diabetes. The 
Wind is visible in the contortion of the tongue body.

116 |    Tongue Diagnosis
The person could do well with treatment of Yang Qiao Mai, but Yang Wei Mai would 
function well due to the Damp-Heat showing. She presented after a diagnosis of high 
cholesterol, fatty liver disease, and hypertension, and experiences headaches, a neuroma, 
palpitations, and obsessive fear. The cholesterol (Dampness) is being manufactured in 
response to the rampant Heat evident throughout the tongue. This Heat is exacerbated by 
dehydration and overeating poorly chosen food.
Te Dai Mai Tongue  
Te function of Dai Mai is to use the pelvis as a receptacle for excesses. Tese excesses may be 
waste or toxins the body can’t dispose of, but Dai Mai can also store Dampness that it will use to 
absorb unprocessed memories or emotions. Since Dai Mai is in the realm of the Jing-Essence, 
and since emotions reside in the Blood, the emotions contained in Dai Mai are truly repressed. 
When Dai Mai functions this way, the body is weighed down and there is depletion of Qi as it 
is diverted to fund the containment. Te resulting compounding stagnation, including ﬂuids and 
masses, can aﬀect fertility, the routes of elimination, and optimism. 
Te Dai Mai tongue is swollen, especially in the posterior portion of the middle, and at the rear. 
Tere may be a line of constraint across the tongue behind the Middle Jiao. Tere may be thorns 
(bumps) and deposits and a thick coat in the rear of the Middle Jiao area and all through the 
Lower Jiao area.

Tongue Diagnosis and the Complement Channels    |
Te Luo Channels and Tongue Diagnosis 
Te Luo Channels are created by the body to contain blood-borne pathogenic factors ranging 
from toxins to unresolved or unexpressed emotions. Tey keep these away from the organs 
using Blood. Luo vessels manifest as spider veins, varicosities, swellings, and later, they elicit 
nodules. Te Luo vessels can hold pathology for hours or decades, depending on toxicity of the 
pathology and also depending on the amount of mediumship—Blood and Fluids—available 
to hold that pathology latent. 
When the Luo Channels are functioning successfully—keeping the pathogen out of circulation 
and away from the organs—the tongue might not show pathology at all. Remember, the tongue 
is an instrument of expression and will not give clues about successful suppression (latency) 
when pathology is hidden. But when the Luos begin to fail—when they can no longer fully 
contain pathology—signs of Blood taxation will appear. Blood may have been consumed in 
the process of maintaining the Luo vessels, causing the sides of the tongue to appear slightly 
translucent, pale, or pale purple. If the body has stagnated Blood to try to conserve Blood, the 
sides of the tongue may be purple or pinched. 
Small protrusions (small thorns) or roughness can indicate that the Luo Channel corresponding 
to that area is beginning to lose latency of a pathogen. If you suspect this is the case, look for 
spider veins or nodules along the relevant Luo pathways and treat the Luo Channels. 
The Stomach Luo  
has lost latency.
The Lung Luo  
has lost latency.
When the pathology overwhelms the body and the Luo vessels can no longer contain it, 
the pathology comes “out of latency,” emptying out into the Primary Channel. Te Primary 
Channel tries to contain the pathology using another medium, Fluids. It creates cysts and 
nodules in the channel to try to arrest the pathogenic factor’s progress to the precious organ. 
When it can sustain the delay no longer, the pathology moves to the Transverse Luo. 
When pathology deepens and moves on to the Transverse Luo, the tongue becomes very 
interesting. Te Transverse Luo is in two sections. Te ﬁrst connects the Luo point to the 
Source point of the Yin-Yang related pair. Te Source point itself has the capacity to hold

<!-- p.137 -->
118 |    Tongue Diagnosis
pathology in the relatively short term; the tongue may still not show pathology other than Blood 
deﬁciency or stagnation. 
Te second section of the Transverse Luos—identical to the internal branches of the Primary 
Channels—are like exit ramps oﬀ the highway of pathology containment; they lead pathology 
to a head-on collision with the Source level and, therefore, the organs. It is the action of the 
Transverse Luos—not the Longitudinal Luos—that is visible. Once the pathology has reached 
this point, the body musters as much Yang Qi as it can, sometimes creating an explosion of Heat 
in a last-ditch attempt to either combust the pathogenic factor, to oust it to the exterior, or at least 
move it away from the organ. Tere will be a dramatic display of Heat in the body of the tongue; 
it will become very red, even crimson. 
As the pathology reaches and aﬀects the organs, the underside of the tongue and the sublingual 
area—the reﬂection of the Yuan-Source level—will show unmistakable signs of toxicity breeching 
the Yuan-Source arena. Te area becomes dark and distended, even purple or blackened. Te 
disease is serious. 
Te Divergent Channels 
If the Luo Channels fail, and there are suﬃcient alternate resources, the Divergent Channels may 
capture the pathogen and move it to the joints—another area of latency. Te Divergent Channels 
can contain a pathogen for hours or decades, depending on the strength of the pathogen. Tey 
attain latency by lassoing the pathogenic factor in a sequence of brilliant moves as mediumship 
becomes depleted. Tey command ﬁrst Jing, then Blood, then Jin-Tin Fluids, the Ye-Tick 
Fluids, then Qi, and ﬁnally Yang to ensure or strive for preservation of the organs. 
When the Divergents are holding a pathogen in latency, there will be no signs and symptoms 
in the body related to that pathology. As resources are consumed over time, latency may become 
lost—the pathogenic factor can come out of hiding—and the tongue will show evidence of the

<!-- p.138 -->
119
Tongue Diagnosis and the Complement Channels    |
body’s response to the depletion of those resources. Signs can include swellings, pallor, and 
wasting. 
Eventually, when the body cannot maintain latency, the pathogenic factor is released and 
evidence of the pathogen itself will show on the tongue as great Heat, or protrusions. Since the 
Divergents move pathology from one side of the body to the other, the tongue may become 
deviated, or the two halves of the tongue may become noticeably diﬀerent. In some cases the 
tongue may point to one side. 
Te rear of the tongue is important, because the Divergents can only hold pathology if Fluids 
or Yin are suﬃcient. Divergent Channels will fail to hold pathology away from the organs if 
Yin is scarce. If the rear of the tongue is particularly cracked, dry, shriveled, or wasting, the 
Divergent Channels have failed or are on the brink of failing. 
Te Sinew Channels 
Te Sinew Channels conduct Wei Qi over the entire surface of the body, creating warmth and 
defense, and allowing movement of the exterior anatomy. Tey also control the Wei Qi that 
resides in the smooth muscle: in the lungs, heart, gut and uterus. Wei Qi is formed by Kidney 
Yang and Stomach Fluids. It is spread evenly by the Liver and brought fully to the exterior by 
the dispersing action of the Lungs. When the body wishes to expel a pathogenic factor, Wei 
Qi acts to initiate sneezing, sweating, coughing, vomiting, urination, and defecation, moving 
the pathogen to the exterior and out of the body. When Wei Qi is engaged in this process, the 
outer rim of the tongue often becomes red. Tis is because the Liver is active in moving the 
Wei Qi, trying to get the pathogen to exit, ﬁrst through the upper Jiao. A more primitive map 
of the tongue is best used to see the evidence of the response of Wei Qi.
