121
Wen Bing (Febrile Disease Teory) and the Tongue   |
13 
Wen Bing (Febrile Disease Teory) and the Tongue 
When viewed through yet another lens, the tongue can show the four stages of the Wen Bing 
model of the Ming Dynasty, the last major tradition in Chinese Medicine. Te basis of Wen 
Bing theory is that Heat is the originating pathogen. One can hardly blame the early Ming 
Dynasty practitioners for their belief that Heat must be pathological. Te presentations of 
pestilent Qi (the Black Death) were terrifying and cruel. Pestilent Qi aside, with the advent of 
Wen Bing theory, a key fact disappeared from the medicine: Heat is a vital, crucial response to 
pathology. Most fevers are our friends, yet even today, fevers are seen as signs of impending grave 
illness. In the clinic, we ﬁnd that it’s important to diﬀerentiate between the Heat of pathology 
and the Heat that is clearly an appropriate response to pathology. Te pulses will save the day 
again. If the deep and moderate pulses are rapid, it’s time to clear Heat. If the pulses are only 
rapid at between one and six beans of pressure, it’s likely Wei Qi is doing its job, acting against 
a pathogenic factor. Nourish Fluids and support it, then watch that it clears when healing is 
complete. Wen Bing theory is best used as emergency medicine for fast-progressing, highly 
infectious illness. Te theory is divided into four stages: Heat in the Wei Level, Heat in the Qi 
Level, Heat in the Ying Level, and Heat in the Blood Level.
1. Heat in Wei Level 
With Heat in the Wei level, the initial stage of response to so-called febrile disease, the patient 
experiences fevers and chills, aversion to Cold, thirst, headache, sore throat, cough, nasal 
congestion, and sinus inﬂammation. Te pulses are ﬂoating and rapid. 
Tongue: Te tongue is red at the perimeter, all along the edges to the tip. As Wei Qi scatters to 
meet the invasion, a slightly yellow coat may form. 
As the Wei level stage develops, complications of Dampness may arise including heaviness of 
the chest, nausea, and loss of appetite. Symptoms can be intermittent. 
Tongue: At this point, the tongue may develop a thin, greasy coat, usually yellow but maybe 
white. Te sides of the tongue become red. 
Meanwhile, the pulse remains rapid and ﬂoating but becomes slippery. 
2. Heat in Qi Level 
At the Heat in Qi level stage, the pathogen has entered the interior. Tis would equate to Yang 
Ming in the channels in the Shang Han Lun tradition. Heat has become exuberant as Wei

<!-- p.141 -->
122 |    Tongue Diagnosis
Qi confronts the pathogen, creating fever. Te patient has thirst, a bitter taste, aversion to Heat, 
dark urine, yellow mucus, hard stool, restlessness, diaphragmatic constraint, disturbed Shen, loud 
cough, rapid breathing, even wheezing or panting. Tey may present with a scorched odor. If 
the Heat is extreme enough, sweat may be absent. Not surprisingly, the pulse will be rapid and 
ﬂooding, since Heat is exuberant and forceful. 
Tongue: Te tongue is deep red with a yellow coat, at least slightly greasy.
As the Qi level stage deepens, it becomes complicated as the body creates Dampness to stem 
Heat damage to tissues. Tis equates with the Shao Yang stage in the Shang Han Lun tradition. 
Te patient experiences nausea, abdominal distention, diﬃcult urination, diarrhea alternating 
with constipation, borborygmus, afternoon fever, chest oppression, lack of thirst, and Dampness 
in the chest. Te pulse becomes wiry, rapid at both the moderate and deep levels. 
Tongue: Te tongue becomes dark red with greasy, yellow coat.
Te Qi level stage can become further complicated as Heat consumes mediumship, leading to 
vacuity in the channels. Tere may be diﬃculty breathing, a cough with yellow phlegm, canker 
sores, and early internal Wind signs. Te pulse becomes unpredictable, perhaps ﬂooding and rapid 
or deep and rapid.
Tongue: Te tongue becomes dark and quivering as Kidney Yin is consumed. 
3. Heat in Ying Level  
Te next stage, Heat in the Ying level, is marked by fevers (high or low but worse at night). As 
the fevers progress, Yin is consumed and internal Wind manifests strongly as neurological signs, 
including seizures and tremors. Tere can also be insomnia, ﬁbrosis, cough, and skin outbreaks 
(even necrosis). As Heat consumes Fluids and Blood and there are no resources to buﬀer the 
pathogen, ﬁre toxins (free radicals) become rampant. Te pulse is rapid and thin.
Tongue: Te tongue is crimson or purple or dark red, peeled and rough-looking. 
As the Heat in the Ying level stage worsens, more Damp responds to the extreme Heat. Te 
Damp-Heat complex damages metabolic mechanisms, leading a variety of wasting scenarios 
including Wasting and Tirsting Syndrome (diabetes). Te pulse is rough, thin, and rapid. 
Tongue: Te tongue body becomes dark due to damage to Fluids. Te coat becomes thick, greasy, 
parched, sometimes brown.

<!-- p.142 -->
123
Wen Bing (Febrile Disease Teory) and the Tongue   |
4. Heat in Blood (Shen) Level     
In the ﬁnal stage of the Wen Bing model, Heat in the Blood or Shen level, hemorrhaging 
begins. Diseases such as Ebola are so rapid, so pestilent, they whip through the previous three 
stages and arrive in the fourth stage soon after the disease is contracted. Tis fourth stage has 
all the signs and symptoms of the third stage, but much more severe. Fevers at night are even 
worse, leading to severe insomnia and disturbed Shen, eruptions on the skin are now dark, 
bruised and purple, and there is reckless Blood (hemorrhaging). Te pulse is thin, weak, rapid, 
rough, all due to extreme depletion of Yin.
Tongue: Te tongue is dark purple, bruised-looking, bright and shiny, since the Qi that was 
managing Blood is seriously depleted. Yin is so depleted, there is no coat and the tongue can 
take on a luminescence as Yang escapes. Te tongue may also be stiﬀ, and protrusions can erupt 
all over the tongue. 
As the Heat in the Blood level stage worsens, and both Yin and Yang are lost, Wind signs 
become extreme and deep depression sets in. If the body is able to create more Damp, depression 
can deepen to manic-depression. Te pulse becomes rough and irregular. At this point, the 
disease is considered terminal. 
Tongue: Ultimately, the tongue becomes pale and ﬂaccid as Yang is consumed.

<!-- p.143 -->
124 |    Tongue Diagnosis
14
Step-by-Step Diagnosis
Make thorough observations before turning attention to diagnosis. 
Observations on the Fly
If you’re running short on time and you want to ensure the eﬀectiveness of a brief treatment, 
focus on the moisture on the tongue. Tis is more important than the color of the coat or body, 
protrusions, the map, size, shape and posture. If the tongue is dry, moistening will be a chief 
strategy. If there is clumping of the coat, as you nourish Fluids, ensure their movement and ensure 
that the bowels are moving. 
As a Chinese Medical practitioner, you are treating the individual, not the disease label. We’re 
making a unique diagnosis for that individual. No two patients diagnosed with cancer, no two 
patients diagnosed with arthritis, no two patients diagnosed with hormone imbalances, no two 
patients with skin rashes are the same; the root cause is a unique combination of the factors 
you are analyzing in the tongue or the pulses. For one single Western diagnosis, the causes, 
when viewed through the lens of Chinese Medicine, could be any combination of countless of 
factors. Tis is what makes Chinese Medical diagnosis special—the absence of labels, seeing 
behind the symptoms, and deducing root causes. All diseases originate in a state of dehydration, 
not necessarily of water, but of wet food and fats. Tongue diagnosis focuses on tracking that 
dehydration, and the resulting Heat and Dampness. Your diagnosis, therefore, should focus on 
ﬂuids ﬁrst. Remember not to be seduced by the tongue map!  
Below are step-by-step protocols arranged from very quick to more in-depth. Naturally, you’ll 
treat according to your own training. 
Step-by-Step Observations (Very Simple)
1.  Does the tongue seems to have good Qi? Does it come out of the mouth and have good 
posture, or is it either ﬂoppy and overextended, or stiﬀ and retracted? 
2. Is the tongue moist? 
Treatment: If retracted or ﬂaccid, tonify Spleen Qi. If not moist, nourish Fluids. 
3. Does the tongue stick out too far?

<!-- p.144 -->
125
Step-by-Step Diagnosis  |
4. Is the tongue body red or purple? 
Treatment: Clear Heat and nourish Fluids. 
Tis very simple approach is not comprehensive but can be a starting point, since Spleen Qi 
and adequate Fluids are the cornerstone of health. 
Step-by-Step Observations (Simple)
1. Note how far the tongue protrudes.
2. Note the posture of the tongue (how it sits).
3. Note the: 
 a. moisture of the tongue coat
 b. color of the tongue coat
4. Note the:
 a. moisture of the tongue body 
 b. color of the tongue body 
5. Note the locations of: 
 a. cracks
 b. coat
 c. color 
 d. spots
 e. accumulations
 f. protrusions
6. Note the color and state of the sublingual veins.

Step-by-Step Observations (Complex)
Note:
1. Distance: how far and how quickly does the tongue move out? Does it:
 a. come out a long way?

 c. stay within the conﬁnes of the mouth?
 d. dart out?
 e. emerge slowly?
2. Posture of the tongue (how it sits). Is it:
 a. turned under?
 b. turned up?
 c. stiﬀ?
 d. ﬂaccid?
 e. pointed sideways? Left or right?
3. Shape. Is it:
 a. narrow in general?
 b. narrowing anywhere across the tongue?
 c. narrow at the rear of the tongue?
 d. pointed at the tip?
 e. squared oﬀ at the tip?
 f. thickened anywhere?
4. Coat. Note the location of any of these qualities. Is the coat:
 a. moist?
 b. dry?
 c. thick?

<!-- p.146 -->
127
Step-by-Step Diagnosis  |
 d. patchy?
 e. powdery?
 f. absent?
 g. color noteworthy? (white, yellow, brown, black, etc)
5. Body. Note the location of any of these qualities. Is the body:
 a. moist?
 b. dry?
 c. withered or atrophied?
 d. swollen? 
 e. color noteworthy? (pink, red, translucent, purple, crimson, etc.) 
 f. shape in general noteworthy?
6. Proﬁle. When turned sideways, is it:
 a. swollen in the Upper Jiao?
 b. compressed in the Upper Jiao?
 c. swollen in the Middle Jiao?
 d. compressed in the Middle Jiao?
7. Note the location and depth of: 
 a. vertical cracks
 b. horizontal cracks
8. Note the location of:
 a. spots
 b. accumulations
 c. petechiae, protrusions, thorns
 d. erosions, divots, missing ﬂesh, pockets
9. Note the state and color of the sublingual veins. Are they:

<!-- p.147 -->
128 |    Tongue Diagnosis
 a. swollen, protruded, sunken, or not visible?
 b. red, purple, blue, pale, black, and so on?
10. Note locations of froth:
 a. sides, front?
 b. sticky, clinging to the palate?
Example of Observations
 
 
1. Unremarkable (Distance)
 2. Very ﬂaccid (Posture)
 3. Narrow at rear (Shape) 
 4.  Tick, greasy, dark yellow coat at rear, no coat on sides of tongue, thin yellow coat elsewhere 
(Coat)
 5. Tin, pale purple, wet body, swollen in the middle (Body)
 6. Compressed Upper Jiao in proﬁle (Proﬁle)
 7. Very small horizontal cracks in the center (Cracks)
 8. Petechiae (red) are present in the area between the Spleen and Liver areas (Spots)
 9. Sublingual veins are slightly swollen (Sublingual veins)
 10. No froth (Froth)
Example of the Related Diagnostic Tought Process
Note: Te following considerations are best made in conjunction with pulse ﬁndings. If you feel 
you are not very skilled in pulse taking, or feel bewildered by them, please know it really is a 
skill you can acquire. Te mythology about the diﬃculty of becoming conﬁdent and accurate in 
pulse reading is overstated and has no place in our profession. (See Te Art of Pulse Diagnosis.) 
Additional note: I’m completing this book during the COVID-19 period and as mentioned in 
the ﬁrst chapter, the tongue is holding up beautifully without pulses being available. 
1. Unremarkable. 
2. Very ﬂaccid. Is there a Yang deﬁciency? Qi Deﬁciency?

<!-- p.148 -->
129
Step-by-Step Diagnosis  |
3.  Narrow at rear. Are these deﬁciencies constitutional? Is there also a Yin deﬁciency? Is there a 
Dai Mai blockage? Are Qi and mediumship in the Middle Jiao being shared with the Lower 
Jiao? Is there profound dehydration aﬀecting the intestines?
4.  Tick greasy yellow coat at rear, no coat on sides of tongue, simply wet elsewhere. Is 
Damp-Heat accumulating in the Lower Jiao? Is it a response to the deﬁciency of Yin (and/or 
Blood)? Is the Yang deﬁciency disabling the movement of Fluids in the Lower Jiao? Is there a 
Liver Yin Deﬁciency resulting in no coat appearing on the sides of the tongue? Is there systemic 
Dampness leaving the tongue wet?
5.  Tin, pale purple, wet body, swollen in the middle. Again, is the swelling and the wetness 
revealing systemic Damp that has accumulated due to a deﬁciency of Yang, since the tongue is 
pale and ﬂaccid? 
6.  Compressed Upper Jiao in proﬁle. Is there a diaphragm blockage? Is there Qi constraint 
in the Upper Jiao? 
7.  Very small horizontal cracks in the center. Is there dehydration? Are there insuﬃcient Fluids 
in the Middle Jiao? 
8.  Petechiae (red) are present between the Spleen and Liver areas. Is there disharmony 
between Spleen and Liver? Is the Liver trying to support the Spleen because the Spleen has 
been weakened? 
9.  Sublingual veins are slightly swollen. Is Dampness beginning to aﬀect the Yuan level? 
10. No froth. Te pathology has not yet resulted in enough depletion to cause internal Wind. 
Treatment Principles 
Integrate your observations of the tongue with your pulses readings. Ten integrate that 
combination with your clinical knowledge and intention. 
Remember, one of the most important principles in Chinese Medicine is that the body does 
not make errors. In its ingenious way, the body keeps rearranging its resources, trying to hold 
on to these resources to ensure that pathology cannot reach the organs. Te organs are the 
deepest level of the interior, precious gems that govern and preserve life. So most of what we 
see is simply a response to pathology; sorting out the diﬀerence is key to a successful treatment. 
Be sure you’re positively treating (nourishing or tonifying) what the tongue is telling you is 
missing. Sometimes, of course, what you see is actual pathology. If the patient is eating a quart 
of ice cream in front of the television every night, and you see a thick cheesy coat and the 
pulses are slow and slippery, probably you are looking at pathological Damp. But if the patient

<!-- p.149 -->
130 |    Tongue Diagnosis
is eating a quart of ice cream every night and the pulses are rapid at the deep level, they might be 
craving it simply to counter Heat at the Yuan level. Te tongue in both cases might have a thick 
coat but there will be a deep red body in the second case. If you begin by clearing Damp in the 
ﬁrst case, the treatment may be successful, but if you begin by clearing Damp in the second case, 
the Heat may become rampant and the condition will worsen. Steer clear of blind protocols. Take 
pulses carefully and really examine and think about what you see. If you are working remotely 
and cannot access pulses, it’s okay as long as you look at all the parameters of the tongue. Allow 
enough time to take yourself through the diagnostic protocols above. Perhaps ask for a photograph 
in advance. 
•  If the cracks are transverse, the treatment principles must include moistening Dryness. 
Nourish Fluids. 
•  If there are swellings and cracks at the same time, moisten dryness and also nourish Yin so that 
the body stops accumulating Dampness (pathological ﬂuid) in an attempt to hydrate itself. 
• If the cracks are vertical, the treatment principles must include clearing Heat. 
•  If the tongue has many transverse (horizontal) cracks and there are also swellings, one must 
promote Yin to moisten Dryness and generate ﬂuids so the body will clear Dampness. Te 
patient must have ample Qi to accomplish this. If the tongue is pale, it can’t be done. Tonify 
Qi if that’s the case.
•  If there is a coat, the practitioner can purge if needed. If there is no coat, purging is not 
possible. Tere must be available Yin to create a purge. 
•  If the pulses are ﬂoating in the Kidney positions, do not try to eliminate Dampness. If you 
do, the Heat will gain dominance since it would be unchecked, and Fluids would be further 
consumed. Te Dampness is there as a perfect response to Heat. Clear the Heat but leave 
the Dampness response mechanism undisturbed. It will correct itself in most cases, once the 
body recognizes that the Heat to which it was responding has been cleared. 
•  If a patient enters a healing crisis—a situation where the body is trying to oﬄoad pathology—
and there are insuﬃcient Fluids to fund a purge (to take them all the way out to the exterior 
for eradication), the patient can enter into a state of serious autointoxication as the toxins are 
released from latency. Te patient must have suﬃcient Fluids to sweat, urinate or defecate 
(or vomit). If these functions are not enabled, the patient’s illness can deepen. Keep the 
routes of elimination freed; the bowels must be moving. 
•  If ﬁre toxins (thorns) are present on the tongue, ensure there is not too much protein in the 
diet. A high protein diet will add enough Heat (Yang) to spread and add to the pathology.

Tongue Diagnosis and Dietary Terapy   |
 
15 
Tongue Diagnosis and Dietary Terapy 
By Andrew Sterman, author of Welcoming Food: Diet as Medicine for the Home Cook and Other Healers 
(Classical Wellness Press, 2020)
Diagnosis is the art of discerning from apparent signs what is happening hidden from view. 
Further, diagnosis is an application of what we know about how things work when functioning 
well, along with a familiarity with how things go awry. In other words, we diagnose through our 
working lens, or paradigm, while always aware that other perspectives are possible. 
For example, a patient arrives (in person or remotely over video) and we investigate the tongue. 
We want to see not only which systems are strong and which are in disharmony, we also want 
to be able to see a history of dietary habits. Ten we want to be able to recommend shifts in 
diet, small or large, either as a single presentation of recommendations or as a beginning of a 
series of supportive dietary changes. 
Te tongue is very responsive to internal changes. If the tongue presentation is consistent, or 
a patient reports, “My tongue always looks like that, it has for years,” then we have to consider 
that the habitual diet and lifestyle is actively holding this individual in place. So often we eat 
to aﬃrm and establish our character in the stance where we are comfortable, and that includes 
holding our pathologies in place through our dietary habits. In simple terms, our diet eﬀectively 
locks us into the results of how that diet interacts with our health status as individuals. 
Tere are nearly inﬁnite details of tongue presentation to be considered with an eye for 
recommending dietary change for health improvement. It is very common that clinicians will 
view a tongue and quickly point to a few speciﬁc features, without assessing overall health 
status as it relates to diet. Tis is the common clinical approach. It can be much more helpful 
(and satisfying for both clinician and patient) to ﬁrst assess overall health before placing 
details into the picture. Tis overview sets the stage for a more complete health and lifestyle 
picture (not simply “what is wrong”), and creates a more meaningful context for the dietary 
recommendations to come. In order to assess this big picture, the clinician will want to know how 
the patient’s current diet is working for them, speciﬁcally regarding the three macronutrients: 
grains (carbohydrates, including sweets and sugar products), proteins (including animal foods, 
dairy, and beans), and oils (lipids, including dairy, nuts, cooking oils, and animal fats). To do this 
we look at the tongue body shape, tongue body color, and coat, respectively. 
Tongue Body Shape
Assessing the size/shape of the tongue body gives us an overview of how an individual’s digestion

<!-- p.151 -->
132 |    Tongue Diagnosis
is handling grains and other carbohydrates. Although the Stomach and Pancreas-Spleen areas of 
the tongue are located in the central region of the tongue body, the overall size and shape of the 
tongue is meaningful in terms of how the individual is assimilating carbs from the diet. Can 
this person handle the carbs they are eating? Is this person assimilating enough from the diet to 
maintain fullness of ﬂesh and ﬂuids? Tings to look for: swelling of the tongue body, thinness of 
the tongue body, scalloping of the sides of the tongue (imprints from the teeth). 
Tongue Body Color
Assessing the color of the tongue body gives us an overview of how an individual is regulating 
Heat, especially heat or cold in the digestion. Redness all over can indicate that Heat has entered 
the blood level. Noticeable paleness shows that Blood is deﬁcient, perhaps because digestive ﬁre 
is insuﬃcient to transform foods and assimilate blood building elements (including dietary iron). 
Cold in digestion (or elsewhere) can also have a pale appearance but more reliably appears as 
slightly dark areas, possibly hinting at blue. 
Tongue Coat
Assessing the tongue’s coat (or lack of coat) gives us an overview of how an individual is handling 
oils or fats in the diet. Tis requires actively applying theoretical understanding of how health 
changes over time. What we’re seeing is in progress. Signs move and change as the body handles 
pathology. Resist the temptation to assign set treatments for symptoms in an absolutist way, 
outside of the profound awareness of change embodied in the fabric of classical Chinese Medicine. 
Looking at the tongue coat is important for assessing the individual’s history of pathology. 
Tongue and Diet Considerations
What follows below are a series of door-openings for ﬁnding meaningful associations between 
tongue presentations and digestion, with a basic guide to appropriate dietary recommendations. 
Often, what a patient should avoid is more important than what he or she should add. Remember, 
we may recommend foods, but people eat meals (and snacks). Our recommendations should 
include meal planning and even cooking tips. At the very least, we should be including advice on 
selecting grains (advising on carbs), vegetables, proteins, oils, and spices. Advice on snacking is 
also important, in order to be practical and address how our patients really live, oﬀering changes 
they can willingly and successfully make. 
Every acupuncture or herbal session should include dietary recommendations. Sometimes they are 
absolutely necessary for survival, other times they simply support the treatment and help ensure 
success of the healing strategy employed. And that’s the point: our dietary recommendations 
should be fully integrated with our other therapies. It is possible to match speciﬁc strategies in 
diet to strategies being used in acupuncture, herbal medicine, or any other modality, rather than

<!-- p.152 -->
133
Tongue Diagnosis and Dietary Terapy   |
performing a sophisticated treatment only to oﬀer dietary advice based on other paradigms 
altogether. 
As with any series of “door-openings,” take these recommendations as starting places to be 
explored in your own practice. And remember, food touches all levels of a person’s life. Apply 
with a sensitive eye for ancestral background, current habits, emotional tenor, cooking skill, 
economic context, and, of course, accurate diagnosis, supported by tongue presentation. 
Finding:  
  Swollen tongue body
Interpretation:   Spleen Qi is not successfully transforming carbs in the diet, causing stagnation. 
Location of swellings can add information on focus of stagnation.
Recommendations to Consider: 
 
 
 
•  Eliminate glutinous grains (wheat, barley, rye, but especially modernized 
wheat)
 
 
•  Increase non-glutinous whole grains to move and clear stagnation (e.g., millet, 
brown rices, buckwheat, oats)
 
 
•  Regular inclusion of spices that aid in digestion of grains (rosemary, oregano, 
fennel seed, cumin, caraway, coriander seed, cardamom, nigella, etc.) as well as 
spices to warm digestion (fresh ginger, turmeric, cinnamon, etc.)
 
 
•  Look honestly at sugar consumption. Deliberate or hidden sugar consumption 
can signiﬁcantly disrupt digestion of otherwise healthy grains. Abstaining 
from all grains may be necessary for a time. 
 
 
•  Assess for SIBO. If present, abstain from all sugar and most grains while 
rebuilding microbiota through gradual introduction of varied ﬁber foods, 
fermented foods, and if desired, pre-and probiotics. 
 
 
•  Assess alcohol use (alcohol is fermented from sugar and functions similarly to 
concentrated sugar in the system). 
Finding: 
 Narrow tongue body
Interpretation:   Spleen and Stomach not able to digest and assimilate substance from diet. 
Inability to absorb and assimilate Yin.

<!-- p.153 -->
134 |    Tongue Diagnosis
Recommendations to Consider:
 
 
•  Assess what factors in diet and lifestyle are draining Yin and/or over-ﬁring Yang. 
 
 
•  Build Yin (slowly) with nuts, seeds, shellﬁsh (clams, mussels, scallops, abalone, 
those which do not turn red when cooked as do lobster, shrimp, crab, which 
should be avoided). 
 
 
• Homemade nut/seed milks are excellent to build Yin. 
 
 
• Grains also build Yin, especially as congee and porridges.
 
 
•  Soups, stews, wet-cooked dishes. Wet-cooked foods are easier to digest and 
assimilate. 
 
 
•  Add digestive spices and kitchen herbs to solve speciﬁc problems of digestion. 
Te narrow tongue is usually indicative of problems assimilating carbs; add spices 
into various dishes to aid carb digestion: fennel seed, cumin, nigella seed, caraway 
seed, cardamom, coriander seed, star anise, rosemary, oregano, basil, dill, shiso, 
mint, and more. Sprouts of all types also strongly aid digestion of carbohydrates. 
Finding: 
  Tinness at the back. Ideally, tongues remain full all the way back.
Interpretation:  Indicates Yin deﬁciency in the lower burner, including gonadal hormones.
Recommendations to Consider: 
 
 
•  Foods with healthy oils: avocado, coconut, oily ﬁsh (salmon, sardines, mackerel, 
herring, black cod, and other cold water ﬁsh).
 
 
•  Healthy kitchen oils: olive oil, butter, nut/seed oils. Avoid overly processed 
commercial oils.
 
 
• Include eggs, nuts, seeds. 
Finding: 
  Tongue Body shows red color
Interpretation:   Heat in the blood is showing in the tongue. Uneven locations of brighter redness 
indicate greater speciﬁcity of where heat is located (usually toward the tip; how 
that appears is signiﬁcant).

<!-- p.154 -->
135
Tongue Diagnosis and Dietary Terapy   |
Recommendations to Consider: 
 
 
• Reduce meat consumption.
 
 
•  Reduce stimulants that raise heat: coﬀee, alcohol, chocolate, hot spices, garlic, 
onion, and nightshades. 
 
 
•  Use more stable oils such as olive oil, butter, and saturated fat, rather than the 
more reactive polyunsaturated fats.
 
 
• More green vegetables will strongly help clear excess heat. 
 
 
•  Add more Yin-building foods (congee, porridges, soups, stews, rice, millet, 
amaranth, barley, clams, mussels, mushrooms, seaweeds). 
 
 
•  Add more bitter vegetables, as the bitter taste strongly cools and drains 
ascending ﬁre (dandelion greens, radicchio, endive, chicory greens, escarole, 
broccoli rabe, etc.)
 
 
• Sprouts strongly help cool excess heat.
 
 
• Root vegetables help bring down heat. 
 
 
•  Mint tea opens the exterior that can help vent heat to relax the Heart, useful 
if redness appears more at the tip. 
Finding: 
  Pale tongue body
Interpretation:  Blood Deﬁciency casts a pale color into the tongue body.
Recommendations to Consider: 
 
 
•  Stomach Fire may be too low; add more warming spices (cinnamon, nutmeg, 
ginger, turmeric, rosemary, oregano, cumin, mustard seed, cardamom, etc. 
Avoid hot spices, although black pepper may be used.)
 
 
•  Cooking with vinegars can raise Stomach Fire (suﬃcient stomach ﬁre is 
necessary for assimilation of dietary iron and the building of rich blood.)
 
 
• Gradually increasing red meats can help build blood. 
 
 
•  Vegetarian Blood-building foods include beets (beetroot), blueberries, 
raspberries, black berries, goji berries, black beans, adzuki beans, red lentils, 
and dark leafy greens.

<!-- p.155 -->
136 |    Tongue Diagnosis
Finding: 
  Dry tongue
Interpretation:   Tin ﬂuids are not available from Stomach, or not being ascended by Pancreas-
Spleen Yang Qi. 
Recommendations to Consider: 
 
 Water is essential, but too often water passes quickly through the body. What we need is 
to slow hydration to increase assimilation of ﬂuids. With this in mind, wet-cooked foods 
can be more beneﬁcial for hydration than water alone. 
 
 
•  Congee, porridges, oatmeal (cooked somewhat wetter than most do), polenta/
grits (without cheese or hot spices). All grains can make excellent porridges for 
breakfast, late night eating, or through convalescence. 
 
 
•  Soups, stews. Te water cooked into stews and soups is released gradually during 
digestion. 
 
 
•  Steamed rice, millet, etc. Tese grains are moistening; steaming is the cooking 
method that best supports stomach and pancreas-spleen, together the distribution 
hub for ﬂuids.
 
 
•  Healthy oils are essential as part of overall hydration: olive oil, butter, nut oils, 
seed oils (all good quality if at all possible), avocado, coconut, eggs, gently cooked 
ﬁsh and meats. Shellﬁsh and cold-water oily ﬁsh are particular good sources of 
dietary oils from the animal food side. 
Finding: 
  Midline crack
Interpretation:   Te midline crack indicates an overheating of Stomach and digestion, either 
from overly spicy foods, or, more commonly, from excess worrying and thinking. 
Recommendations to Consider 
 
 
• Consider reducing meats.
 
 
•  Reduce or eliminate foods that stoke stomach ﬁre, including: garlic, onion, chili 
peppers, coﬀee, chocolate, nightshades, and alcohol. 
 
 
•  Add or increase foods that clear heat: green vegetables, root vegetables, well-
cooked grains, small beans, sprouts of all types, mushrooms, seaweeds, ﬁsh and 
seafood (avoiding seafood that turns red when cooked such as shrimp, crab,

<!-- p.156 -->
137
Tongue Diagnosis and Dietary Terapy   |
lobster). 
 
 
• Add Yin-building foods such as congee, soups, stews. 
Finding: 
  Horizontal Cracks
Interpretation:  Horizontal cracks are usually signs of dehydration. 
Recommendations to Consider: 
 
 
•  Develop a water practice; warm water ﬁrst thing in morning and regularly 
through the day.
 
 
• Include porridges, congee, etc. 
 
 
• Include steamed rice, millet, oats, polenta.
 
 
• Include more soups and stews.
 
 
•  Avoid foods that stoke stomach ﬁre and consume stomach ﬂuids: garlic, onion, 
chili peppers, coﬀee, chocolate, nightshades, and alcohol.
 
 
•  Rely on plain water and wet-cooked foods rather than non-hydrating drinks 
such as tea, coﬀee, alcohol, herbal tea, carbonated water or drinks, etc. 
Finding: 
  Wet or Sticky tongue
Interpretation:  Sugars or oils are not being digested/transformed well enough.
Note: A wet or sticky tongue, or even a thick coat, can appear to increase or appear where 
there was none when a person starts a cleansing diet or healing protocol. As dampness clears 
internally, a new or thickened coat often enough appears on the tongue. Don’t be surprised; 
continue through the healing process. 
Recommendations to Consider: 
 
 Sugar is such a concentrated simple carb that the tongue coat quickly shows a response 
indicating something from within, usually as a wetness, sometimes glistening, and 
sometimes sticky. 
 
 
•  Have sugar-free days, saving the sweet taste for several times a week only, or less. 
 
 
•  Have sweets or desserts only after meals digest at least for a good duration,

<!-- p.157 -->
138 |    Tongue Diagnosis
between 30 minutes and 2 hours. 
 
 
• Add sprouts to your diet to aid digestion of sugar. 
 
 
•  Be honest about snack sugar and hidden sugar, even in “healthy” foods, especially 
store-bought things.
 
 Excess oils can also bring a presentation of shiny or oily tongue. Trough experience and 
hearing a patient’s story it is usually possible to discern the role of sugar and oils, knowing 
where to focus (it may be a combination). 
 
 
•  Avoid fried foods; instead, add a drizzle of olive oil (or other healthy fat) on 
vegetables, grains, or animal foods when serving. Too much heat denatures oils. 
 
 
•  Add spices that aid digestive success with oils and fatty foods in order to gain 
the beneﬁts of healthy oils without their potential drawbacks: fresh dill, cilantro, 
parsley, fermented foods, radishes, seed spices such as mustard seed, cumin, 
coriander, cardamom (especially black cardamom), and more.
Finding: 
  Tick coat, white or yellow
Interpretation:   Response to heat or Yin-deﬁciency is causing the internal body to gather 
pathological Yin. If the tongue coat is yellow, long-standing heat has been 
present along with dampness. 
Recommendations to Consider: 
 
 
•  Before enacting clearing strategies, satisfy the body’s need to gather Yin by 
providing Yin-nourishing foods, as listed above (grains, greens, seaweeds, nuts, 
seeds, mushrooms, eggs, shellﬁsh, porridges, etc.)
 
 
•  Once satisﬁed, clear pathological Yin through avoiding dairy, sugar, and gluten. 
Also avoid irritating foods that elicit a protective response within: onions, garlic, hot 
spices, alcohol, coﬀee, chocolate, nightshades. Avoid personal allergens or irritants. 
 
 
•  Consider separating proteins from carbs, to create clear meals that digest better 
and avoid internal fermentation. 
 
 
•  Gradually and steadily add more ﬁber to rebuild the microbiome and clear food 
stagnation (bitter greens help here, too). 
 
 
• Add more fermented foods to aid digestion and gain control of the microbiome.

<!-- p.158 -->
139
Tongue Diagnosis and Dietary Terapy   |
Finding: 
   White or yellow coat gathering in the front or the back, or along the center 
line, or bumpiness growing on the back.
Interpretation:   Locations of stagnation are being identiﬁed. Bumpiness in the back indicates 
sluggish and insuﬃcient clearing of toxins from the lower belly, perhaps along 
with hormonal deﬁciency. 
Recommendations to Consider: 
 
 
•  Congestion in the upper burner indicates more need of green vegetables such 
as bok choy, collard, kale, and Napa cabbage. 
 
 
•  Cook with fresh ginger and scallions to help clear lungs and lymphatic system 
(bok choy sautéed with slivered ginger is a strong example dish for clearing the 
lymphatic system).  
 
 
•  Aromatic spices help clear stickiness and phlegm from the upper burner: mint, 
shiso, tarragon, sage, dill, parsley, cilantro, cumin, coriander, cardamom, black 
cardamom, etc.)
Finding: 
  Bumpiness or thorns in the back of the tongue
Interpretation:   Problems of toxins in the Lower Jiao. Improve the microbiome, resolve food 
stagnation, clear constipation, and attend to conglomerations in lower burner.
Recommendations to Consider: 
 
 
•  Follow advice listed previously for clearing stagnation and congestion with 
vegetables and aromatic spices.
 
 
•  Strongly reduce sugar, irritants, and dairy, while also increasing ﬁber and root 
vegetables to aid clearing. 
Finding: 
  Lack of coat (naked “raw meat” appearance)
Interpretation:   Spleen Yang Qi isn’t bringing enough Yin upward to the chest; this is reﬂected 
on the tongue by profound lack of normal protective coat.
Recommendations to Consider: 
 
 
 First, hydrate and nourish Yin, then support ascension. Diet will vary based on further 
speciﬁcs of diagnosis.

<!-- p.159 -->
140 |    Tongue Diagnosis
 
 
•  Warming spices help transportation of nourishment: cinnamon, ginger, turmeric, 
cumin, clove, nutmeg, rosemary, oregano, mustard seed, more.
 
 
•  Sprouts and spring greens (eaten any time of year) stimulate ascension, e.g., 
asparagus, pea shoots, cooked bean sprouts. 
 
 
•  Steaming supports Spleen, but more ascension is needed here. Pair steamed 
grains with sautéed asparagus or other vegetables, with warming spices. 
Finding: 
  Abnormal sublingual veins
Interpretation:   Swollen sublingual veins indicate stagnation of blood circulation. Causes can 
include Spleen Yang Qi deﬁciency resulting in insuﬃcient blood transport, Liver 
Qi stagnation and Heat, Heart Heat and Luo Channel issues. 
 
 
   Te sublingual veins also oﬀer another look at the status of Yuan Qi; the sublingual 
veins and the back of the tongue oﬀer two glimpses of the lower burner, providing 
insight into progression of conditions. 
Recommendations to Consider: 
 
 
 To work with conditions of the Lower Jiao, be aware of nourishing Yin before clearing 
conglomerations and accumulations. 
 
 
•  Congee and porridges are important for ease of digestion and the contribution 
of ﬂuids. Be sure to include healthy oils (toasted sesame oil, eggs, etc.)
 
 
•  Whole grains help clear accumulations from the bowels. Cook brown rice, oats, 
wild rice, bulgur wheat, and others, with plenty of olive oil or other healthy oil. 
 
 
•  Nuts and seeds nourish the Lower Jiao while also clearing accumulations 
through their oils and ﬁber. Add pumpkin and sunﬂower seeds to grain dishes, 
and include walnuts (best served after soaking over night then drying in the oven 
set to lowest possible temperature for an hour or two). 
 
 
• Mushrooms drain dampness and strengthen the lower burner. 
 
 
•  Seaweeds resonate at the Lower Jiao level and also help clear accumulations and 
conglomerations. 
 
 
•  Root vegetables are important for clearing the lower intestines and improving 
energy there. 
 
 
•  Shellﬁsh can powerfully nourish the Kidneys and the Lower Jiao. For Kidney

<!-- p.160 -->
141
Tongue Diagnosis and Dietary Terapy   |
Yin, have clams, mussels, oysters, abalone: the shellﬁsh that does not turn red 
when cooked. For Kidney Yang, ﬁrst nourish Kidney Yin, then judiciously add 
the shellﬁsh that do turn red when cooked: shrimp/prawns, crab, and lobster.
 
 
•  Spices are very important to help uplift Spleen Yang Qi that is suﬀering from 
lack of contribution from Kidney Yang Qi and the Lower Jiao (rosemary, 
oregano, tarragon, sage, chive, scallion, fresh ginger, etc.)  Spices used well (and 
not overused) are also very important for freeing the circulation of stagnated 
blood (important for clearing conglomerations) including turmeric, ginger, 
cumin, cardamom, star anise, cinnamon, saﬀron, and vanilla.
From this brief guide, the clinician can extrapolate dietary recommendations for all the varied 
tongue presentations in this book, and in the clinic. Te main point here is that dietary change 
is essential for real and sustained systemic change.

<!-- p.161 -->
142 |    Tongue Diagnosis
16 
Cases 
Case 1 
I’m presenting this tongue as an example of the life-preserving mechanism of accumulation 
of pathological Dampness in a last-ditch attempt to buﬀer a pathogen. Tis woman’s Western 
diagnosis was fourth stage liver cancer metastasized to the lungs, breast, large intestine, brain, and 
bone. Te pulses suggested that the initial pathogen might in fact have been in the Lungs, however, 
and that it had metastasized to the breast and Large Intestine, then Liver, then Gallbladder and 
the rest of the Curious Organs because the Lung pulse was at 14 beats per breath and the others’ 
signiﬁcantly slower. It’s hard to say. Clearly, looking at the left side of her tongue, the Gallbladder 
was very taxed, trying to assist the Liver in its management of Blood and eradication of toxicity. 
Regardless of the chronology of the disease, her body was trying to gather any Fluid and Phlegm it

Dampness as compensation. 
Te second tongue photo was taken after the patient was caught in a draft while standing in a 
high wind talking to a relative she met on the street. Here we see a comparative pallor in the 
upper Jiao, delineated by a line across the diaphragm which has tightened to block the passage 
of Cold to the interior. Te deepening of the tongue body redness shows the raising of a fever to 
break up and release the Cold. Wind is evident in the slight froth. Te cracks have deepened as 
the ﬂuids are further tied by the fever. Te deep, darkened tight bands between the Spleen and 
Liver regions reﬂect the Heat being generated to combat the Wind-Cold invasion; also, the Heat 
is not fully articulated so that it becomes Wei Qi, and therefore interferes with the smooth ﬂow 
of Qi in the Middle Jiao.

General Questions 
Why don’t you talk about how to locate speciﬁc diseases on the tongue, like cancers of certain 
organs?
Chinese medicine is the art of understanding and treating the mechanism underlying illness, 
not the symptoms or even manifestations. Cancers (damage by ﬁre-toxins) arise as a result of 
a complex chronology of deﬁciencies and excesses that result in an internal environment in 
which a disease can arise. For this reason, Chinese medicine never treats diseases, it only treats 
the cause of diseases, it treats the failure of mechanisms and functions that allowed that disease 
to present. Te location of cancer signs on the tongue might be interesting, but it will not help 
with diagnosis. Te diagnosis must be based on how those ﬁre toxins arrived there in the ﬁrst 
place. What happened to allow that illness to arise? And where is the pathology headed? Also, 
ﬁre-toxins show up on the tongue in locations that might be quite removed from the origin of 
the disease. Fire-toxins might arise in the breast but have originated as Heat in the stomach,

<!-- p.179 -->
160 |    Tongue Diagnosis
for example. Treatment focused in the Upper Jiao in a common case like that will completely miss 
the root. Always treat the patient, not the disease. 
How can I tell if Heat is responding to Dampness or Dampness is responding to Heat? 
If you’re ﬁnding it diﬃcult to work out whether Dampness is responding to Heat or Heat is 
responding to Dampness so that you can treat the root, ask about history and cravings. Is there a 
long history of Damp-related issues or Heat-related issues? Issues that arose a long time ago could 
be the root. Ten ask about cravings. Very often, a patient will crave the response to pathology. For 
example, they might look forward all day to arriving home to a cold beer—Dampness is craved 
as a response to Heat pathology. Or maybe the patient wants to put hot sauce on everything—
Heat is craved as a response to Dampness. Remember, if Dampness is responding to Heat, the 
Dampness must be kept in place to protect the viscera and Yin until the Heat is cleared. Don’t 
clear Dampness when it is protecting the body from the Heat that preceded it. 
Can I tell if Heat is responding to Damp or vice versa from Lower Jiao symptoms? 
Generally, if Heat is responding to Damp, there will be urinary symptoms of Damp-Heat as it 
clears, including urinary tract infections. (Treat Bladder Divergent, perhaps, and remember the 
gua sha.) If Dampness is responding to Heat, there will be bowel symptoms of Damp-Heat as it 
clears. (Use the lower He-Sea points, perhaps.) 
What message am I sending if I treat the response to pathology instead of the pathology itself? 
If you clear the response to pathology instead of the cause of disease, you are sedating the immune 
system and deepening the disease; you’re telling the body it’s reacting wrongly. 
My patient’s tongue is very thin, like a crepe, and dry. Where should I focus?
Tis is often a sign of Dryness of the Stomach mucosa. Stomach Yin is insuﬃcient to create 
movement and lubrication. Nourish Stomach Yin and Fluids. Pay attention to the diet; they need 
soups, broths, stews, and wet breakfasts. 
I see a big midline crack, yet the tongue is very pale. I thought Heat causes cracks!
Yes, Heat (or dehydration) causes cracks, but over a long time, Heat consumes Qi, leaving the 
tongue pale. However, a big midline crack is generally present in those who cannot stop thinking. 
Can I generate a purge if the tongue is pale? 
No, if the tongue is pale, there is not enough Qi to mount a healing crisis. Heat has exhausted the 
Qi needed for a purge. Clear Heat and engender Qi to nourish Fluids. 
When my very overweight patient showed me her tongue, I was amazed to see it. It was so tiny 
and withered and gray. How does this make sense? 
Te patient is likely very swollen. Tey may be accumulating Dampness in a desperate attempt to 
accumulate Yin, or perhaps in this case, since the tongue is gray, Jing-Essence. Te channels that

<!-- p.180 -->
161
Questions and Discussion   |
reach the tongue are so deﬁcient in mediumship that they are not able to carry enough Qi to 
move Qi and Fluids to the tongue. Te tongue becomes gray as the Kidneys become weaker.
My patient insists on scraping her tongue just prior to appointments. Can the appearance of 
the tongue coat be so changed that is it not useful in diagnosis? 
Yes, but the rest of the tongue will still yield valuable information. 
My patient’s pulses are very rapid on the deep level, but the tongue is not red or dry at all. In 
fact, it looks very sticky. Why can’t I see this serious Heat on the tongue? 
Te responses to pathology are complicated. Sometimes the body responds to Heat by retaining 
Dampness. If successful in this response, Dampness succeeds in controlling the Heat and keeps 
it suppressed; it keeps it from expressing upward and outward. You might see cracks, crevices, 
or ulcers, but there might be stickiness around them. Tese are clues that the body has felt the 
need to respond to Heat with Dampness. You’re seeing the aftermath of the Heat (the cracks 
and the glue) trying to come to the surface. Te pulses conﬁrm that the Heat is very much 
present and must be released from the viscera or the constitution. Clear Heat. 
My patient’s tongue looks enormously long. Is this pathological? 
It can be. A long tongue is showing you plenty of Yang, perhaps an exuberance of Yang.
My patient’s tongue looks extremely lustrous and shiny and yet it is deep red. 
Heat has consumed Yin. Yin is no longer able to anchor Yang Qi down and so it is escaping. 
Both Yang in general and Wei Qi, which is a subset of Yang Qi, are escaping. When Yang 
escapes, it creates a luminescence. Nourish Yin to anchor Yang. 
My patient’s tongue is incredibly stiﬀ, so stiﬀ he has trouble speaking! 
Heat stiﬀens the tongue and blocks Stomach Yin from reaching the tongue. If there is also 
obstruction of the Heart’s connection to the tongue, the speech will be aﬀected. Begin by 
bleeding the Heart Luo point. 
Why does my patient’s tongue droop like a dog’s? 
Te Spleen is unable to uphold the muscle of the tongue. Ascend Spleen Qi. 
Te pulses are so slippery, but the tongue is very short, not protruding at all. It looks like a ball 
stuck in the patient’s mouth. Why? 
You might be seeing turbid Phlegm. Te function of separation of pure and impure is harmed. 
Turbidity from the Stomach and congealed Phlegm from a weakened Spleen may be sent to the 
Lungs for expectoration, but if there is overwhelming epigastric distention and/or diaphragmatic 
constriction, Qi cannot move. Free the diaphragm, separate pure and impure, tonify Spleen Qi, 
and nourish Fluids.

<!-- p.181 -->
162 |    Tongue Diagnosis
Why might my patient’s tongue be curled under, as though the tip is touching the chin? 
Tis usually manifests in a patient who has experienced long-term disappointment. Moxa KI-6, 
KI-2, and KI-8 (the foundation of Yin Qiao Mai) in that order, to straighten it out and to give 
you a clearer view of the big picture in the pulses. 
Why might my patient’s tongue look like a canoe when showing it? 
Yin is depleted and the ﬂesh is retracted, creating a hollowing of the tongue. 
How does the appearance of the tongue relate to Wen Bing theory?  
Heat in the Wei level is reﬂected in the coat; Heat in the Qi level is seen in the tongue body; Heat 
in the Ying level aﬀects the distribution of the coat; Heat in the Blood level shows as protrusions.
You haven’t spoken about Cold very much. I’m a Shang Han Lun practitioner. How can I see 
Cold in the tongue?  
Tongue diagnosis originated in the Ming Dynasty, the time of the Wen Bing tradition. Terefore, 
tongue diagnosis is focused on Heat as pathology, not Cold. Tongue diagnosis was not a practice 
in the Han Dynasty. Te tongue was merely observed brieﬂy as a sign in itself, as a whole. You 
could invent a new system and try to apply it to earlier periods, but it wouldn’t be classical.
My patient’s tongue is ﬂoppy and covered in bleeding lesions. Where should I focus? 
Te Spleen is not controlling Blood and there is Heat in the Blood. Heat has consumed Qi. Focus 
on strengthening the Spleen. Clear Heat from the Blood by bleeding Luo points. 
What does it mean if the tongue is very stiﬀ and rigid? 
Tis is a sign of internal Wind. It may be trembling, also, showing Kidney Yin is depleted. Do not 
simply release Wind, but locate the mediumship that’s deﬁcient and nourish that ﬁrst. Tis can 
be done in the same session. 
My patient, who is very thin, has a chief complaint of canker sores in the mouth. She doesn’t 
appear strong, but when she sticks her tongue out I’m very surprised at how long, red, pointed, 
dry, and energetic it seems. How can one explain this apparent contradiction? 
If the patient has canker sores you wouldn’t be surprised to see a red, dry tongue. It’s long and 
energetic because Yang is escaping. Tere is insuﬃcient Yin to anchor Yang, and hence, it spills 
forth, giving the tongue a very Yang presentation. 
After the treatment to clear Heat in the Wen Bing style, my patient’s tongue became pale! What 
does it mean? 
Te Wen Bing style of treatment spends Yang and posits the idea that it is never too late to purge. 
(Te Shang Han Lun style reveres Yang.) It may be that you cleared Yang Qi when there was a 
deﬁciency of Yang. When you examine the pulse and complexion after treatments, there should 
be evidence of Heat having cleared. Te clearing should be immediate. If the pulse becomes thin

<!-- p.182 -->
163
Questions and Discussion   |
or the complexion pales, there was a Blood and Qi deﬁciency and you expended Qi that could 
not be aﬀorded. Find which pulse is narrow and nourish Fluids or Blood accordingly.
Can I tell whether Dampness is responding to Heat or vice versa from looking at the tongue? 
Swellings indicate Dampness. If the tongue is dry and swollen, there is more Heat than 
Dampness. Dampness is likely responding. If it’s swollen and moist, there is more Damp than 
Heat. Dampness may be primary there. Te pulses help tremendously with this. Also, ask the 
patient about what they crave. Frequently, the response is craved.
My patient has a history of treatment-resistant thrush. Te tongue is coated in thick stubborn 
Dampness. What should I do? 
Inquire about the diet. You can treat till the cows come home but these Damp conditions will 
not budge until there is no sugar in the diet. Reﬁned carbohydrates should come out, too. 
My patient has a strange vacant look about them. Tere is a thick turbid-looking coat all over 
the tongue.
Examine the possibility of an entitic invasion. If likely, perform a Sun Si Miao entitic treatment: 
needle GV-26 and moxa LU-11 and SP-1. 
My patient has sciatica that is not responding to treatment for pain. Te tongue is so narrow 
that there are large gaps between the sides of the tongue and the sides of the mouth. What 
might I be doing wrong?
Treatment for pain cannot be eﬀective if a patient is so deﬁcient in mediumship that the tongue 
has shrunk dramatically. Drop your treatments for pain and treat to replenish Yin, Blood, and 
Fluids. Give strong dietary advice: the patient needs a wet diet, rich in soups, stews, broths, 
porridges. Send them to sleep early. Build them back and then treat for pain. By the time you 
get to that point, the pain may well have disappeared. 
I feel very slippery pulses, but I can’t see Dampness on the tongue; I can’t even see much 
moisture on the tongue. Why? 
Te Dampness might not be evident on the tongue if it is smothering deep internal Heat.
My patient’s tongue is very wet. What does this mean? 
Tis usually indicates very early stage Dampness. Te chronology of Dampness can extend 
from very wet tongue to a thick, cheesy-looking tongue.
What does it mean if the sublingual veins are severely distended? 
Remember, the sublingual veins tell you that a condition originating in the Middle Jiao is so 
chronic that it has begun to aﬀect the constitution. If the veins are very swollen, there was long-
term stagnation in the Middle Jiao.

<!-- p.183 -->
164 |    Tongue Diagnosis
My patient’s tongue is unremarkable except for its dryness. How do I know where the dryness is 
coming from? 
Te moderate level of the Spleen pulse might be thin or narrow, indicating Stomach Fluids are 
deﬁcient. You might ﬁnd the Spleen pulse is not ascending to the Upper Jiao. Either nourish Stomach 
Fluids or ascend Spleen Qi. Or the Lungs are not dispersing, so Fluids are not moving. Disperse 
Lung Qi. Check the pulses after the needles are inserted, to make sure you’re on the right track. 
Why does my patient’s tongue look like polished glass? 
Yang is escaping. You’ll ﬁnd the Kidney pulses sitting up high. Nourish and anchor Yin. 
Why might my patient’s tongue look dull? 
Te tongue receives Blood from the Heart and Fluids from the Stomach. Take the pulses and ﬁnd 
out where they are blocked. 
What does a tongue that looks like a strawberry tell you? 
Te strawberry tongue, which can described as shaped like a strawberry and deep red with white 
dots on it, is showing you that there is stagnation of Qi with tremendous Heat, perhaps with 
spiking or unrelenting fevers, Heat in the Ying level, disturbed sleep, and possibly ﬁre toxins. Tis 
condition can quickly become critical. 
My patient’s tongue is very short but has a thick turbid coat on it. 
Tese two facts are related. If the Stomach has turbidity, the Fluids it sends up are also turbid 
and inherently stagnating. Te tongue will have less freedom of movement and cannot be fully 
extended. 
Questions About the Coat
Why might the coat not be evenly distributed? 
Te Kidneys might not be supporting Spleen Qi. Perhaps the Lungs are not distributing Wei Qi 
adequately. 
My patient’s pulses are nice and wide without being slippery; I don’t see dehydration, but the 
tongue coat is very unevenly distributed. 
Te Lungs are unable to distribute Fluids to support Wei-Defensive Qi. 
Te tongue has no coat at all. Why? 
Te body is unable to produce Wei-Defensive Qi. 
Te tongue has a patchy coat and is geographic with a depression in the Lung region. What 
might be happening?

<!-- p.184 -->
165
Questions and Discussion   |
Te Lungs are unable to distribute Wei-Defensive Qi. Find the broken link in Wei Qi 
production. Is the Spleen unable to send Fluids upward from the Middle Jiao to the Lungs? 
Are the Kidneys unable to provide Yang Qi to the Spleen for it to ascend to the Middle Jiao? 
Are there suﬃcient Fluids in the ﬁrst place?  
My patient’s tongue coat looks like a layer of white powder. What does it mean? 
You’ll likely ﬁnd the Spleen pulse very weak and thin. Tere are inadequate Fluids, but also 
Spleen Qi is unable to hold the coat to the tongue. 
My patient’s tongue coat looks like sandpaper. 
Stomach Yin is drying. 
Can there be Heat if the tongue coat is thick and white? 
Yes, Heat can be so smothered by Dampness that the Heat is hidden even on the tongue. Te 
pulse will show you the location of the Heat that has become trapped. Look on the moderate 
or deep level for rapid pulses. Clear or drain the Heat, leaving the Damp in place until that is 
achieved. 
What can it mean if the tongue coat is greasy?  
If there is dryness in the Middle Jiao, the Spleen can become weakened, unable to transform 
what Fluids there are; Dampness results. Te body responds to the Dampness with Heat, and 
the resulting Damp-Heat combination is sent to the tongue, appearing as a greasy coat. Or the 
Heat pushes the Dampness to the surface in a Hot-Phlegm presentation. 
My patient’s tongue is covered in a thick, greasy coat. Te sides are red. Why might my 
treatments not be clearing the coat? 
Heat is trapped in the Zang Fu and the Damp is playing an important role of mediation. Te 
body will not release the Damp until the Heat is released. 
What does it mean if there is a coat only on the interior of the tongue cracks? 
It means that Dampness is responding to Heat. Te body is trying to use pathological Yin to 
glue mediumship back together. 
Why is the coat of the tongue peeled in patches in patients with deﬁcient Yin? 
In cases of Yin deﬁciency, the body is unable to consolidate Yin. It’s not able to hold it together, 
so the coat—an expression of substance—is unable to maintain integrity in its coverage. 
My patient eats a quart of ice cream every night and has a very thick tongue coat with a red 
body. His pulses are very rapid at the deep level. Should I clear Dampness? 
No. Te patient is most likely craving the response to his pathology (internal Heat). Clear Heat 
ﬁrst and deal with the Dampness later.

<!-- p.185 -->
166 |    Tongue Diagnosis
My patient’s tongue is narrow but very high. I mean there are large gaps between the tongue and 
the sides of the mouth and yet the tongue is very thick. What causes this? 
Tis occurs when the body is gathering Qi but not distributing it. Tere may be blockages in the 
diaphragm and in Dai Mai, preventing outward movement of Qi. 
My patient’s tongue is very swollen but has lots of clear ﬂuid on it. Given the swelling, shouldn’t 
the tongue have a sticky or turbid coat? 
Te Spleen is likely in an early stage of weakness; the accumulations haven’t had enough time to 
gather. 
Te left side of my patient’s tongue is very swollen and red and has a much thicker coat on it than 
on the right side. Te chief complaint is brain fog and constipation. 
Te left side of the tongue reﬂects the Gallbladder. Due to stagnation, the Damp-Heat has 
become so advanced, it has likely gained access to the Curious Organs via the Gallbladder and 
entered the Brain. Move the bowels ﬁrst. 
Questions About Color
My patient has a history of exuberant Heat but her tongue appears very pale. Why? 
A tongue can become pale as Heat consumes Blood, Fluids, Yin, and Qi, leaving the tongue 
reﬂecting expenditure of resources. 
Does a pale tongue prevent me from performing certain kinds of treatments? 
A pale tongue means there is not enough Qi to mount a purge, or to support a healing crisis. Heat 
may have consumed the Qi. If you were to try to release toxins, you would likely create a state of 
autointoxication where the toxins are released from their safe holding in latency, but are unable to 
move out through the exterior. Clear the Heat and nourish Fluids before trying to release other 
pathology.
My patient has Leaky Gut Syndrome and her tongue is very pale. I thought I would see Heat 
creating this damage. 
A pale tongue can indicate that there is insuﬃcient Stomach Yang to conduct the complete 
transformation of food and Fluids. Incompletely digested food irritates the gut wall.
My patient’s tongue is very pale but very moist. I thought that after Qi was consumed, Fluids 
could not be generated or ascended. 
You are likely seeing that the Spleen is losing its capacity to control Dampness and you are seeing 
evidence of the accumulation of Damp.

<!-- p.186 -->
167
Questions and Discussion   |
My patient can’t digest anything and has explosive diarrhea. Te pulses are very rapid in the 
Stomach, but the tongue is pale, swollen, and has a deep midline crack. So many Heat signs, 
but the tongue is so pale! 
Te Stomach and Spleen must be in balance. Ironically, digestion can be weak if there is too 
much Stomach Fire as the Heat damages Spleen Qi. Te tongue has a deep midline crack 
showing a long history of Heat—or Heat from overthinking. It’s pale because the Heat has 
consumed Qi. It’s swollen because the Fire in the digestive tract has depleted Spleen Qi and 
damaged the mechanism of transformation. 
What does it mean if the tongue is gray? 
Te Kidneys are trying hard to support the interior. Either the pathogen is so severe that Heat 
that has consumed Kidney Qi, or the Lungs and Spleen are so weak that Kidney Qi is valiantly 
trying to support them. 
What does it mean if the tongue is black and shriveled? 
Kidney Essence is severely depleted. Both Yin and Yang are decimated. 
My patient’s tongue is black but moist. What does this mean? 
Heat has damaged the Kidneys causing Cold-Phlegm to accumulate. 
My patient swears he doesn’t eat anything on my list of things that exacerbate Stomach Fire, 
but his tongue has a deep midline crack and is quite red. He admits to reaching for sugar to 
keep going, though. He is the happy father of three teenagers. Where is the Heat coming 
from? 
In modern urban settings, the cultivation of periods of seclusion and reﬂection is not valued. 
Constant mental stimulation takes a toll on the Middle Jiao. Heat accumulates as the Spleen 
struggles to process endless onslaught of stimuli, both meaningful and meaningless, important 
and unimportant. Te Spleen and Stomach are constantly engaged in trying to keep up a 
supply of Qi for the conscious and unconscious maintenance and processing of these thoughts, 
with no prospect of respite. Tis creates Stomach Fire and Spleen depletion. Te quest for more 
fuel for the incessantly-thinking brain creates sugar cravings. Te sugar further depletes the 
Spleen-Pancreas and creates more Stomach Fire. 
After a treatment to resolve Dampness, my patient’s face and tongue became pale but the 
pulses stayed rapid. What did I do? 
You might have forgotten to nourish Fluids as you cleared Dampness. And you probably also 
cleared Qi. If the face became pale and the pulse remained rapid, it’s telling you that there is a 
deﬁcit of Fluids—they should have been nourished prior to the clearing phase of the treatment. 
Don’t worry; start with nourishing Fluids when you next see the patient.

<!-- p.187 -->
168 |    Tongue Diagnosis
My patient’s tongue is very red all along the sides and particularly red at the tip but there is a 
thick white coat in the middle of the tongue. Why is the coat not yellow? 
Heat aﬀected the Liver which passed it up to the Heart. Te Small Intestine syphoned some of 
the Heat oﬀ and brought the rest to the digestive tract, which then responded with Dampness. 
Te Dampness suppressed or contained the Heat. Because the Dampness has not yet developed 
into stagnated Damp-Heat, it is still white in color. 
My patient’s tongue is dark red with no coat, but it’s very swollen along the sides. Why hasn’t the 
Heat consumed Fluids? 
It probably has consumed Fluids; what you’re seeing is compensation as the body tries to 
accumulate and hold on to Fluids pathologically. In this case, the body is likely holding onto 
Blood to conserve it. It could be holding Yin also, due to Dryness. You would be able to determine 
which Fluid is depleted by examining the width of the pulses in the Stomach, Liver, and Kidneys, 
respectively. 
What might it mean if the tongue looks fairly regular but is purple? 
Te patient might be struggling to say what they really want to say. Treat the Heart Luo Channel 
by bleeding HT-5. 
My patient’s tongue is very red at the diaphragm area. Why is the redness isolated in this area?
 Tere is diaphragmatic constraint. Vent the Heat through the Lung and Liver Channels, perhaps 
by reducing LU-6 and LR-6.
My patient’s tongue is cracked and crimson all over but there is an unmistakably pale area in the 
center. Why might that be? 
Fire has consumed Spleen Qi. 
Te tongue shows loss of Qi (it’s pale) and is very dry and swollen. Where should I focus?  
Begin with Fluids. Nourish Fluids so that you can move Qi. 
My patient’s tongue has a startlingly deep red body with a bright white coat. How can I make 
sense of this? 
Tis presentation is often seen with Shen issues. Heat trapped particularly in the Upper Jiao becomes 
increasingly suppressed and moves into the interior, causing the Shen to become unsettled. Te 
body responds with Dampness to control it. Antidepressants can make the coat white. Eventually, 
mania ensues as Heat moves more deeply, causing loss of memory and recognition problems.
My patient’s tongue is simply dark. It’s hard to label its color. It’s muted. 
When the tongue is dark all over, there may be fear in play. Treat the Kidneys and bleed GB-37.

<!-- p.188 -->
169
Questions and Discussion   |
What does it mean if the sublingual veins are very dark, almost black? 
Tis can indicate deep trauma, which has aﬀected the Kidneys. Te Kidney Channel connects 
to the root of the tongue. 
Questions About the Upper Jiao 
My patient’s very pointed tongue has red spots in the front third of the tongue. Te area 
is precisely demarcated as though an invisible line were drawn across the tongue. What is 
happening? 
It’s likely you’re seeing signs of a constrained diaphragm. Zong-Chest Qi is therefore 
constrained, pushing the tongue to form a sharp point. Te body responds with Heat to move 
Qi to free the constraint, but the Heat has become trapped in the chest, showing as red papillae. 
My patient’s tongue tip is turned right under. What can this mean? 
Tis can be a sign of deep disappointment. Te Heart is not experiencing joy and is showing 
despair or dejection. 
My patient’s tongue is not particularly long, but the tip itself looks very stretched and pointy. 
Why?
Te patient may be having a diﬃcult time communicating. Te Pericardium causes constriction 
in the blood vessels to protect the Heart. 
My patient’s tongue tip is stretched and constricted and there are tiny cracks behind the region of 
the Pericardium that appear to be caused by this constriction. What are the mechanisms in play?
 Te patient may have constraint in the Lungs as they try to convey the patient’s conscience to 
the Heart for expression. 
What does it mean when there is an elevation at the tip of the tongue covered in protrusions, 
with a tight band around the tip? 
Tis can be an indication of latent Heat in the throat that might manifest as goiter, thyroid 
issues, constriction in the throat, or Plum Pit Qi. 
I see a tight band right across the tip of the tongue. What does this mean? 
Tis means there is Heat trapped somewhere above the Heart, perhaps the throat or the sensory 
oriﬁces. 
When my fertility patient shows her tongue, I don’t see any indication of Blood or Fluid 
pathology. But there’s a white patch on the Lung region. What am I missing? 
Te Jia Yi Jing reminds us that the Lungs command Qi to move Blood. Te Lungs descend

<!-- p.189 -->
170 |    Tongue Diagnosis
Qi and Blood and disperse Wei-Defensive Qi. Tey instruct the Heart and Pericardium to move 
Blood. Hence Lung Qi is responsible not only for initiating bowel movements, but for beginning 
the period and for enabling conception as it descends the egg from the ovary to the fallopian tube. 
If the Lung region is white, the Lungs are depleted. Lung Qi has been consumed. Te Lungs are 
the principle organ of fertility. 
Te tip of my patient’s tongue is very elevated. What severe conditions might be present in such 
a tongue? 
Tere may be rage, resentment, and seizures. 
Questions About the Middle Jiao
My patient’s tongue is huge, pale, and blocklike in the Middle and Upper Jiaos. Te Lung pulse 
is weak. What is this scenario? 
Te Upper Jiao is responsible for moving Fluids. But here the Middle Jiao is not transforming 
the Fluids (and food) in the ﬁrst place. Drain the Fluids (through the Lower Jiao), don’t expel 
(through Upper Jiao). Te Lungs are too weak for expulsion. If the tongue were not so pale, you 
might be able to strengthen the Lungs and expel concurrently. 
My patient’s tongue looks swollen from side to side right across the middle. What does this 
indicate?
 When the middle third of the tongue is swollen all the way from one edge of the tongue to the other, it 
often means that grains are being poorly digested—usually wheat. Te tongue shows accumulation 
of Dampness (the swelling) because the person is not keeping up with the task of processing, 
separating, moving, and eliminating what is incoming. Poor food combinations eventually cause 
problems. When digestion is slow, food ferments in the gut, causing autointoxication. Processed 
foods and reﬁned sugars greatly aggravate this problem. Fermentation that originates in the gut 
eventually damages digestion, leading to accumulations and reactions even to foods that are easy 
to digest. Te gut must be strengthened; start by tonifying the Spleen. 
My patient’s tongue is very red and has a swollen area right in the center. What could it mean?
It could mean that internal Heat is being smothered by Stomach Yin. 
Te sides of my patient’s tongue are crimson and dry. What can this mean? 
Tis can mean that Blood is being sacriﬁced to support Fluids. Te Liver tries to stagnate the 
Blood. Heat responds to the stagnation. Te pulses will clarify this for you. 
What does it mean if the tongue is tiny and dry? 
Jing-Essence is exhausted.

<!-- p.190 -->
171
Questions and Discussion   |
What could it mean if only the center portion of the sides of the tongue are scalloped and also 
covered in thorns?  
Te Liver or Gallbladder may be harboring ﬁre toxins. Te body is holding them in the Liver 
to prevent them reaching the Heart. As the Liver tries to contain them it stagnates its own Yin, 
causing the sides to swell and become scalloped. 
What does it mean when the tongue is elevated all the way down the middle, like the opposite 
of a midline crack? 
Te sides are squeezing the tongue inward toward the center midline. Te Liver and Gallbladder 
are constrained, creating weakness in the Spleen and Stomach. 
I keep nourishing Fluids but my patient’s tongue is stubbornly cracked right through the

Questions About the Sides
How can I tell if my patient is eating too much protein? 
Te Liver and Gallbladder tongue regions will show swelling, stagnation, and Heat. Meat 
requires Stomach Heat (stomach acids) to digest. Since we can’t directly use protein from food, 
we must digest it by breaking it up into amino acids. To protect the villi of the upper intestines 
as the steak and acid (acidic chyme) descend into the Small Intestine, bile (manufactured in 
the Liver) is excreted to neutralize the acid. Digestion of carbohydrates can then follow. Te 
higher the protein consumption, the higher the volume of stomach acid, and therefore bile, 
required. Te spent bile leaves behind bile salts, which are sent back to the Liver for processing 
and recycling. Te amino acids proceed to the Small Intestine where they are separated. Tey’re 
then transported to the Liver, where human proteins are synthesized from them. So the Liver 
is called upon not only to make suﬃcient bile, but to recycle the spent bile and to synthesize 
usable proteins at the end. When the Liver and Gallbladder are required to process too much 
protein, they become inﬂamed. Hence excess protein creates excess Heat. Te sides of the 
tongue will be red.

be yellowed, too (jaundice).

greasy foods, pizza, and sugary foods such as cake and ice cream. Tese foods create constraint in 
the Gallbladder as it struggles to keep up with the demand for bile. Tey might also be worrying 
a lot, weakening the Spleen and causing a response in the Liver and Gallbladder. Tere may be 
a bitter taste in the mouth. Te deep purple discoloration is often on the left, conﬁrming the 
Gallbladder is constrained. Sour foods will neutralize the Heat: lemon, lime, artichokes, pickles. 
Sour berries also stimulate and move the Gallbladder.

Scalloping is only a sign of Spleen Qi deﬁciency if the center is swollen and that swelling extends 
to the edges. If the swelling is isolated on the edges, or mainly on the edges, or if it’s clear that 
the swelling is not emanating from the center, the Liver is stagnating as it attempts to store 
more Blood. Spleen scalloping is a sign of deﬁciency; the Spleen is unable to control Damp. 
Liver scalloping is a sign of compensation; the Liver is unable to store suﬃcient Blood or there 
is insuﬃcient Blood to store, or there is Liver Qi constraint preventing deep penetration of a
