What does it mean and why is it so common? 
Tis is a sign of disharmony between Spleen and Liver. It’s generally caused by poor dietary habits, 
which are endemic in our culture. Sometimes the spots are pale, sometimes red. Sometimes there 
are no dots in these regions, but elevated bands. Te body is trying to work out how to deal with 
Dampness or Heat. Dietary change is essential in these cases. 
My patient’s tongue is covered in patches of purplish spots. What might this mean? 
Tere may be emotional trauma resulting in or caused by long-term Blood stagnation.

<!-- p.202 -->
183
Questions and Discussion   |
What does it mean when there are black spots at the posterior underside of the tongue? 
Pathology is likely at the Yuan level. 
I see many ﬁne white dots at the front of my patient’s tongue. Tey are conﬁned to a very small 
area near the tip. What does this mean? 
Tis is a sign of ﬁre toxins in the Upper Jiao. Check the pulses of the Upper Jiao—the left 
and right cun positions—and look for rapid pulses at the deep level. If there is insuﬃcient 
mediumship to move toxins, put them into latency using the Divergent Channels. 
Why is my patient’s entire tongue rough, like ground beef, covered in protrusions and cracks?
Extreme and unresolved Heat is drying the tongue body. Clear Heat. 
My patient’s tongue is entirely covered in thorns. Te tongue is pale and shiny. Te edges are 
as dry as cardboard. What does this mean? 
Tis type of tongue has a diﬃcult prognosis. Heat has consumed Qi, Blood, and Yin, leaving 
the tongue shiny, pale, and dry respectively. Te body has lost the resources it would need to 
hold toxins latent or move them out if it chose to do so. Te toxins are rampant systemically. 
If you’re interested in the Complement Channels, check the Triple Heater pulse and consider 
starting with Triple Heater Divergent Channel, deep-superﬁcial-deep insertion.
If I see a cluster of thorns in a certain area, does it mean that the ﬁre toxins are located there? 
Yes, but likely not only there. Remember that the body will move ﬁre toxins until it can ﬁnd 
an exit or latency (hiding) for them. Te tongue might only be showing you the location of the 
exit or place of deposit being explored by the body. Te pulse will give you the original location. 
Look for the location of deep and rapid pulses. Clear Heat from these areas also. 
My patient has a cluster of red petechiae at the tip of the tongue and nowhere else. 
Tis will occurs when Qi has gathered in the chest and is unable to move. 
My patient was given intravenous barbiturates for persistent migraines and then presented 
with ﬁne white thorns all over the tongue. 
Intravenous drugs become systemic immediately. Te thorns can emerge overnight, since the 
body is unable to contain the toxicity. 
Te back of my patient’s tongue is covered in thorns. Tey are large and dark, like cut-oﬀ 
cones. Is his “no carb” diet a factor in the tongue presentation? 
Probably. Tis kind of presentation is contraindicated to a high protein diet. Torns at the back 
of the tongue indicate that latency of ﬁre toxins has been lost. Te ﬁre toxins have become 
systemic and burned the lining of the intestines. Excess Yang from the overconsumption of 
protein will spread the disease (toxins).

<!-- p.203 -->
184 |    Tongue Diagnosis
Questions About Froth and Wind 
Te tongue is undulating, moving like a rolling sea. 
Tis is a sign of systemic Wind. Build the medium that is deﬁcient before treating the Wind.
I see a tongue with normal appearance so why it is noticeably shaking? 
Tere may be a strong external pathogenic factor that is lingering after antibiotics were taken. 
Te antibiotics  suppressed the body’s response to the pathogenic factor, sending its escort, Wind, 
deeper into the body.
My patient’s tongue is deep red all over and shaking very ﬁnely. 
Heat originating anywhere can cause shaking. Exuberant Heat results in Wind due to consumption 
of ﬂuids. 
My patient’s tongue is pale and shaking. He cannot hold it still and yet he is exhausted. Te sides 
of the tongue are dry, slightly purple, and pinched tightly. Te Kidney pulses are both found up 
in the Wei level. 
Te Kidney Primary Channel connects to the tongue and so the behavior of the tongue can reﬂect 
Kidney pathology. If Kidney Yin is insuﬃcient to ground Kidney Yang and bring it to a state of 
rest, Kidney Yang will escape, causing instability. Te Yin deﬁciency leads to congestion of Liver 
Qi as it tries to stagnate Yin to conserve it. Tis causes the sides of the tongue to crimp. As Yin is 
not able to move eﬃciently, the body’s eﬀorts to move it create more instability, resulting in Wind. 
Te tongue then shakes. 
Why might the froth on my patient’s tongue be concentrated at the back? 
Tere may be a crisis resulting in a Kidney Yin deﬁciency. Lung Yin and Liver Blood are trying 
to support the Kidneys and these commodities are moving to the Lower Jiao with urgency. 
Why is there froth concentrated near the tip of the tongue? 
Lung Yin has been insulted by the Liver. Regulate the Liver. 
Why are the sides of my patient’s tongue very tight, red, and stretched out, with frothy lines on 
it?
Te Liver may be struggling to move Qi. It’s constrained and the stagnation has caused Heat to 
accumulate. As the Liver tries to create movement, it stirs Yin, producing froth. Clear Heat and 
relax the Liver. Consider bleeding some Luo points to release the Heat. 
Why is my epileptic patient’s tongue covered in froth, mainly along the edges but also in lines 
halfway between the edge and the midline? 
Usually this pattern has an emotional etiology. Tere’s a persistent emotion that the patient cannot 
resolve or shift. Te Heat created as a result of that emotion stirs and depletes Yin, creating Wind,

<!-- p.204 -->
185
Questions and Discussion   |
which rises to the sensory oriﬁces, causing tics, tremors or seizures, and sometimes epilepsy, 
as in the Wind-Phlegm presentation of your patient. Te patient is somatizing their protest, 
demanding resolution and change but not knowing how to achieve it, the body engaging in 
violent outbursts. Te Mind must be treated. In cases like this, I often bleed the points and 
varicosities I ﬁnd along the Yang Qiao Mai trajectory and then complete a course in needling 
Yang Qiao Mai.
I was clearing Damp and suddenly Wind emerged even though there was no froth on the tongue 
at all. 
Te Dampness that was controlling the Wind was suddenly cleared, leaving Heat unchecked. 
Te Heat stirred up Wind that had been held in latency. Be careful not to clear Dampness 
when it is generated as a buﬀer for the true pathology.

<!-- p.205 -->
186 |    Tongue Diagnosis
appendix
I 
Te Invitation to Show the Tongue
Te way the practitioner asks a patient to show their tongue greatly aﬀects the way the patient 
presents the tongue. If unprepared, new patients can ﬁnd the request plainly bizarre. Children can 
ﬁnd it hilarious. Others may ﬁnd it embarrassing for a number of reasons. Perhaps they just ate 
something and they know there’s food stuck on it, or perhaps they think it’s rude. Or perhaps they 
think their tongue is ugly. Some apologize for not having scraped it that day.  Some may ﬁnd it 
an imposition, or feel exposed. 
Te way around all this can be to give a mini-lesson, since patients mostly want to understand 
what you’re doing. After scrawling a very quick map of the tongue, I explain that although the 
pulses are the main source of information for diagnosis, the tongue can be quite useful in seeing 
where the inﬂammation or dehydration has been referred. Ten, leaning back a little, and without 
expectation, I say, “If it’s okay, I’d like to take a quick look at your tongue in case it’s oﬀering some 
more information.” Tat keeps the patient in charge (they can refuse) and keeps the patient from 
feeling pressured (I’m not saying I have to see it). 
Sometimes the patient will ask how to show it: “Should I just stick it out?” to which I lightly 
answer, “When the mouth is opened they tend to show themselves, no worries.” Tis enables the 
patient to release the task to the tongue itself. 
Most often the patient will open their mouth and allow the tongue to emerge. Some will try to 
be extra helpful and stick it out as far as they can. In some, the tongue will dart out quickly and 
appear quite rigid, in others it will ﬂop, doglike. Some patients will open their mouths but not 
allow the tongue to move beyond the teeth. In some of these cases, Qi is retracting the tongue, 
in others, the tongue is simply short. All these possibilities are in themselves clues to diagnosis. 
Occasionally a patient will stick out their tongue but clamp around it very ﬁrmly as if to say, “I 
don’t like this, and I feel intruded upon.” In these cases, as soon as you can, indicate you’ve seen 
enough and thank them—honor their tacit request for privacy. 
(See Tongue Posture, page 30)

<!-- p.206 -->
187
Te Examination of the Tongue  |
Te Examination of the Tongue
If you’ve been taking pulses and are next to the patient, be sure to move away a little before 
asking to see the tongue. Tongue diagnosis must happen fairly quickly because the act of holding 
the tongue in an unnatural position causes changes in color, posture, moisture. Nonetheless, it 
does take a little time at least. While the tongue is out, run commentary to buy time while you 
look quickly and eﬃciently. For example, “Perfect, thank you. Okay, I see some redness, a little 
swelling in the middle, a little thickening of the coat at the back. Tank you.”  Te patient will 
focus on what you’re saying instead of their tongue sticking out. Some patients might allow you 
to take a photograph of their tongue if you reassure them that you will only photograph the 
tongue. It’s important to ask permission, of course. Be careful not to photograph the entire face 
so that when they see the photo they’re not distracted or worried that such a photo exists. If 
they appear a little reluctant to allow it, they might be more amenable to the photo being taken 
on their phone. And if you get the feeling they really don’t want any of it, it’s not a good idea; 
the tension will adversely aﬀect the treatment to follow. Te advantage of having a photo is that 
you can leisurely take them on a diagnostic tour of their tongue and they can see what you’re 
speaking about. Tis can be empowering to the patient, and can also raise their conﬁdence in 
the wonderful treatment they are about to receive.

<!-- p.208 -->
189
 Te Passage of a Pathogenic Factor  |
Te complete acupuncture system comprises the Primary Channels—the channels responsible 
for the moment-to-moment functioning of the organs—and the Complement Channels (the 
Sinew, Luo, Divergent and Eight Extraordinary Channels). Te function of the Complement 
Channels is to steer pathogenic factors away from the viscera, ensuring uninterrupted function 
of the Primary Channels. Te diagram on the facing page shows the brilliant design of the 
entire network, the connections and interactions between the channels as they act to preserve 
life.
 
1.  If the Sinews fail, the pathogen moves either to the Divergent or to the Luo Channels. 
When those avenues are exhausted, the Eight Extra Channels can absorb the 
pathology. When a pathogen is being held latent in the Luos, Divergents or Eight 
Extras, there are no signs or symptoms.
 
2.  If the Tai Yang Sinew—the very ﬁrst line of defense for an external pathogenic 
factor—fails and the Luo Channels do not absorb the pathogen, the pathogen 
can enter the He-Sea point of the Bladder Primary Channel. Te body diverts the 
pathogen from the Bladder organ (to which the He-Sea has access) to the joints. 
Te pathogen has entered the Divergent Channel sequence at that point.
 
3.  If the Luo Channels are taxed due to Ying (Blood or Fluid) deﬁciency, the 
pathogen can go to the Divergent Channels.
 
4.  If the Sinews fail due to insuﬃcient Yang, the pathogen can go to the joints. As 
Jing  
declines, latency is lost (symptoms emerge as the pathogen becomes unhidden in 
the joints) and arthritis ensues.
 
5.  If Ying is taxed by heat, the Jing may step in and use its cold to hold the pathogen. 
When latency is lost from the Jing, heat along with the pathology is released, 
causing the Triple Heater pulse to ﬂoat as it tries to clear the Jing of pathology. Te 
pathogen then has access to the organs. Terefore the body will try at all costs to 
ﬁnd an alternative reservoir of latency.
 
6.  Te Luo Channels can empty into the Yuan-Source level if they overﬂow with 
pathology and fail to ﬁnd a neighboring Luo to occupy.
 
7.  Te Luos can empty into their related Primary Channel or a neighboring Luo.
 
 8.  Pathology in the Luos can ﬁnd its way to the Eight Extra Channels. Te last Yang 
Luo (the Gallbladder Luo) can empty its pathology to ST-42 which connects to 
Chong Mai, and the last Yin Luo (the Liver Luo) can empty into the Ren Mai at 
the end of its trajectory, CV-2, giving entry to the Constitution.

<!-- p.209 -->
190 |    Tongue Diagnosis
 
9.  Te Constitution can pass the pathology to its main reservoir, Dai Mai. When Dai 
Mai ﬁlls, it should drain, but if it cannot, it uses Kidney Divergent Channel to extend 
its ﬁeld of latency.
 
10.  Once in a Divergent Channel, the pathogen can then move through the Divergent  
Channel sequence until it reaches the last conﬂuence, the Large Intestine and Lung 
Divergent Channels.
 
 11.  At the end of the Divergent Channel sequence, the pathogen can enter the Primary  
Channels at ST-12, but the body may try to push the pathology to the Da Bao at 
GB-22 which is another point on the Lung Divergent Channel trajectory. GB-22 is 
also the Great Luo of the Spleen and the pathogen can ﬁnd latency in the Luo arena 
there, also.
 
12.  When the Great Luo of the Spleen ﬁlls, the pathology can spill down via either the GB 
Primary Channel or Bao Mai to Dai Mai, a major holding site.
 
13.  At the end of the Divergent Channel sequence, the pathogen can go to Du Mai at 
GB-8. 
 
14.  When the capacity to hold the pathogen in latency is exhausted, the pathogen moves 
to the organs and the disease becomes terminal.
With knowledge of all the channels of acupuncture, a full set of classical techniques, and a 
clear diagnosis, the opportunities for healing are limitless, and the results of treatment can be 
astonishing. While there is life and while there is breath, all possibilities are open.

Further Reading
Dr. Jeﬀrey Yuen, oral transmission. A primary source of oral transmission of Classical Chinese 
Medicine at an international level is Jeﬀrey Yuen, the 88th generation of his Taoist lineage: Yu 
Ching Huang Lao Pai, (Jade Purity School, Yellow Emperor/Lao Tzu sect); 26th generation of 
Chuan Chen Lung Men Pai (Complete Reality School, Dragon Gate Sect).
Wu Jing-Nuan, translator, Ling Shu or Te Spiritual Pivot (Honolulu: University of Hawaii Press, 
1993).
Zhongjing Zhang, Feng Ye, Nigel Wiseman, Craig Mitchell, and Ye Feng, Shang Han Lun, On 
Cold Damage (Brookline: Paradigm, 1999).
Bing Wang with Nelson Liansheng Wu, Andrew Qi Wu, translators, Yellow Emperor’s Canon 
Internal Medicine (Beijing: China Science and Technology Press, 1999).
Maoshing Ni, Te Yellow Emperor’s Classic of Medicine (Boston: Shambhala, 1995).
Paul Unschuld, general editor and translator, Hermann Tessenow, translator, Huang Di Nei Jing 
Su Wen: An Annotated Translation of Huang Di’s Inner Classic (Berkeley: University of California 
Press, 2011).
Huang-Fu Mi, Te Systematic Classic of Acupuncture and Moxibustion (Boulder: Blue Poppy Press, 
1993).
Paul Unschuld, translator, Nan Ching, Classic of Diﬃcult Issues (Berkeley: University of California 
Press, 1986).
Andrew Sterman, Welcoming Food, Diet as Medicine for the Home Cook and Other Healers, 2 volumes 
(New York: Classical Wellness Press, 2020).
Ioannis Solos, Gold Mirrors and Tongue Reﬂections: Te Cornerstone Classics of Chinese Medicine 
Tongue Diagnosis - Te Ao Shi Shang Han Jin Jing Lu, and the Shang Han She Jian (Vancouver, 
Canada: Singing Dragon Press, 2012).  Tis book translates the ﬁrst textbook of tongue diagnosis, 
Scholar Ao’s Gold Mirror Records, ﬁrst published in 1341; and a second book, Tongue Reﬂections in 
Cold Damage, ﬁrst published in 1668, featuring the tongue color analysis. 
Nancy Holroyde-Downing, Tongues on Fire: On the Origins and Transmission of a System of Tongue 
Diagnosis, PhD dissertation, University College London, 2017.

<!-- p.212 -->
193
About the Author   |
About the Author 
Ann Cecil-Sterman, MS, LAc, is the author of the widely acclaimed books, Te Art of Pulse 
Diagnosis, Illustrations of the Complete Acupuncture System, and Advanced Acupuncture: A Clinic 
Manual, a required text in many acupuncture schools in the United States. She travels all 
over the world, to Spain, England, Mexico, Italy, Australia,  Canada, Poland, and all over the 
United States, to teach the application and methodology of the Complement Channels, the 
art of pulse diagnosis, and with her husband, Andrew Sterman, the use of food as medicine. 
For many years she taught Advanced Clinical Observation and was a senior clinic supervisor 
at the school of acupuncture founded by Dr. Jeﬀrey Yuen in 1997 in New York City. She is a 
long-time student of Dr. Yuen, having extensively studied acupuncture, diet, Chinese medical 
history, herbs, Qigong, essential oils, stones, and philosophy with him across North America. 
Ann was director of the Classical Wellness Center in Manhattan where for many years she 
practiced and taught classes on advanced diagnosis and the theory and application of Classical 
Chinese Medicine. Currently, her patients—children and adults of all ages—come from all 
over the world, working through illnesses, or on personal cultivation. Ann’s practice features the 
Complement Channels of acupuncture—the Sinew, Luo, Divergent, and Eight Extraordinary 
Channels—and is augmented with Classical Chinese dietary therapeutic guidance. Ann 
also paints large-scale abstract works depicting physiological function, especially pulses. As 
a musician, one of her three CDs was nominated for an ARIA (Australian Grammy). She 
occasionally tours with the Philip Glass Ensemble, having toured with them in Europe, 
Australia and the United States. She lives in Chelsea, Manhattan, with her husband and two 
children. www.anncecilsterman.com
About the Designer
Cody Dodo, MS, LAc, studied acupuncture under Dr. Jeﬀrey Yuen at the Swedish Institute in 
New York and later completed advanced studies with Dr. Yuen in acupuncture and Chinese 
dietary therapy. Prior to his pursuit of acupuncture, Cody had a long career as a graphic designer 
in the publishing industry. Cody has a private acupuncture practice in Manhattan, and teaches 
Classical Acupuncture internationally, and at Paciﬁc College of Health and Science. He also 
designed the author’s other books, Advanced Acupuncture: A Clinic Manual (2012, 2018), 
Illustrations of the Complete Acupuncture System (2014), and Te Art of Pulse Diagnosis (2015).
